Term in \(x^2\) in \((2 + 3x)(32 - 40ax + 20a^2x^2 - \cdots)\):

\[ 2(20a^2) + 3(-40a) = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; 40a(a - 3) = 0 \]

Since \(a\) is positive, \(a = 3\).

\(a = 3\)
(c)

Coefficient of \(x^3\):

\[ 2(-5a^3) + 3(20a^2) = 2(-135) + 3(180) = -270 + 540 = 270 \]
\(270\)
Teaching script for parts (b)(c)
(b)

Multiply by two plus three x and pick out the x squared terms. That's two times twenty a squared x squared, and three x times minus forty a x.

\[ 2(20a^2) + 3(-40a) = 0 \]

There's no x squared term, so those add to zero. Factorise: forty a times a minus three equals nought. a is positive, so a is three.

\[ 40a(a - 3) = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; a = 3 \]

Watch for: giving \(a = 0\) as well. The question says \(a\) is positive.

(c)

For the x cubed term: two times minus five a cubed, plus three times twenty a squared. Put in a equals three. Minus five times twenty-seven is minus one hundred and thirty-five, and twenty times nine is one hundred and eighty.

The coefficient of x cubed is two hundred and seventy.

Watch for: using only one of the two pairings. Each \(x^3\) term in the product needs both.

A5 Binomial expansions › Product with a bracket and a coefficient condition
63/2162023 P2 Q1Whole question
Model solution

General term of \(\left(2 - \tfrac{1}{2}x\right)^6\): \(\dbinom{6}{r}2^{6-r}\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}x\right)^r\).

\[ \begin{aligned} x^2 \text{ term } (r=2)&: \binom{6}{2}(2^4)\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)x^2 = 60x^2, \\ x^3 \text{ term } (r=3)&: \binom{6}{3}(2^3)\left(-\frac{1}{8}\right)x^3 = -20x^3. \end{aligned} \]

Coefficient of \(x^3\) in \((k + 2x)\left(2 - \tfrac{1}{2}x\right)^6\):

\[ k(-20) + 2(60) = 0 \quad\Rightarrow\quad 20k = 120. \]
\(k = 6\)
Teaching script

The bracket k plus two x multiplies the whole expansion. An x cubed term comes from k times the x cubed term, plus two x times the x squared term. So you need those two terms of two minus a half x, to the sixth.

\[ T_{r+1} = \binom{6}{r}2^{6-r}\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}x\right)^r \]

For x squared, r is two: fifteen times sixteen times a quarter, which is sixty. For x cubed, r is three: twenty times eight times minus an eighth, which is minus twenty.

\[ 60x^2, \qquad -20x^3 \]

The x cubed term disappears, so its coefficient must be zero.

\[ k(-20) + 2(60) = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; k = 6 \]

Watch for: the sign. Minus a half, cubed, is negative, so the x cubed coefficient is minus twenty.

A5 Binomial expansions › Product with a bracket and a coefficient condition
64/2162024 P1 Q5Parts (b)
Model solution for parts (b)
(b)

The \(x^2\) term of \((1 + x^2)\left(2x - \frac{3}{2x}\right)^6\) comes from \(1 \times (x^2\text{ term}) + x^2 \times (\text{constant term})\).

\(x^2\) term: \(6 - 2r = 2\), so \(r = 2\):

\[ \binom62 2^4\left(-\tfrac32\right)^2 x^2 = 15 \times 16 \times \tfrac94 x^2 = 540x^2 \]

Constant term \(= -540\) (from part (a)).

\[ \text{Coefficient of } x^2 = 540 + (-540) = 0 \]
The coefficient of \(x^2\) is 0, so there is no term in \(x^2\).
Teaching script for parts (b)
(b)

Once you multiply by one plus x squared, an x squared term can turn up two ways. Either one times the x squared term of the expansion, or x squared times the constant term.

For the x squared term, six minus two r equals two, so r is two. That's fifteen times sixteen times nine quarters, which is five hundred and forty.

\[ \binom62 2^4\left(-\tfrac32\right)^2 = 540 \]

The constant term is minus five hundred and forty, from part (a). Add the two together: five hundred and forty plus minus five hundred and forty is zero.

\[ 540 + (-540) = 0 \]

The coefficient of x squared is zero, so the x squared term cancels out completely. That's what they wanted you to show.

Watch for: counting only one times the x squared term and forgetting the constant term. Both routes count.

A5 Binomial expansions › Product with a bracket and a coefficient condition
65/2162025 P1 Q7Parts (b)
Model solution for parts (b)
(b)
\[ \left(4 + \frac{5}{ax}\right)\left(\tfrac{1}{32} - \tfrac{5a}{16}x + \tfrac{5a^2}{4}x^2 - \tfrac{5a^3}{2}x^3 + \cdots\right) \] \[ \text{Coefficient of } x = 4\left(-\tfrac{5a}{16}\right) + \frac{5}{a}\left(\tfrac{5a^2}{4}\right) = -\tfrac{5a}{4} + \tfrac{25a}{4} = 5a \] \[ \text{Coefficient of } x^2 = 4\left(\tfrac{5a^2}{4}\right) + \frac{5}{a}\left(-\tfrac{5a^3}{2}\right) = 5a^2 - \tfrac{25a^2}{2} = -\tfrac{15a^2}{2} \] \[ \begin{aligned} 5a &= 2\left(-\tfrac{15a^2}{2}\right) \\ 15a^2 + 5a &= 0 \\ 5a(3a + 1) &= 0 \end{aligned} \]
\(a = -\tfrac{1}{3}\) (\(a \ne 0\))
Teaching script for parts (b)
(b)

Multiply four plus five over a x by your expansion, but only find the terms you need. For the x term: four times the x term, plus five over a x times the x squared term. The x on the bottom cancels one power of x.

\[ 4\left(-\tfrac{5a}{16}\right) + \frac{5}{a}\left(\tfrac{5a^2}{4}\right) = 5a \]

For the x squared term: four times the x squared term, plus five over a x times the x cubed term.

\[ 4\left(\tfrac{5a^2}{4}\right) + \frac{5}{a}\left(-\tfrac{5a^3}{2}\right) = -\tfrac{15a^2}{2} \]

The x coefficient is twice the x squared coefficient, so five a equals negative fifteen a squared. Bring it all to one side and factorise: five a, times three a plus one, equals zero. a isn't zero, so a is negative a third.

Watch for: pairing five over a x with the x term. Because of that x on the bottom, it has to pair with the term one power higher.

A6 Exponential and logarithmic functions › Exponential equations
66/2162020 P2 Q8Parts (a)Syllabus 4047 paper
Model solution for parts (a)
(a)

Let \(u = e^x\).

\[ \begin{aligned} u(1 + u) &= \tfrac34 \\ 4u^2 + 4u - 3 &= 0 \\ (2u - 1)(2u + 3) &= 0 \end{aligned} \]

\(e^x = -\tfrac32\) has no solution since \(e^x \gt 0\). So \(e^x = \tfrac12\).

\(x = \ln\tfrac12 = -\ln 2 = -0.693\) (3 s.f.)
Teaching script for parts (a)
(a)

e to the x shows up twice, so call it u. The equation becomes u times one plus u equals three quarters. Multiply by four and factorise.

\[ 4u^2 + 4u - 3 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; (2u - 1)(2u + 3) = 0 \]

u equals minus three halves can't happen, because e to the x is always positive. So e to the x is a half, and x is ln a half, which is minus ln two, minus zero point six nine three.

Watch for: keeping the negative root, or rejecting it without saying why. Write that e^x = −3/2 is impossible because e^x is positive.

A6 Exponential and logarithmic functions › Exponential equations
67/2162021 P1 Q12Parts (a)
Model solution for parts (a)
(a)
\[ \begin{aligned} 6^x &= 5 \times 3^{x+1} \\ 2^x \times 3^x &= 15 \times 3^x \\ 2^x &= 15 \\ x\lg 2 &= \lg 15 \end{aligned} \]
\(x = \dfrac{\lg 15}{\lg 2}\)  (shown)
Teaching script for parts (a)
(a)

Six to the x splits into two to the x times three to the x. On the other side, three to the x plus one is three times three to the x, so the right side is fifteen times three to the x.

\[ 2^x \times 3^x = 15 \times 3^x \]

Divide both sides by three to the x, which is never zero, so that's allowed. Two to the x equals fifteen. Take lg of both sides and bring the power down to the front.

\[ x\lg 2 = \lg 15 \;\Rightarrow\; x = \frac{\lg 15}{\lg 2} \]

Watch for: turning 5 × 3^(x+1) into 15^(x+1). Only the 3 carries the power. The 5 stays where it is.

A6 Exponential and logarithmic functions › Exponential equations
68/2162021 P2 Q1Whole question
Model solution

Multiply by \(e^x\) and let \(u = e^x\):

\[ \begin{aligned} 3e^{2x} + 5e^x &= 2 \\ 3u^2 + 5u - 2 &= 0 \\ (3u - 1)(u + 2) &= 0 \\ u = \tfrac13 \quad &\text{or} \quad u = -2 \end{aligned} \]

Since \(e^x \gt 0\) for all real \(x\), \(e^x = -2\) has no solution. Only \(e^x = \tfrac13\) gives a solution, so the equation has only one solution.

\[ x = \ln \tfrac13 = -1.0986\ldots \]
\(x = -1.1\) (2 s.f.)
Teaching script

See the e to the minus x? Multiply every term by e to the x to get rid of it. Then the whole thing turns into a quadratic in e to the x.

\[ 3e^{2x} + 5e^x = 2 \]

Let u be e to the x. You get three u squared plus five u minus two equals nought, and that factorises as three u minus one, times u plus two.

\[ 3u^2 + 5u - 2 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; (3u - 1)(u + 2) = 0 \]

So u is a third or minus two. Now here's the explanation they're after. e to the x is positive for every real x, so e to the x equals minus two has no solution at all. Only one value of u works, and that's why there's only one solution.

From e to the x equals a third, take natural logs. x is ln of a third, which is minus one point one to two significant figures.

\[ x = \ln\tfrac13 \approx -1.1 \]

Watch for: writing "no solution" with no reason. Say that \(e^x \gt 0\) for all \(x\). That sentence is the mark.

Model solution for parts (a)
(a)

\(4^{x-1} = \dfrac{(2^x)^2}{4}\). Let \(u = 2^x\):

\[ \begin{aligned} u + \frac{u^2}{4} &= 15 \\ u^2 + 4u - 60 &= 0 \\ (u + 10)(u - 6) &= 0. \end{aligned} \]

\(u = -10\) is rejected since \(2^x \gt 0\). So \(2^x = 6\):

\[ x = \frac{\lg 6}{\lg 2}. \]
\(x = 2.58\) (3 s.f.)
Teaching script for parts (a)
(a)

Two bases, two and four, but four is two squared in disguise. Rewrite four to the x minus one as two to the x, squared, divided by four. Now the whole thing is a quadratic in two to the x.

\[ u = 2^x: \quad u + \frac{u^2}{4} = 15 \;\Rightarrow\; u^2 + 4u - 60 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; (u+10)(u-6) = 0 \]

Two to the x can never be negative, so throw out minus ten and say why. That leaves two to the x equals six. Take logs.

\[ x = \frac{\lg 6}{\lg 2} = 2.58 \]

Watch for: rejecting u equals minus ten without a reason. One short line saying two to the x is always positive does it.

A6 Exponential and logarithmic functions › Exponential equations
70/2162023 P2 Q6Parts (b)(c)
Model solution for parts (b)(c)
(b)

Let \(u = 2^y\), so \(4^y = u^2\) and \(2^{-y} = \dfrac1u\).

\[ \begin{aligned} 6u^2 + \frac2u &= 7u + 1 \\ 6u^3 - 7u^2 - u + 2 &= 0 \\ (2u+1)(3u-2)(u-1) &= 0 \quad \text{(from part (a))} \end{aligned} \]

\(u = -\tfrac12\) is rejected since \(2^y \gt 0\). So \(2^y = \tfrac23\) or \(2^y = 1\).

\[ 2^y = 1 \Rightarrow y = 0, \qquad 2^y = \tfrac23 \Rightarrow y = \frac{\lg \frac23}{\lg 2} = -0.585 \ (3\text{ s.f.}) \]
\(y = 0\) or \(y = -0.585\)
(c)
\[ y = \log_2 \tfrac23 = \log_2 2 - \log_2 3 = 1 - \log_2 3 \]
\(a = 2,\ b = 3\)
Teaching script for parts (b)(c)
(b)

Look at the powers here: four to the y, two to the y, two to the minus y. They're all powers of two, so let u be two to the y. Then four to the y becomes u squared, and two to the minus y becomes one over u.

\[ 6u^2 + \frac2u = 7u + 1 \;\Rightarrow\; 6u^3 - 7u^2 - u + 2 = 0 \]

Multiply through by u, move everything to one side, and you get the same cubic as part (a). The examiner didn't put them side by side by accident. So u is minus a half, two thirds, or one.

Throw away minus a half, because two to any power is positive. Two to the y equals one gives y equals zero. For two to the y equals two thirds, take logs: y is lg of two thirds over lg two, which is minus zero point five eight five to three significant figures.

\[ y = 0 \quad \text{or} \quad y = -0.585 \]

Watch for: keeping u equals minus a half, or forgetting that y equals zero is a solution as well.

(c)

The non-zero solution is y equals log base two of two thirds. Split it with the quotient law into log base two of two, minus log base two of three.

\[ y = \log_2 \tfrac23 = 1 - \log_2 3 \]

Log base two of two is one, so y is one minus log base two of three. That makes a equal to two and b equal to three.

Watch for: treating log two over log three as if it were log two minus log three. Dividing logs is not the same as subtracting them.

Let \(u = e^x\).

(b)

E to the minus x is one over e to the x, so let u be e to the x. The equation turns into u plus one over u equals two point nine.

A6 Exponential and logarithmic functions › Exponential equations
72/2162025 P2 Q8Whole question
Model solution
(a)
\[ \begin{aligned} 2^y &= 5\left(3^{2-y}\right) = \frac{5 \times 9}{3^y} \\ 2^y \times 3^y &= 45 \\ 6^y &= 45 \\ y &= \frac{\lg 45}{\lg 6} = 2.1245 \end{aligned} \]
\(y = 2.12\)
(b)

Let \(u = 2^x\) and \(v = 3^y\). Then \(3^{y+1} = 3v\) and \(2^{x-1} = \tfrac{1}{2}u\).

\[ \begin{aligned} 9u + 6v &= 38 \quad (1) \\ \tfrac{15}{2}u - 21v &= 23 \quad (2) \end{aligned} \]

\((1) \times 3.5\): \(31.5u + 21v = 133\). Adding to (2):

\[ 39u = 156 \;\Rightarrow\; u = 4 \] \[ 6v = 38 - 36 = 2 \;\Rightarrow\; v = \tfrac{1}{3} \] \[ 2^x = 4 \Rightarrow x = 2, \qquad 3^y = 3^{-1} \Rightarrow y = -1 \]
\(x = 2\), \(y = -1\)
Teaching script
(a)

The unknown is stuck in two different bases, two and three. Split three to the two minus y into nine over three to the y, then multiply across so the y-powers end up together.

\[ 2^y \times 3^y = 45 \;\Rightarrow\; 6^y = 45 \]

Two to the y times three to the y is six to the y, because the powers match. Take lg of both sides: y is lg forty-five over lg six, which is two point one two to three significant figures.

Watch for: writing three to the two minus y as nine minus three to the y. A power with a minus in it splits into a division, not a subtraction.

(b)

Both equations have powers of two and powers of three, so give them nicknames. Let u be two to the x and v be three to the y. Then three to the y plus one is three v, and two to the x minus one is half of u.

\[ 9u + 6v = 38,\qquad \tfrac{15}{2}u - 21v = 23 \]

Get rid of v. Multiply the first equation by three point five so the v term becomes twenty-one v, then add it to the second.

\[ 39u = 156 \;\Rightarrow\; u = 4,\qquad v = \tfrac{1}{3} \]

Now swap the nicknames back. Two to the x equals four gives x equals two. Three to the y equals a third, which is three to the minus one, so y is negative one.

Watch for: stopping at u and v. The question wants x and y.

Logarithmic equations

4 questions
A6 Exponential and logarithmic functions › Logarithmic equations
73/2162019 P2 Q4Whole questionSyllabus 4047 paper
Model solution
(a)
\[ \log_{16}x = \frac{\log_2 x}{\log_2 16} = \frac{\log_2 x}{4} \] \[ \begin{aligned} \log_2 x + \tfrac{1}{4}\log_2 x &= -2.5 \\ \tfrac{5}{4}\log_2 x &= -2.5 \\ \log_2 x &= -2 \\ x &= 2^{-2} \end{aligned} \]
\(x = \dfrac{1}{4}\)
(b)(i)
\[ \begin{aligned} \lg\frac{z}{y} &= \lg(z+y) \\ \frac{z}{y} &= z + y \\ z &= zy + y^2 \\ z(1-y) &= y^2 \end{aligned} \]
\(z = \dfrac{y^2}{1-y}\)
(b)(ii)

\(\lg z\) is defined only for \(z \gt 0\), so \(z \gt 0\).

\(\lg y\) is defined only for \(y \gt 0\). Since \(z = \dfrac{y^2}{1-y} \gt 0\) and \(y^2 \gt 0\), the denominator must be positive: \(1 - y \gt 0\), so \(y \lt 1\). Hence \(0 \lt y \lt 1\).

\(z \gt 0\); \(0 \lt y \lt 1\) because \(y \gt 0\) for \(\lg y\) to exist and \(1 - y \gt 0\) for \(z\) to be positive
Teaching script
(a)

The logs have different bases, two and sixteen. Change log base sixteen into base two. Log base two of sixteen is four.

\[ \log_{16}x = \frac{\log_2 x}{4} \]

Now everything is log base two of x. Add them up: one and a quarter of them.

\[ \tfrac54\log_2 x = -2.5 \;\Rightarrow\; \log_2 x = -2 \;\Rightarrow\; x = 2^{-2} \]

x is one quarter.

Watch for: rejecting x because it's a fraction. A negative log is fine. Only x itself has to be positive.

(b)(i)

Combine lg z minus lg y into lg of z over y. With one log on each side, you can drop the logs.

\[ \frac{z}{y} = z + y \]

Multiply by y, then gather the z terms on one side and factorise.

\[ z = zy + y^2 \;\Rightarrow\; z(1-y) = y^2 \;\Rightarrow\; z = \frac{y^2}{1-y} \]

Watch for: writing lg z minus lg y as lg z over lg y. The law gives the log of a quotient, not a quotient of logs. Very common, very wrong.

(b)(ii)

Logs only exist for positive numbers. lg z appears, so z is greater than zero. lg y appears, so y is greater than zero.

Now use your formula. z is y squared over one minus y. The top is positive and z has to be positive, so the bottom must be positive too. That means y is less than one.

\[ z \gt 0, \qquad 0 \lt y \lt 1 \]

Watch for: stating only y greater than zero. The upper limit comes from the formula in part (i).

74/2162020 P2 Q8Parts (b)Syllabus 4047 paper
Model solution for parts (b)
(b)
\[ \frac{1}{\log_8 2} = \log_2 8 = 3 \] \[ \begin{aligned} 1 + \log_2 y + 3 &= \log_2(y + 3) \\ \log_2 16 + \log_2 y &= \log_2(y + 3) \\ 16y &= y + 3 \end{aligned} \]
\(y = \tfrac15\)
Teaching script for parts (b)
(b)

The odd one out here is one over log base eight of two. Flip it: one over log base a of b is log base b of a. So it's log base two of eight, which is three.

\[ \frac{1}{\log_8 2} = \log_2 8 = 3 \]

The left side becomes one plus log two y plus three, so four plus log two y. Write the four as log two of sixteen and combine.

\[ \log_2 16y = \log_2(y + 3) \;\Rightarrow\; 16y = y + 3 \]

Fifteen y is three, so y is one fifth.

Watch for: turning 1/log₈2 into log₈(1/2). Taking the reciprocal swaps the base and the number, it doesn't flip the number.

A6 Exponential and logarithmic functions › Logarithmic equations
75/2162021 P1 Q12Parts (b)
Model solution for parts (b)
(b)
\[ \log_9(x + 2) = \frac{\log_3(x + 2)}{\log_3 9} = \tfrac12\log_3(x + 2) \] \[ \begin{aligned} \log_3 x + \tfrac12\log_3(x + 2) &= 2 \\ 2\log_3 x + \log_3(x + 2) &= 4 \\ \log_3\left[x^2(x + 2)\right] &= 4 \\ x^2(x + 2) &= 3^4 = 81 \end{aligned} \]
\(x^3 + 2x^2 - 81 = 0\)
Teaching script for parts (b)
(b)

We've got bases three and nine, so change the base nine log into base three. Log base three of nine is two, which makes log base nine of x plus two equal to half of log base three of x plus two.

\[ \log_9(x + 2) = \tfrac12\log_3(x + 2) \]

Multiply the whole equation by two to get rid of the half. Two log three x becomes log three of x squared. Combine into a single log, then remove the log: x squared times x plus two equals three to the four, which is eighty-one.

\[ x^2(x + 2) = 81 \;\Rightarrow\; x^3 + 2x^2 - 81 = 0 \]

Watch for: forgetting to double the 2 on the right when you multiply through. It becomes 4, which is where the 3⁴ comes from.

A6 Exponential and logarithmic functions › Logarithmic equations
76/2162024 P2 Q3Whole question
Model solution
\[ 3\log_8 2 = 3 \times \frac{1}{3} = 1, \qquad \log_4(x + 3) = \frac{\log_2(x + 3)}{\log_2 4} = \frac12\log_2(x + 3) \] \[ \begin{aligned} \log_2 x - \tfrac12\log_2(x + 3) &= 1 \\ 2\log_2 x - \log_2(x + 3) &= 2 \\ \log_2 \frac{x^2}{x + 3} &= 2 \\ x^2 &= 4(x + 3) \\ x^2 - 4x - 12 &= 0 \\ (x - 6)(x + 2) &= 0 \end{aligned} \]

\(x = -2\) is rejected since \(\log_2 x\) is undefined for \(x \le 0\).

\(x = 6\)
Teaching script

Three different bases in one equation: two, four and eight. You can't combine logs with different bases, so get everything into base two first.

Log base eight of two is one third, because eight to the one third is two. So the right-hand side, three log base eight of two, is one. Log base four of x plus three is log base two of x plus three, divided by log base two of four, which is two.

\[ \log_2 x - \tfrac12\log_2(x + 3) = 1 \]

Double everything to clear the half, then use the power law and the quotient law to squash it into one log.

\[ \log_2 \frac{x^2}{x + 3} = 2 \;\Rightarrow\; x^2 = 4(x + 3) \;\Rightarrow\; (x - 6)(x + 2) = 0 \]

You can't take the log of a negative number, so minus two is out. The answer is x equals six.

Watch for: doubling the left side and forgetting to double the right. Whatever you do to one side, do to the other.

Growth and decay models

5 questions
A6 Exponential and logarithmic functions › Growth and decay models
77/2162019 P1 Q5Whole questionSyllabus 4047 paper
Model solution
(i)

Each minute, 21% of the germs present are destroyed, so 79% of them survive. After 1 minute \(0.79N\) germs are alive; after 2 minutes \(0.79 \times 0.79N = (0.79)^2N\). The number alive is multiplied by \(0.79\) every minute, so after \(n\) minutes it is \((0.79)^nN\).

(ii)
\[ \text{Fraction alive after 20 minutes} = (0.79)^{20} = 0.008965 \] \[ x = 100\left(1 - 0.008965\right) = 99.10 \]
\(x = 99\) (2 s.f.)
(iii)
\[ \begin{aligned} Ne^{kn} &= (0.79)^nN \text{ for all } n \\ e^{k} &= 0.79 \\ k &= \ln 0.79 = -0.2357 \end{aligned} \]
\(k = \ln 0.79 \approx -0.236\)
Teaching script
(i)

This is explaining a percentage decrease. If twenty-one per cent of the germs get destroyed each minute, seventy-nine per cent survive. So every minute, the number alive gets multiplied by nought point seven nine.

\[ N \to 0.79N \to (0.79)^2N \to \cdots \to (0.79)^nN \]

After one minute you have nought point seven nine N. After two, you multiply again and get nought point seven nine squared N. After n minutes you've done it n times, and that's where the power n comes from.

Watch for: writing one minus nought point two one n. The decrease is repeated multiplication, not the same amount knocked off each minute.

(ii)

Find the fraction still alive after twenty minutes. The percentage destroyed is whatever's left over.

\[ (0.79)^{20} = 0.008965 \]

That's the fraction alive. The percentage destroyed is one hundred times one minus that.

\[ x = 100(1 - 0.008965) = 99.10 \]

So x is ninety-nine, to two significant figures. Bad news for the germs.

Watch for: giving nought point nine per cent. That's the percentage still alive, not the percentage destroyed.

(iii)

The two models have to agree for every n. So N e to the k n must equal nought point seven nine to the n times N.

\[ Ne^{kn} = (0.79)^nN \;\Rightarrow\; e^{k} = 0.79 \]

Cancel the N and take the n-th root of both sides, so e to the k equals nought point seven nine. Then take natural logs.

\[ k = \ln 0.79 \approx -0.236 \]

k is ln nought point seven nine, about minus nought point two three six. Negative, which is what you want for decay.

Watch for: a positive k. If your k comes out positive, your germs are multiplying, not dying.

Model solution
(a)

In 2013, \(t = 3\) and \(N = 2050\):

\[ 2050 = 2400e^{-3k} \;\Rightarrow\; e^{-3k} = \frac{2050}{2400} \;\Rightarrow\; k = \frac13\ln\frac{2400}{2050} = 0.052543\ldots \]

In 2017, \(t = 7\):

\[ N = 2400e^{-7(0.052543)} = 1661.4\ldots \]
About 1660 birds
(b)
\[ 2400e^{-kt} \lt 1200 \;\Rightarrow\; e^{-kt} \lt \tfrac12 \;\Rightarrow\; t \gt \frac{\ln 2}{k} = \frac{\ln 2}{0.052543} = 13.19\ldots \]

\(t = 13.19\) falls in the year \(2010 + 13 = 2023\).

2023
Teaching script
(a)

t counts years from twenty-ten, so twenty thirteen is t equals three. Put N equals two thousand and fifty in to find k.

\[ 2050 = 2400e^{-3k} \;\Rightarrow\; k = \tfrac13\ln\frac{2400}{2050} \approx 0.0525 \]

Keep that value in your calculator, don't round it. Twenty seventeen is t equals seven.

\[ N = 2400e^{-7k} \approx 1661 \]

That's about one thousand six hundred and sixty birds.

Watch for: using a rounded \(k\) such as 0.05. It changes the answer more than you'd expect.

(b)

Below half the starting number means N is less than one thousand two hundred. Divide by two thousand four hundred and take logs.

\[ e^{-kt} \lt \tfrac12 \;\Rightarrow\; t \gt \frac{\ln 2}{k} \approx 13.19 \]

The inequality flips when you divide by minus k. t equals thirteen point one nine falls during the year twenty-ten plus thirteen, so the answer is twenty twenty-three.

\[ 2023 \]

Watch for: rounding up to \(t = 14\) and saying 2024. The stretch from \(t = 13\) to \(t = 14\) is the year 2023.

79/2162023 P2 Q3Whole question
Model solution
(a)

At \(t = 0\), \(T_c = 86e^0 = 86\).

\(86^\circ\)C
(b)
\[ 86e^{-0.06t} = 37 \quad\Rightarrow\quad e^{-0.06t} = \frac{37}{86} \quad\Rightarrow\quad t = \frac{\ln\frac{86}{37}}{0.06} = 14.06. \]
\(t = 14.1\) minutes (3 s.f.)
(c)(i)
\[ 86e^{-60\lambda} = 82 \quad\Rightarrow\quad \lambda = \frac{1}{60}\ln\frac{86}{82} = 0.000794. \]
\(\lambda = 0.000794\) (3 s.f.)
(c)(ii)
\[ \begin{aligned} 86e^{-0.06t} &= \frac{1}{2}\left(86e^{-\lambda t}\right) \\ e^{-(0.06 - \lambda)t} &= \frac{1}{2} \\ t &= \frac{\ln 2}{0.06 - 0.000794} = 11.7. \end{aligned} \]
\(t = 11.7\) minutes (3 s.f.)
Teaching script
(a)

Initial means t equals nought. E to the nought is one, so the coffee starts at eighty-six degrees Celsius. Hot enough to regret drinking straight away.

\[ T_c = 86e^0 = 86^\circ\text{C} \]

Watch for: leaving out the units.

(b)

Set the temperature equal to thirty-seven, get the exponential on its own, then take natural logs.

\[ e^{-0.06t} = \tfrac{37}{86} \;\Rightarrow\; t = \frac{\ln\frac{86}{37}}{0.06} = 14.1 \text{ minutes} \]

Flipping the fraction inside the log gets rid of the minus sign tidily.

Watch for: a negative time. That comes from dropping the minus in the exponent.

(c)(i)

The second drink also starts at eighty-six and is at eighty-two after sixty minutes. Substitute and solve for lambda the same way.

\[ 86e^{-60\lambda} = 82 \;\Rightarrow\; \lambda = \tfrac{1}{60}\ln\tfrac{86}{82} = 0.000794 \]

Watch for: rounding lambda too early. Keep the full value for part (ii).

(c)(ii)

"Half the temperature of the other" gives you one equation. Divide both sides by eighty-six and combine the exponentials.

\[ e^{-(0.06 - \lambda)t} = \tfrac{1}{2} \;\Rightarrow\; t = \frac{\ln 2}{0.06 - 0.000794} = 11.7 \text{ minutes} \]

Watch for: putting the half on the wrong side. Read carefully which drink is half the other.

Model solution
\[ \begin{aligned} 1000 &= 1800e^{-12k} \\ e^{-12k} &= \tfrac59 \\ k &= \frac{1}{12}\ln\frac95 = 0.048982\ldots \end{aligned} \]

New machine purchased when \(V = 0.25 \times 1800 = 450\):

\[ \begin{aligned} 450 &= 1800e^{-kt} \\ e^{-kt} &= \tfrac14 \\ t &= \frac{\ln 4}{k} = \frac{12\ln 4}{\ln 1.8} = 28.30\ldots \end{aligned} \]
\(t = 28.3\) (3 s.f.)
Teaching script

There's one unknown, k, and one fact to pin it down: after twelve months the machine is worth one thousand dollars. So find k first, then use it.

\[ 1000 = 1800e^{-12k} \;\Rightarrow\; e^{-12k} = \tfrac59 \;\Rightarrow\; k = \frac{1}{12}\ln\frac95 \]

Take natural logs of both sides. Ln of five ninths is negative, so flip it to nine fifths and the minus sign goes away. Keep k exact or store it in your calculator; it's zero point zero four eight nine eight.

The new machine gets bought when the old one is worth twenty-five per cent of eighteen hundred, which is four hundred and fifty dollars.

\[ 450 = 1800e^{-kt} \;\Rightarrow\; e^{-kt} = \tfrac14 \;\Rightarrow\; t = \frac{\ln 4}{k} = 28.30\ldots \]

So t is twenty-eight point three. The new machine is bought about twenty-eight point three months after the first one. A bit over two years of loyal service.

A6 Exponential and logarithmic functions › Growth and decay models
81/2162025 P1 Q1Whole question
Model solution

After \(n\) complete months the price is \(300(0.95)^n\). The item sells once this is first below \$120.

\[ \begin{aligned} 300(0.95)^n &\lt 120 \\ (0.95)^n &\lt 0.4 \\ n \lg 0.95 &\lt \lg 0.4 \\ n &\gt \frac{\lg 0.4}{\lg 0.95} \quad (\lg 0.95 \lt 0) \\ n &\gt 17.86 \end{aligned} \]

Check: \(300(0.95)^{17} = \$125.44\) and \(300(0.95)^{18} = \$119.16\).

\(18\) complete months
Teaching script

Dropping five per cent a month means multiplying by nought point nine five each month. After n complete months, the price is three hundred times nought point nine five to the n. The shop sells it when that first goes below a hundred and twenty dollars.

\[ 300(0.95)^n \lt 120 \;\Rightarrow\; (0.95)^n \lt 0.4 \]

Take lg of both sides and bring the n down. Now the step everyone forgets: lg of nought point nine five is negative, so when you divide by it, the inequality flips.

\[ n \gt \frac{\lg 0.4}{\lg 0.95} = 17.86 \]

n has to be a whole number of months, so round up to eighteen. Check it, because the check is quick: after seventeen months it's a hundred and twenty-five dollars forty-four, still too pricey. After eighteen it's a hundred and nineteen dollars sixteen. So eighteen complete months. Patient shoppers win.

Watch for: keeping the less-than sign after dividing by lg nought point nine five. That gives n less than seventeen point eight six, which would mean the item sells before it even gets cheap enough.

Features of exponential and logarithmic graphs

2 questions
A6 Exponential and logarithmic functions › Features of exponential and logarithmic graphs
82/2162022 P2 Q6Parts (b)
Model solution for parts (b)
(b)(i)

At \(A\), \(y = 0\): \(e^{2x} = 5\), so \(x = \tfrac{1}{2}\ln 5\).

At \(B\), \(x = 0\): \(y = 5 - 1 = 4\).

\(A\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\ln 5,\ 0\right)\), \(B(0, 4)\)
(b)(ii)
\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = -2e^{2x}. \]

At \(A\), \(e^{2x} = 5\), so \(\dfrac{dy}{dx} = -2(5) = -10\).

Gradient at \(A = -10\)
Teaching script for parts (b)
(b)(i)

A is on the x-axis, so y is zero: e to the two x equals five. Take natural logs, then halve.

\[ x = \tfrac{1}{2}\ln 5 \]

B is on the y-axis, so x is zero. E to the nought is one, and five minus one is four.

\[ A\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\ln 5, 0\right), \quad B(0, 4) \]

Watch for: treating e to the nought as zero. That puts B at nought, five, which is wrong.

(b)(ii)

Differentiate. The derivative of e to the two x is two e to the two x, and keep the minus in front.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = -2e^{2x} \]

No need for a decimal x here. At A you already know e to the two x is five, so the gradient is minus two times five, minus ten.

Watch for: forgetting the factor of two that the chain rule brings out.

A6 Exponential and logarithmic functions › Features of exponential and logarithmic graphs
83/2162024 P2 Q5Parts (a)
Model solution for parts (a)
(a)
\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = e^x - e^{-x}; \qquad \text{at } x = 0:\ \frac{dy}{dx} = e^0 - e^0 = 1 - 1 = 0 \]
\(\dfrac{dy}{dx} = 0\) at \(x = 0\), so the curve has a stationary point at \(x = 0\).
Teaching script for parts (a)
(a)

To show there's a stationary point, show that dy by dx is zero there. E to the x differentiates to itself, and e to the minus x differentiates to minus e to the minus x.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = e^x - e^{-x}; \quad x = 0: \ 1 - 1 = 0 \]

At x equals zero both exponentials are one, so the gradient is one minus one, zero. That's your stationary point at x equals zero.

Watch for: differentiating e to the minus x as plus e to the minus x. The minus in the power comes down in front.

Proving identities

6 questions
G1 Trigonometric functions, identities and equations › Proving identities
84/2162019 P2 Q2Parts (i)Syllabus 4047 paper
Model solution for parts (i)
(i)
\[ \begin{aligned} \text{LHS} &= \frac{\sin^2\theta - (1-\cos\theta)}{(1-\cos\theta)\sin\theta} \\ &= \frac{1 - \cos^2\theta - 1 + \cos\theta}{(1-\cos\theta)\sin\theta} \\ &= \frac{\cos\theta - \cos^2\theta}{(1-\cos\theta)\sin\theta} \\ &= \frac{\cos\theta(1-\cos\theta)}{(1-\cos\theta)\sin\theta} \\ &= \frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta} = \cot\theta = \text{RHS} \end{aligned} \]
Teaching script for parts (i)
(i)

Start from the left-hand side, the two fractions. Put them over a common denominator, one minus cos theta times sin theta.

\[ \frac{\sin^2\theta - (1-\cos\theta)}{(1-\cos\theta)\sin\theta} \]

Swap sin squared theta for one minus cos squared theta. The ones cancel.

\[ = \frac{\cos\theta - \cos^2\theta}{(1-\cos\theta)\sin\theta} \]

Take cos theta out of the top. That leaves one minus cos theta, which cancels with the same thing underneath.

\[ = \frac{\cos\theta(1-\cos\theta)}{(1-\cos\theta)\sin\theta} = \frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta} = \cot\theta \]

And that's the right-hand side.

Watch for: a sign slip when you expand minus one minus cos theta. The bracket gives minus one plus cos theta.

G1 Trigonometric functions, identities and equations › Proving identities

Model solution for parts (a)
(a)
\[ \begin{aligned} \text{LHS} &= \frac{\sin^2\theta - (1 - \cos\theta)}{(1 - \cos\theta)\sin\theta} \\ &= \frac{1 - \cos^2\theta - 1 + \cos\theta}{(1 - \cos\theta)\sin\theta} \\ &= \frac{\cos\theta(1 - \cos\theta)}{(1 - \cos\theta)\sin\theta} \\ &= \frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta} = \cot\theta = \text{RHS} \end{aligned} \]
\(\dfrac{\sin\theta}{1 - \cos\theta} - \dfrac{1}{\sin\theta} = \cot\theta\)  (proved)
Teaching script for parts (a)
(a)

Start from the left side, since that's the one with two fractions to play with. Combine them over the common denominator, one minus cos theta, times sin theta.

\[ \frac{\sin^2\theta - (1 - \cos\theta)}{(1 - \cos\theta)\sin\theta} \]

Swap sin squared for one minus cos squared. The ones cancel, leaving cos theta minus cos squared theta on top. Take out cos theta, and the top becomes cos theta times one minus cos theta.

\[ \frac{\cos\theta(1 - \cos\theta)}{(1 - \cos\theta)\sin\theta} = \frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta} = \cot\theta \]

The one minus cos theta cancels top and bottom, leaving cos over sin. That's cot theta, which is the right side. Done.

Watch for: working on both sides at the same time. Start from one side and finish at the other.

86/2162022 P2 Q3Parts (a)
Model solution for parts (a)
(a)
\[ \begin{aligned} \text{LHS} &= \tan 2\theta\left(2\cos\theta - \frac{1}{\cos\theta}\right) \\ &= \frac{\sin 2\theta}{\cos 2\theta}\cdot\frac{2\cos^2\theta - 1}{\cos\theta} \\ &= \frac{2\sin\theta\cos\theta}{\cos 2\theta}\cdot\frac{\cos 2\theta}{\cos\theta} \\ &= 2\sin\theta = \text{RHS}. \end{aligned} \]
\(\tan 2\theta(2\cos\theta - \sec\theta) = 2\sin\theta\) (shown)
Teaching script for parts (a)
(a)

Start on the left-hand side, since that's where all the action is. Write tan two theta as sine two theta over cos two theta, turn secant into one over cos theta, and then squash the bracket into one fraction.

\[ \tan 2\theta\left(2\cos\theta - \frac{1}{\cos\theta}\right) = \frac{\sin 2\theta}{\cos 2\theta}\cdot\frac{2\cos^2\theta - 1}{\cos\theta} \]

Now look for two familiar faces. Two cos squared theta minus one is cos two theta, and sine two theta is two sine theta cos theta.

\[ = \frac{2\sin\theta\cos\theta}{\cos 2\theta}\cdot\frac{\cos 2\theta}{\cos\theta} = 2\sin\theta \]

Cos two theta cancels, cos theta cancels, and what's left is two sine theta. That's the right-hand side, done.

Watch for: working on both sides at the same time. Stay on one side and finish with "equals RHS".

G1 Trigonometric functions, identities and equations › Proving identities
87/2162023 P1 Q3Whole question
Model solution
\[ \begin{aligned} \text{LHS} &= \frac{\cos^2\theta}{\operatorname{cosec}^2\theta - 1} + \frac{\sin^2\theta}{\sec^2\theta - 1} \\ &= \frac{\cos^2\theta}{\cot^2\theta} + \frac{\sin^2\theta}{\tan^2\theta} \\ &= \cos^2\theta\cdot\frac{\sin^2\theta}{\cos^2\theta} + \sin^2\theta\cdot\frac{\cos^2\theta}{\sin^2\theta} \\ &= \sin^2\theta + \cos^2\theta \\ &= 1 = \text{RHS}. \end{aligned} \]
Identity proved.
Teaching script

Start from the left-hand side. The two denominators are cosec squared minus one and sec squared minus one. Each one is a Pythagorean identity in disguise: cot squared and tan squared.

\[ \frac{\cos^2\theta}{\cot^2\theta} + \frac{\sin^2\theta}{\tan^2\theta} \]

Dividing by cot squared is the same as multiplying by tan squared, which is sine squared over cos squared. Dividing by tan squared is multiplying by cos squared over sine squared.

\[ \cos^2\theta\cdot\frac{\sin^2\theta}{\cos^2\theta} + \sin^2\theta\cdot\frac{\cos^2\theta}{\sin^2\theta} = \sin^2\theta + \cos^2\theta = 1 \]

Add them up and you get one, the right-hand side.

Watch for: writing cosec squared minus one as tan squared. It's cot squared. The cosec goes with the cot.

G1 Trigonometric functions, identities and equations › Proving identities
88/2162024 P2 Q9Parts (a)
Model solution for parts (a)
(a)
\[ \begin{aligned} \tan x + \cot x &= \frac{\sin x}{\cos x} + \frac{\cos x}{\sin x} \\ &= \frac{\sin^2 x + \cos^2 x}{\sin x \cos x} \\ &= \frac{1}{\tfrac{1}{2}\sin 2x} \\ &= \frac{2}{\sin 2x} \\ &= 2\,\mathrm{cosec}\, 2x \end{aligned} \]
\(\tan x + \cot x = 2\,\mathrm{cosec}\,2x\) (shown)
Teaching script for parts (a)
(a)

You're proving tan x plus cot x equals two cosec two x. Start on the left, because that side has more to play with. Turn both terms into sine and cosine, then put them over one denominator.

\[ \frac{\sin x}{\cos x} + \frac{\cos x}{\sin x} = \frac{\sin^2 x + \cos^2 x}{\sin x \cos x} \]

The top is one, by sine squared plus cos squared. For the bottom, sine x cos x is half of sine two x, from the double-angle formula.

\[ = \frac{1}{\tfrac{1}{2}\sin 2x} = \frac{2}{\sin 2x} = 2\,\mathrm{cosec}\,2x \]

That's the right-hand side, so we're done.

Watch for: writing sine x cos x as sine two x. It's only half of it.

G1 Trigonometric functions, identities and equations › Proving identities
89/2162025 P1 Q9Parts (a)
Model solution for parts (a)
(a)
\[ \begin{aligned} \text{LHS} &= \frac{1 + 2\sin\theta\cos\theta}{\sin\theta} - \frac{1 + (2\cos^2\theta - 1)}{\cos\theta} \\ &= \frac{1 + 2\sin\theta\cos\theta}{\sin\theta} - \frac{2\cos^2\theta}{\cos\theta} \\ &= \frac{1}{\sin\theta} + 2\cos\theta - 2\cos\theta \\ &= \mathrm{cosec}\,\theta = \text{RHS} \end{aligned} \]
Proved
Teaching script for parts (a)
(a)

The left side has sine two theta and cos two theta, so the plan is to swap both for double-angle forms in single angles. For cos two theta, pick two cos squared theta minus one, because then the one on top cancels.

\[ \frac{1 + 2\sin\theta\cos\theta}{\sin\theta} - \frac{2\cos^2\theta}{\cos\theta} \]

Split the first fraction into one over sine theta plus two cos theta. The second fraction cancels down to two cos theta.

\[ = \frac{1}{\sin\theta} + 2\cos\theta - 2\cos\theta = \mathrm{cosec}\,\theta \]

The two cos theta terms cancel each other, and you're left with cosec theta, the right-hand side.

Watch for: picking one minus two sine squared for cos two theta. It still works, but you'll write a lot more.

(ii)
\[ \begin{aligned} \cot\theta &= 3\tan\theta \\ \frac{1}{\tan\theta} &= 3\tan\theta \\ \tan^2\theta &= \frac{1}{3} \\ \tan\theta &= \pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} \end{aligned} \]

Basic angle \(30^\circ\). For \(0^\circ \le \theta \le 180^\circ\): \(\tan\theta = \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\) gives \(30^\circ\); \(\tan\theta = -\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\) gives \(150^\circ\). (\(\theta = 0^\circ, 180^\circ\) are excluded since \(\sin\theta = 0\) there.)

\(\theta = 30^\circ,\ 150^\circ\)
Teaching script for parts (ii)
(ii)

"Hence" means swap the left-hand side for cot theta using part (i). The equation becomes cot theta equals three tan theta.

\[ \frac{1}{\tan\theta} = 3\tan\theta \;\Rightarrow\; \tan^2\theta = \tfrac13 \;\Rightarrow\; \tan\theta = \pm\tfrac{1}{\sqrt3} \]

The basic angle is thirty degrees. Positive tan in the range gives thirty degrees. Negative tan, second quadrant, gives one hundred and fifty degrees.

Nought and one eighty are out, because sin theta is zero there and the original fractions fall apart.

\(\theta = 30^\circ,\ 150^\circ\)

Watch for: dropping the negative root and losing one hundred and fifty degrees.

93/2162024 P2 Q9Parts (b)(c)
Model solution for parts (b)(c)
(b)
\[ 2\,\mathrm{cosec}\,2x = 3 \;\Rightarrow\; \sin 2x = \frac{2}{3} \]

For \(0 \le x \le 2\pi\), \(0 \le 2x \le 4\pi\). Basic angle \(\alpha = \sin^{-1}\tfrac{2}{3} = 0.72973\). \(\sin 2x \gt 0\), so \(2x\) lies in the first or second quadrant.

\[ 2x = 0.72973,\ \pi - 0.72973,\ 2\pi + 0.72973,\ 3\pi - 0.72973 \] \[ 2x = 0.72973,\ 2.41186,\ 7.01292,\ 8.69505 \]
\(x = 0.365,\ 1.21,\ 3.51,\ 4.35\)
(c)

Since \(-1 \le \sin 2x \le 1\) and \(\sin 2x \ne 0\), \(|\mathrm{cosec}\,2x| \ge 1\), so \(|2\,\mathrm{cosec}\,2x| \ge 2\). The expression never takes values strictly between \(-2\) and \(2\).

\(\{k : -2 \lt k \lt 2\}\)
Teaching script for parts (b)(c)
(b)

"Hence" means use part (a). So the equation turns into two cosec two x equals three, which gives sine two x equals two thirds.

\[ \sin 2x = \tfrac{2}{3} \]

Before solving, double the range. x goes from nought to two pi, so two x goes from nought to four pi, which is two full laps of the circle. The basic angle is inverse sine of two thirds, nought point seven two nine seven three radians. Sine is positive, so two x is in the first or second quadrant, on both laps.

\[ 2x = 0.72973,\ 2.41186,\ 7.01292,\ 8.69505 \]

Then halve each one. x is nought point three six five, one point two one, three point five one and four point three five.

Watch for: finding only two answers because you forgot the range doubled to four pi. Two laps, four answers.

(c)

Think about what cosec two x can actually be. Sine two x sits between negative one and one, and it's never zero here, so cosec two x is always at least one in size. Double it, and two cosec two x is always two or more, or negative two or less.

\[ |2\,\mathrm{cosec}\,2x| \ge 2 \]

So there's no solution when k is strictly between negative two and two.

Watch for: including the end values. At k equals two or negative two, there are solutions, so the inequalities have to be strict.

G1 Trigonometric functions, identities and equations › “Hence solve” after an identity
94/2162025 P1 Q9Parts (b)
Model solution for parts (b)
(b)
\[ \begin{aligned} \mathrm{cosec}\,\theta &= 2\sin\theta \\ \sin^2\theta &= \tfrac{1}{2} \\ \sin\theta &= \pm\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \end{aligned} \]

For \(\pi \lt \theta \lt \tfrac{3}{2}\pi\) (third quadrant), \(\sin\theta \lt 0\), so \(\sin\theta = -\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\). Basic angle \(\tfrac{\pi}{4}\).

\[ \theta = \pi + \tfrac{\pi}{4} \]
\(\theta = \tfrac{5\pi}{4}\)
Teaching script for parts (b)
(b)

Use part (a) to swap the left side for cosec theta. The equation becomes cosec theta equals two sine theta. Multiply both sides by sine theta.

\[ \sin^2\theta = \tfrac{1}{2} \;\Rightarrow\; \sin\theta = \pm\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \]

Now check the range. Theta is between pi and three pi over two, the third quadrant, where sine is negative. So take the negative root only. The basic angle is pi over four, and in the third quadrant theta is pi plus pi over four.

\[ \theta = \tfrac{5\pi}{4} \]

Watch for: forgetting the plus-or-minus when you square-root, then picking the wrong sign for the quadrant.

Trigonometric equations

3 questions
G1 Trigonometric functions, identities and equations › Trigonometric equations
95/2162020 P1 Q7Whole questionSyllabus 4047 paper
Model solution

Write \(\sec A\) and \(\tan A\) in terms of \(\cos A\) and \(\sin A\), then multiply through by \(\cos A\) (\(\cos A \ne 0\)).

\[ \begin{aligned} 3\cos A &= \frac{1}{\cos A} - \frac{5\sin A}{\cos A} \\ 3\cos^2 A &= 1 - 5\sin A \\ 3(1-\sin^2 A) &= 1 - 5\sin A \\ 3\sin^2 A - 5\sin A - 2 &= 0 \\ (3\sin A + 1)(\sin A - 2) &= 0 \end{aligned} \]

\(\sin A = 2\) has no solution, since \(-1 \le \sin A \le 1\). Hence \(\sin A = -\tfrac{1}{3}\).

Basic angle \(\alpha = \sin^{-1}\tfrac{1}{3} = 19.47^\circ\). \(\sin A\) is negative, so \(A\) lies in the third or fourth quadrant.

\[ A = 180^\circ + 19.47^\circ \quad\text{or}\quad A = 360^\circ - 19.47^\circ \]
\(A = 199.5^\circ\) or \(A = 340.5^\circ\)
Teaching script

Cos, sec and tan all in one equation. When they turn up together, the first move is always the same: write everything in terms of sine and cosine. Sec A is one over cos A, and tan A is sin A over cos A.

\[ 3\cos A = \frac{1}{\cos A} - \frac{5\sin A}{\cos A} \]

Now multiply every term by cos A to get rid of the fractions. We're allowed to, because cos A can't be zero here. If it were, sec A and tan A wouldn't even exist.

\[ 3\cos^2 A = 1 - 5\sin A \]

We've got a cos squared and a sin, which is one too many trig functions. Swap cos squared for one minus sin squared, and now it's all in sin A. Expand, bring everything to one side, and you get a quadratic in sin A that factorises.

\[ 3\sin^2 A - 5\sin A - 2 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; (3\sin A + 1)(\sin A - 2) = 0 \]

Sin A equals two? Sine never goes above one, so that one's out. That leaves sin A equals minus one third. Find the basic angle from the positive value: sin inverse of one third is nineteen point four seven degrees. Sine is negative in the third and fourth quadrants, so A is one hundred and eighty plus the basic angle, or three hundred and sixty minus it.

\[ A = 199.5^\circ \text{ or } 340.5^\circ \]

Watch for: typing sin inverse of minus a third straight into the calculator and getting minus 19.5 degrees. That's not in the range. Get the basic angle from the positive value, then place it by quadrant.

G1 Trigonometric functions, identities and equations › Trigonometric equations
96/2162023 P1 Q5Whole question
Model solution
\[ \begin{aligned} \frac{\cos\theta + 4\sin\theta}{2\cos\theta + \sin\theta} &= \frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta} \\ \sin\theta\cos\theta + 4\sin^2\theta &= 2\cos^2\theta + \sin\theta\cos\theta \\ 4\sin^2\theta &= 2\cos^2\theta \\ \tan^2\theta &= \frac{1}{2} \\ \tan\theta &= \pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}. \end{aligned} \]

Basic angle \(= \tan^{-1}\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}} = 0.6155\). For \(-\tfrac{\pi}{2} \le \theta \le \tfrac{\pi}{2}\), \(\tan\theta \gt 0\) gives \(\theta = 0.6155\) and \(\tan\theta \lt 0\) gives \(\theta = -0.6155\).

\(\theta = 0.616\) or \(\theta = -0.616\) (3 s.f.)
Teaching script

Write cot theta as cos over sine, then cross-multiply to clear both fractions in one go.

\[ \sin\theta\cos\theta + 4\sin^2\theta = 2\cos^2\theta + \sin\theta\cos\theta \]

The sine-cos term turns up on both sides and cancels out. That leaves four sine squared equals two cos squared. Divide by four cos squared.

\[ \tan^2\theta = \tfrac{1}{2} \;\Rightarrow\; \tan\theta = \pm\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \]

The range is minus pi over two to pi over two, so you're in radians. The basic angle is nought point six one five five. Positive tan gives that angle, and negative tan gives its negative, because the range reaches into the fourth quadrant.

\[ \theta = 0.616 \text{ or } -0.616 \]

Watch for: taking only the positive square root, or answering in degrees.

97/2162025 P2 Q6Whole question
Model solution

Use \(\mathrm{cosec}^2\theta = 1 + \cot^2\theta\):

\[ \begin{aligned} 2(1 + \cot^2\theta) - \cot\theta - 3 &= 0 \\ 2\cot^2\theta - \cot\theta - 1 &= 0 \\ (2\cot\theta + 1)(\cot\theta - 1) &= 0 \end{aligned} \]

\(\cot\theta = 1 \Rightarrow \tan\theta = 1\). Basic angle \(45^\circ\); \(\tan\theta \gt 0\) in quadrants 1 and 3.

\[ \theta = 45^\circ,\ 225^\circ \]

\(\cot\theta = -\tfrac{1}{2} \Rightarrow \tan\theta = -2\). Basic angle \(\tan^{-1} 2 = 63.435^\circ\); \(\tan\theta \lt 0\) in quadrants 2 and 4.

\[ \theta = 180^\circ - 63.435^\circ,\ 360^\circ - 63.435^\circ = 116.565^\circ,\ 296.565^\circ \]
\(\theta = 45^\circ,\ 116.6^\circ,\ 225^\circ,\ 296.6^\circ\)
Teaching script

This mixes cosec squared and cot. Swap cosec squared for one plus cot squared, so everything is in cot theta. Then it's a quadratic in disguise.

\[ 2\cot^2\theta - \cot\theta - 1 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; (2\cot\theta + 1)(\cot\theta - 1) = 0 \]

Take each factor separately, and switch to tan, since that's the button your calculator actually has. Cot theta equals one means tan theta equals one. The basic angle is forty-five degrees, and tan is positive in the first and third quadrants: forty-five and two hundred and twenty-five degrees.

Cot theta equals negative a half means tan theta equals negative two. The basic angle is inverse tan of two, sixty-three point four three five degrees. Tan is negative in the second and fourth quadrants.

\[ \theta = 116.565^\circ,\ 296.565^\circ \]

Altogether, theta is forty-five, a hundred and sixteen point six, two hundred and twenty-five and two hundred and ninety-six point six degrees.

Watch for: typing negative two into inverse tan and getting negative sixty-three degrees. Get the basic angle from positive two, then place it by quadrant.

R cos(θ ± α), R sin(θ ± α), usually in context

G1 Trigonometric functions, identities and equations › R cos(θ ± α), R sin(θ ± α), usually in context
98/2162019 P2 Q11Parts (i)(ii)Syllabus 4047 paper
Model solution for parts (i)(ii)
(i)

Draw the perpendicular from \(M\) to \(KN\). In the right-angled triangle formed with hypotenuse \(MN = 12\):

\[ \text{horizontal side} = 12\sin\theta = KL, \qquad \text{vertical side} = 12\cos\theta \] \[ KN = 5 + 12\cos\theta \] \[ \begin{aligned} P &= KL + LM + MN + NK \\ &= 12\sin\theta + 5 + 12 + 5 + 12\cos\theta \\ &= 22 + 12\cos\theta + 12\sin\theta \end{aligned} \]
(ii)
\[ R = \sqrt{12^2 + 12^2} = \sqrt{288} = 12\sqrt{2}, \qquad \tan\alpha = \frac{12}{12} = 1 \Rightarrow \alpha = \frac{\pi}{4} \]

The maximum value of \(12\sqrt{2}\cos\left(\theta - \frac{\pi}{4}\right)\) is \(12\sqrt{2}\).

\[ P_{\max} = 22 + 12\sqrt{2} = 38.97 \]
\(R = 12\sqrt{2}\ (\approx 16.97)\); maximum perimeter \(= 22 + 12\sqrt{2} \approx 39.0\) cm
Teaching script for parts (i)(ii)
(i)

You need every side of the trapezium in terms of theta. Drop a perpendicular from M down to KN. That gives you a right-angled triangle with hypotenuse MN, which is twelve.

The side opposite theta is twelve sin theta, and that's KL. The side next to theta is twelve cos theta, and it adds onto the five to make KN.

\[ KL = 12\sin\theta, \qquad KN = 5 + 12\cos\theta \]

Now add up the four sides: KL, LM which is five, MN which is twelve, and NK.

\[ P = 12\sin\theta + 5 + 12 + 5 + 12\cos\theta = 22 + 12\cos\theta + 12\sin\theta \]

Watch for: swapping sine and cosine in the triangle. Check which side is opposite theta in the diagram before you write either one.

(ii)

Write twelve cos theta plus twelve sin theta as R cos of theta minus alpha. R is the square root of twelve squared plus twelve squared.

\[ R = \sqrt{288} = 12\sqrt2, \qquad \tan\alpha = \tfrac{12}{12} = 1 \Rightarrow \alpha = \tfrac{\pi}{4} \]

Cosine is at most one, so the biggest the R cos term can be is twelve root two. Add the twenty-two.

\[ P_{\max} = 22 + 12\sqrt2 \approx 39.0 \]

The maximum perimeter is twenty-two plus twelve root two, about thirty-nine point nought centimetres.

Watch for: giving alpha as forty-five degrees when the question is in radians. Match the units the question uses.

99/2162020 P2 Q1Whole questionSyllabus 4047 paper
Model solution
(i)
\[ 7\cos\theta + 4\sin\theta = R\cos\theta\cos\alpha + R\sin\theta\sin\alpha \]

\(R\cos\alpha = 7\), \(R\sin\alpha = 4\).

\[ R = \sqrt{7^2 + 4^2} = \sqrt{65}, \qquad \tan\alpha = \tfrac{4}{7} \Rightarrow \alpha = 0.5191 \] \[ \begin{aligned} \sqrt{65}\cos(\theta - 0.5191) &= 6 \\ \cos(\theta - 0.5191) &= \frac{6}{\sqrt{65}} \end{aligned} \]

Basic angle \(= \cos^{-1}\dfrac{6}{\sqrt{65}} = 0.7314\). Since \(0 \le \theta \le \pi\), \(-0.5191 \le \theta - 0.5191 \le 2.622\).

\(\theta - 0.5191 = 0.7314\) is the only value in this range (\(-0.7314\) lies outside it).

\(\theta = 1.25\) rad (3 s.f.)
(ii)

\(80 - (7\cos\theta + 4\sin\theta)^2 = 80 - 65\cos^2(\theta - \alpha)\), where \(0 \le \cos^2(\theta - \alpha) \le 1\) for \(0 \le \theta \le \pi\).

Largest value when \(\cos(\theta - \alpha) = 0\): \(\theta - 0.5191 = \tfrac{\pi}{2}\), so \(\theta = 2.09\).

Smallest value when \(\cos^2(\theta - \alpha) = 1\): \(\theta - 0.5191 = 0\), so \(\theta = 0.519\).

Largest value \(= 80\) at \(\theta = 2.09\) rad; smallest value \(= 15\) at \(\theta = 0.519\) rad
Teaching script
(i)

They want R cos of theta minus alpha. Expand it and match it up with seven cos theta plus four sin theta: R cos alpha is seven, and R sin alpha is four.

\[ R\cos\alpha = 7, \quad R\sin\alpha = 4 \]

For R, square both and add: root of forty-nine plus sixteen, so root sixty-five. For alpha, divide: tan alpha is four sevenths, which gives zero point five one nine one radians. Hang on to four decimal places for now, because we'll use it again.

\[ R = \sqrt{65}, \quad \alpha = 0.5191 \]

Now solve root sixty-five cos of theta minus alpha equals six. Cos of theta minus alpha is six over root sixty-five, so the basic angle is zero point seven three one four.

\[ \cos(\theta - 0.5191) = \frac{6}{\sqrt{65}} \]

Here's the step people skip: adjust the range. Theta goes from zero to pi, so theta minus alpha goes from minus zero point five one nine one up to two point six two two. Cosine is positive in the first and fourth quadrants, but minus zero point seven three one four sits below the bottom of that range. So only one value makes it, and theta equals one point two five radians.

Watch for: skipping the range shift and handing in a second answer that doesn't exist. Shift the range by alpha first, then pick your angles.

(ii)

That bracket squared is sixty-five times cos squared of theta minus alpha. So the whole expression is eighty minus sixty-five cos squared.

\[ 80 - 65\cos^2(\theta - \alpha) \]

Cos squared can only be between zero and one. The expression is biggest when cos squared is zero: you get eighty, with theta minus alpha equal to pi over two, so theta is two point zero nine. It's smallest when cos squared is one: eighty minus sixty-five, which is fifteen, with theta minus alpha equal to zero, so theta is zero point five one nine.

\[ \max = 80 \text{ at } \theta = 2.09, \qquad \min = 15 \text{ at } \theta = 0.519 \]

Watch for: going for cos equals minus one to get the smallest value. It's squared, so plus one and minus one both give cos squared equal to one.

100/2162021 P2 Q4Whole question
Model solution
(a)

The height of \(B\) above the ground is \(6\sin\theta\). Since \(\angle ABC = 90^\circ\) and \(AB\) makes angle \(\theta\) with the horizontal, \(BC\) makes angle \(\theta\) with the vertical, so \(C\) is \(4\cos\theta\) above \(B\).

\[ h = 6\sin\theta + 4\cos\theta \]
\(h = 6\sin\theta + 4\cos\theta\) (shown)
(b)
\[ R\sin(\theta + \alpha) = R\sin\theta\cos\alpha + R\cos\theta\sin\alpha \] \[ R\cos\alpha = 6, \quad R\sin\alpha = 4 \] \[ R = \sqrt{6^2 + 4^2} = \sqrt{52} = 7.211\ldots, \qquad \tan\alpha = \frac46 \;\Rightarrow\; \alpha = 33.69^\circ \]
\(h = \sqrt{52}\,\sin(\theta + 33.7^\circ)\), i.e. \(h = 7.21\sin(\theta + 33.7^\circ)\)
(c)

\(h\) is greatest when \(\sin(\theta + 33.69^\circ) = 1\):

\[ \theta + 33.69^\circ = 90^\circ \;\Rightarrow\; \theta = 56.31^\circ \] \[ h_{\max} = \sqrt{52} = 7.21 \]
\(h = 7.21\) m when \(\theta = 56.3^\circ\)
Teaching script
(a)

Think of the height of C as two vertical pieces stacked up: from the ground to B, then from B up to C.

AB is six metres, tilted at theta to the horizontal, so B is six sine theta above the ground.

\[ \text{height of } B = 6\sin\theta \]

Angle ABC is a right angle. So if AB is tilted theta from the horizontal, BC is tilted theta from the vertical. The vertical rise along BC is the adjacent side, which makes it four cos theta.

\[ h = 6\sin\theta + 4\cos\theta \]

Watch for: writing \(4\sin\theta\) for the second piece. Mark the angle \(\theta\) at \(B\) against the vertical before you pick sine or cosine.

(b)

Expand R sine theta plus alpha: that's R sine theta cos alpha plus R cos theta sine alpha. Line it up with six sine theta plus four cos theta.

\[ R\cos\alpha = 6, \qquad R\sin\alpha = 4 \]

Square and add to get R: root of thirty-six plus sixteen, which is root fifty-two, about seven point two one. Divide to get alpha: tan alpha is four over six, so alpha is thirty-three point seven degrees.

\[ R = \sqrt{52} \approx 7.21, \qquad \alpha = 33.7^\circ \]

So h equals root fifty-two sine of theta plus thirty-three point seven degrees.

\[ h = 7.21\sin(\theta + 33.7^\circ) \]

Watch for: taking \(\tan\alpha = \frac64\). The \(\sin\alpha\) equation goes on top.

(c)

Sine can never go above one. So h is biggest when sine of theta plus thirty-three point seven degrees is one, which means the angle inside is ninety degrees.

\[ \theta + 33.69^\circ = 90^\circ \;\Rightarrow\; \theta = 56.3^\circ \]

The greatest height is R itself, seven point two one metres, and that happens when theta is fifty-six point three degrees.

Watch for: subtracting the rounded 33.7° and getting 56.3° from rounded working. Keep \(\alpha\) unrounded in your calculator until the very end.

Model solution
(a)

Distance of \(C\) below the border \(= 30\sin\theta\).

Since \(\angle ACD = 90^\circ\) and \(AC\) makes angle \(\theta\) with the border, \(CD\) makes angle \(\theta\) with the vertical through \(C\). So \(D\) is \(20\cos\theta\) nearer to the border than \(C\).

\[ \text{Shortest distance of } D \text{ from the border} = 30\sin\theta - 20\cos\theta. \]
\((30\sin\theta - 20\cos\theta)\) km (shown)
(b)
\[ R = \sqrt{30^2 + 20^2} = \sqrt{1300} = 36.06, \qquad \tan\alpha = \frac{20}{30} \Rightarrow \alpha = 33.69^\circ. \]
\(30\sin\theta - 20\cos\theta = \sqrt{1300}\sin(\theta - 33.7^\circ)\) \(\approx 36.1\sin(\theta - 33.7^\circ)\)
(c)
\[ \sqrt{1300}\sin(\theta - 33.69^\circ) = 10 \quad\Rightarrow\quad \sin(\theta - 33.69^\circ) = \frac{10}{\sqrt{1300}} = 0.2774. \]

\(\theta - 33.69^\circ = 16.10^\circ\) (the other value, \(163.90^\circ\), gives \(\theta \gt 90^\circ\), which is not possible in the diagram).

\(\theta = 49.8^\circ\)
Teaching script
(a)

Imagine the border as a flat line through A. You want how far D is below it. So find how far C is below the border, then take away how far D climbs back up.

AC is thirty kilometres at angle theta to the border, which puts C thirty sine theta below it.

\[ \text{C below border} = 30\sin\theta \]

Angle ACD is ninety degrees. If AC makes theta with the horizontal, then CD makes theta with the vertical through C. The vertical part of CD is the adjacent side, twenty cos theta, and it heads back up towards the border.

\[ \text{D below border} = 30\sin\theta - 20\cos\theta \]

Watch for: using twenty sine theta for the vertical bit of CD. Mark the angle with the vertical on the diagram before you write anything.

(b)

The target form is R sine of theta minus alpha. Expand it: R sine theta cos alpha minus R cos theta sine alpha. Match it up, and R cos alpha is thirty while R sine alpha is twenty.

\[ R = \sqrt{30^2 + 20^2} = \sqrt{1300} \approx 36.1, \qquad \tan\alpha = \tfrac{20}{30} \Rightarrow \alpha = 33.7^\circ \]

So the distance is root one thousand three hundred times sine of theta minus thirty-three point seven degrees. Keep alpha to two decimal places in your working, because part (c) needs it.

Watch for: tan alpha upside down, thirty over twenty. That gives fifty-six point three degrees, which is the wrong angle.

(c)

Set the expression equal to ten and get the sine on its own.

\[ \sin(\theta - 33.69^\circ) = \frac{10}{\sqrt{1300}} = 0.2774 \]

The basic angle is sixteen point one degrees. Sine's also positive in the second quadrant, giving one hundred and sixty-three point nine, but add thirty-three point seven to that and theta is obtuse. The diagram needs theta acute, so reject it and say so in a line.

\[ \theta - 33.69^\circ = 16.10^\circ \;\Rightarrow\; \theta = 49.8^\circ \]

Watch for: leaving the second solution sitting there with no comment. Say why it goes.

102/2162023 P2 Q8Whole question
Model solution
(a)

Triangle \(ABE\) is isosceles with \(AB = BE = r\) and base angles \(\theta\), so \(\angle ABE = 180^\circ - 2\theta\). By symmetry (\(BC \parallel AD\)), triangles \(ABE\), \(BEC\) and \(ECD\) are congruent.

\[ \begin{aligned} \text{Area} &= 3 \times \tfrac12 r^2 \sin(180^\circ - 2\theta) \\ &= \tfrac32 r^2 \sin 2\theta \end{aligned} \]
Area \(= \dfrac32 r^2 \sin 2\theta\ \text{m}^2\)
(b)

The maximum value of \(\sin 2\theta\) is 1, when \(2\theta = 90^\circ\).

Maximum area \(= \dfrac32 r^2\ \text{m}^2\), when \(\theta = 45^\circ\)
(c)

\(AE = ED = BC = 2r\cos\theta\) (base of each isosceles triangle). Total length of wood:

\[ \begin{aligned} 4r + 3(2r\cos\theta) &= 8r\sin\theta \\ 4 + 6\cos\theta &= 8\sin\theta \\ 4\sin\theta - 3\cos\theta &= 2 \quad \text{(shown)} \end{aligned} \]
(d)
\[ R = \sqrt{4^2 + 3^2} = 5, \qquad \tan\alpha = \tfrac34 \Rightarrow \alpha = 36.870^\circ \] \[ \begin{aligned} 5\sin(\theta - 36.870^\circ) &= 2 \\ \sin(\theta - 36.870^\circ) &= 0.4 \end{aligned} \]

Since \(0^\circ \lt \theta \lt 90^\circ\), \(-36.870^\circ \lt \theta - 36.870^\circ \lt 53.130^\circ\), so the only solution is

\[ \theta - 36.870^\circ = 23.578^\circ \;\Rightarrow\; \theta = 60.448^\circ \]
\(\theta = 60.4^\circ\) (1 d.p.)
Teaching script
(a)

Break the framework into three triangles: ABE, BEC and ECD. In triangle ABE, AB and BE are both r, so it's isosceles with base angles theta. That leaves one hundred and eighty degrees minus two theta at B.

The shape is symmetrical, so all three triangles are identical. Work out one with half a b sine C, then times by three.

\[ \text{Area} = 3 \times \tfrac12 r^2 \sin(180^\circ - 2\theta) = \tfrac32 r^2 \sin 2\theta \]

Sine of one hundred and eighty minus two theta is the same as sine two theta, so the area is three halves r squared sin two theta square metres.

Watch for: using theta as the angle between the two r sides. The angle you want is the one between them, at B.

(b)

The only thing that can change is sin two theta, and the biggest that ever gets is one. That happens when two theta is ninety degrees.

\[ \text{Maximum area} = \tfrac32 r^2 \ \text{m}^2 \ \text{ at } \ \theta = 45^\circ \]

Watch for: answering theta equals ninety degrees. That's two theta, so halve it.

(c)

Add up all seven pieces of wood. Four of them are length r. The other three, AE, ED and BC, are each the base of one of those isosceles triangles, and that base is two r cos theta.

\[ 4r + 6r\cos\theta = 8r\sin\theta \]

That total has to equal the one long piece, eight r sin theta. Divide through by r, then by two, rearrange, and you get four sin theta minus three cos theta equals two.

Watch for: counting only two horizontal pieces. BC across the top is the seventh one, and it's easy to miss.

(d)

For R sin theta minus alpha, R is the square root of four squared plus three squared. That's five, the good old three-four-five triangle. Tan alpha is three over four, so alpha is thirty-six point eight seven degrees.

\[ 5\sin(\theta - 36.870^\circ) = 2 \;\Rightarrow\; \sin(\theta - 36.870^\circ) = 0.4 \]

Theta is between zero and ninety, so theta minus alpha is between minus thirty-six point eight seven and fifty-three point one three. In that window, sine equals zero point four only once, at twenty-three point five seven eight degrees.

\[ \theta = 23.578^\circ + 36.870^\circ = 60.4^\circ \]

Watch for: adding the second-quadrant answer as well. It falls outside the shifted range, so it doesn't count.

103/2162024 P1 Q11Whole question
Model solution
(a)

The vertical height of \(B\) above \(AE\) is \(3\sin\theta\) (\(AB = 3\), inclined at \(\theta\) to the horizontal).

\(BC = 2\) makes angle \(\theta\) with the vertical \(CM\), so the vertical height of \(C\) above \(B\) is \(2\cos\theta\).

\[ CM = 2\cos\theta + 3\sin\theta \quad \text{(shown)} \]
(b)
\[ \begin{aligned} R\cos(\theta - \alpha) &= R\cos\theta\cos\alpha + R\sin\theta\sin\alpha \\ R\cos\alpha = 2, &\quad R\sin\alpha = 3 \\ R = \sqrt{2^2 + 3^2} = \sqrt{13}, &\quad \tan\alpha = \tfrac32 \Rightarrow \alpha = 56.310^\circ \end{aligned} \] \[ CM = \sqrt{13}\cos(\theta - 56.3^\circ) \]

The maximum value of \(\cos(\theta - 56.3^\circ)\) is 1 (when \(\theta = 56.3^\circ\)).

Maximum \(CM = \sqrt{13} = 3.61\) m (3 s.f.)
(c)
\[ \begin{aligned} \sqrt{13}\cos(\theta - 56.310^\circ) &= 3.5 \\ \cos(\theta - 56.310^\circ) &= \frac{3.5}{\sqrt{13}} \\ \theta - 56.310^\circ &= \pm 13.898^\circ \end{aligned} \]
\(\theta = 42.4^\circ\) or \(\theta = 70.2^\circ\)
Teaching script
(a)

CM is a vertical height, so build it out of two vertical pieces: how far you rise going from A to B, and how far you rise going from B to C.

AB is three metres at angle theta to the horizontal, so B sits three sin theta above AE. BC is two metres and makes angle theta with the vertical line CM, so going from B to C you rise two cos theta.

\[ CM = 2\cos\theta + 3\sin\theta \]

Watch for: using sine for both pieces. The angle at C is measured from the vertical, so that piece needs cosine.

(b)

Expand R cos theta minus alpha and match it to two cos theta plus three sin theta. That gives R cos alpha equals two and R sin alpha equals three.

\[ R = \sqrt{2^2 + 3^2} = \sqrt{13}, \qquad \tan\alpha = \tfrac32 \Rightarrow \alpha = 56.3^\circ \]

So CM is root thirteen cos of theta minus fifty-six point three degrees. Cosine never goes above one, so the longest CM can be is root thirteen, which is three point six one metres.

Watch for: getting tan alpha upside down. It's the sine number over the cosine number, three over two.

(c)

Set root thirteen cos theta minus alpha equal to three point five, and divide through by root thirteen.

\[ \cos(\theta - 56.310^\circ) = \frac{3.5}{\sqrt{13}} \;\Rightarrow\; \theta - 56.310^\circ = \pm 13.898^\circ \]

Cosine is positive in the first and fourth quadrants, so you take plus and minus the basic angle. That gives theta equals seventy point two degrees or forty-two point four degrees. Both are acute, so both fit the diagram, and you keep both.

Watch for: forgetting the minus case and losing forty-two point four degrees.

G1 Trigonometric functions, identities and equations › R cos(θ ± α), R sin(θ ± α), usually in context
104/2162025 P2 Q11Whole question
Model solution
(a)
Both speeds are positive, so \(\cos\theta \gt 0\) and \(\sin\theta \gt 0\). Both are positive only in the first quadrant, so \(\theta\) is acute.
(b)

Each object travels \(15\) cm in the same time \(t\):

\[ 6\cos\theta \cdot t = 15 \quad\text{and}\quad 8\sin\theta \cdot t = 15 \] \[ 6\cos\theta = 8\sin\theta \;\Rightarrow\; \tan\theta = \tfrac{3}{4} \;\Rightarrow\; \cos\theta = \tfrac{4}{5} \] \[ t = \frac{15}{6 \times \tfrac{4}{5}} = \frac{15}{4.8} = 3.125 \]
\(3.125\) s
(c)(i)

In 4 seconds the total distance covered is \(30\) cm:

\[ \begin{aligned} 4(6\cos\theta) + 4(8\sin\theta) &= 30 \\ 24\cos\theta + 32\sin\theta &= 30 \\ 3\cos\theta + 4\sin\theta &= 3.75 \end{aligned} \]
Shown
(c)(ii)
\[ R = \sqrt{3^2 + 4^2} = 5, \qquad \tan\alpha = \tfrac{4}{3} \;\Rightarrow\; \alpha = 53.130^\circ \] \[ 5\cos(\theta - 53.130^\circ) = 3.75 \;\Rightarrow\; \cos(\theta - 53.130^\circ) = 0.75 \]

Basic angle \(= 41.410^\circ\).

\[ \theta - 53.130^\circ = 41.410^\circ \ \text{ or } \ -41.410^\circ \] \[ \theta = 94.540^\circ \ (\text{rejected, not acute}) \ \text{ or } \ \theta = 11.720^\circ \] \[ 6\cos 11.720^\circ = 5.87, \qquad 8\sin 11.720^\circ = 1.63 \]
Speeds: \(5.87\) cm/s (from \(A\)) and \(1.63\) cm/s (from \(B\))
Teaching script
(a)

The speeds are six cos theta and eight sine theta. Speeds are positive, so cos theta and sine theta both have to be positive. That only happens in the first quadrant, so theta is acute.

Watch for: talking about only one of the speeds. You need both signs to pin it to the first quadrant.

(b)

They meet exactly halfway, so each one covers fifteen centimetres in the same time t. Write distance equals speed times time for both.

\[ 6\cos\theta \cdot t = 15,\qquad 8\sin\theta \cdot t = 15 \]

The right-hand sides match, so six cos theta equals eight sine theta. Divide and you get tan theta equals three quarters. That's our old friend the three-four-five triangle, so cos theta is four fifths.

\[ t = \frac{15}{6 \times \tfrac{4}{5}} = 3.125 \]

They meet after three point one two five seconds.

Watch for: rounding theta first and using a rounded cosine. The exact four fifths gives you an exact time.

(c)(i)

This time they meet after four seconds, so together they've covered the full thirty centimetres. Each distance is speed times four.

\[ 24\cos\theta + 32\sin\theta = 30 \]

Divide every term by eight, and you get three cos theta plus four sine theta equals three point seven five, as required.

Watch for: setting each distance to fifteen again. They don't meet halfway any more.

(c)(ii)

Write three cos theta plus four sine theta as R cos of theta minus alpha. R is root of nine plus sixteen, five. Tan alpha is four over three, so alpha is fifty-three point one three degrees.

\[ 5\cos(\theta - 53.130^\circ) = 3.75 \;\Rightarrow\; \cos(\theta - 53.130^\circ) = 0.75 \]

The basic angle is forty-one point four one degrees. Theta minus alpha can be positive or negative forty-one point four one. That gives theta as ninety-four point five four degrees, which we throw out because theta must be acute, or theta as eleven point seven two degrees.

\[ 6\cos 11.720^\circ = 5.87,\qquad 8\sin 11.720^\circ = 1.63 \]

So the speeds are five point eight seven centimetres per second from A and one point six three from B.

Watch for: missing the negative-angle solution. Here, the only answer that works comes from theta minus alpha equals negative forty-one point four one.

105/2162019 P1 Q12Whole questionSyllabus 4047 paper
Model solution
(i)

Since \(-1 \le \sin 2x \le 1\), \(-2 \le 2\sin 2x \le 2\).

Least value \(= -2\); greatest value \(= 2\)
(ii)

Since \(-1 \le \cos\frac{x}{2} \le 1\), \(-3-1 \le 3\cos\frac{x}{2}-1 \le 3-1\).

Least value \(= -4\); greatest value \(= 2\)
(iii)
\[ \text{Period} = \frac{360^\circ}{2} = 180^\circ \]
\(180^\circ\)
(iv)
\[ \text{Period} = \frac{360^\circ}{\tfrac{1}{2}} = 720^\circ \]
\(720^\circ\)
(v)

\(y = 2\sin 2x\): two complete cycles, starts at \((0, 0)\), maximum \(2\) at \(45^\circ\) and \(225^\circ\), minimum \(-2\) at \(135^\circ\) and \(315^\circ\), crosses the \(x\)-axis at \(90^\circ, 180^\circ, 270^\circ, 360^\circ\).

\(y = 3\cos\frac{x}{2} - 1\): half a cycle, starts at \((0, 2)\), decreases to \((360^\circ, -4)\), passes through \((180^\circ, -1)\).

(vi)

\(2\sin 2x + 1 = 3\cos\frac{x}{2}\) rearranges to \(2\sin 2x = 3\cos\frac{x}{2} - 1\), i.e. \(f(x) = g(x)\). The number of solutions is the number of intersections of the two graphs in part (v).

3 solutions
Teaching script
(i)

Sine of anything sits between minus one and one. Multiply the whole thing by two.

\[ -1 \le \sin 2x \le 1 \;\Rightarrow\; -2 \le 2\sin 2x \le 2 \]

So the least value is minus two and the greatest is two. The two inside the sine changes the period, not how high or low it goes.

Watch for: letting the 2x inside the bracket mess with the range.

(ii)

Same idea here. Cosine is between minus one and one, so three cos is between minus three and three. Then take one off both ends.

\[ -3-1 \le 3\cos\tfrac{x}{2}-1 \le 3-1 \]

The least value is minus four and the greatest is two.

Watch for: shifting only one end. Both ends move down by one.

(iii)

For sine of b x, the period is three hundred and sixty degrees divided by b. Here b is two.

\[ \text{Period} = \frac{360^\circ}{2} = 180^\circ \]

So the period is one hundred and eighty degrees.

Watch for: multiplying by two instead of dividing.

(iv)

Here b is a half, and dividing by a half doubles it.

\[ \text{Period} = \frac{360^\circ}{\tfrac12} = 720^\circ \]

The period is seven hundred and twenty degrees. That means between nought and three sixty you only see half a cycle.

Watch for: giving one hundred and eighty again because x over two looked a bit like 2x.

(v)

Now draw both on the same axes from nought to three sixty, using everything you've worked out.

First curve, two sin 2x. Period one eighty, so two full waves. Start at the origin, up to two at forty-five, back through the axis at ninety, down to minus two at one thirty-five, through the axis at one eighty, and do it all again: two at two twenty-five, minus two at three fifteen, finishing on the axis at three sixty.

Second curve, three cos x over two, minus one. Only half a cycle, so it's a lazy one. Start at two when x is nought, slide down through minus one at one eighty, and finish at minus four at three sixty.

Label each curve with its equation and mark the maximum and minimum values on the y-axis.

Watch for: drawing a full cycle of the cosine curve. Its period is seven twenty, so in this range it only goes down once.

(vi)

Rearrange the equation so each side matches one of your graphs. Move the one over to the right-hand side.

\[ 2\sin 2x + 1 = 3\cos\tfrac{x}{2} \;\Leftrightarrow\; 2\sin 2x = 3\cos\tfrac{x}{2} - 1 \]

Now the solutions are where the two curves cross. Count the crossings on your sketch.

There are three solutions.

Watch for: counting before you rearrange. If the equation doesn't match the curves you drew exactly, the count means nothing.

G1 Trigonometric functions, identities and equations › Graphs of sine and cosine
106/2162021 P1 Q7Whole question
Model solution
(a)

The curve oscillates symmetrically about the line \(y = c\). The maximum value is 2 and the minimum value is \(-8\).

\[ c = \frac{2 + (-8)}{2} = -3 \]
\(c\) is the mean of the maximum and minimum values, so \(c = -3\).
(b)

From the maximum at \(x = \tfrac{\pi}{8}\) to the next minimum at \(x = \tfrac{3\pi}{8}\) is half a period: \(\tfrac{3\pi}{8} - \tfrac{\pi}{8} = \tfrac{\pi}{4}\).

\[ \text{Period} = \frac{\pi}{2} = \frac{2\pi}{b} \;\Rightarrow\; b = 4 \]
The period is \(\tfrac{\pi}{2}\), so \(b = \dfrac{2\pi}{\pi/2} = 4\).
(c)

Amplitude \(= \dfrac{2 - (-8)}{2} = 5\). At \(x = \tfrac{\pi}{8}\), \(\sin\left(4 \times \tfrac{\pi}{8}\right) = \sin\tfrac{\pi}{2} = 1\) and \(y = 2\), so \(a(1) - 3 = 2 \Rightarrow a = 5\).

\(y = 5\sin 4x - 3\)
Teaching script
(a)

The graph goes up to two and down to minus eight. The whole sine curve has been shifted so that its centre line is y equals c, which sits halfway between the maximum and the minimum.

\[ c = \frac{2 + (-8)}{2} = -3 \]

Watch for: taking c as the minimum value. c is the average of the maximum and minimum.

(b)

Going from a maximum to the next minimum is half a period. Here that's from pi over eight to three pi over eight, a gap of pi over four. So the full period is pi over two.

\[ \frac{2\pi}{b} = \frac{\pi}{2} \;\Rightarrow\; b = 4 \]

Watch for: treating π/4 as the full period. Max to min is only half the journey.

(c)

The amplitude is half the distance from top to bottom: two minus minus eight is ten, and half of that is five. Double-check with the maximum point: at pi over eight, sin four x is sin pi over two, which is one. So a minus three is two, and a is five.

\[ y = 5\sin 4x - 3 \]

Watch for: taking a = 10. The amplitude is half the range, not the whole thing.

Model solution
(a)(i)
Amplitude \(= 2\); period \(= 2\pi\)
(a)(ii)

Amplitude is the coefficient magnitude \(|-4| = 4\); period \(= \dfrac{2\pi}{2} = \pi\).

Amplitude \(= 4\); period \(= \pi\)
(b)

\(y = 2\sin x\): one full cycle, \(-2 \le y \le 2\), passing through \((0,0)\), \((\pi,0)\), \((2\pi,0)\), maximum at \(\left(\frac\pi2, 2\right)\), minimum at \(\left(\frac{3\pi}2, -2\right)\).

\(y = 3 - 4\cos 2x\): two full cycles, \(-1 \le y \le 7\), minima \(-1\) at \(x = 0, \pi, 2\pi\), maxima \(7\) at \(x = \frac\pi2, \frac{3\pi}2\).

Both curves drawn with correct shape, period, maximum and minimum values, and end points.
Teaching script
(a)(i)

For a times sine b x, the amplitude is the size of a and the period is two pi over b. Here a is two and b is one, so the amplitude is two and the period is two pi.

\[ \text{amplitude } 2, \quad \text{period } 2\pi \]

Watch for: giving the period in degrees. This question is in radians.

(a)(ii)

For three minus four cos two x, the three only lifts the graph up, so it has nothing to do with the amplitude. The amplitude is the size of minus four, which is four. The period is two pi over two, which is pi.

\[ \text{amplitude } 4, \quad \text{period } \pi \]

Watch for: saying the amplitude is \(-4\) or 7. Amplitude is a positive size, and it ignores the vertical shift.

(b)

Draw both curves on one set of axes from nought to two pi, and mark pi over two, pi, three pi over two and two pi.

Two sine x is one full wave. It starts at the origin, goes up to two at pi over two, comes back through pi, drops to minus two at three pi over two, and is back at zero at two pi.

Three minus four cos two x is a cos turned upside down and lifted by three, so it starts at its lowest point, minus one, when x is nought. It goes up to seven at pi over two, down to minus one at pi, up to seven at three pi over two, and ends at minus one at two pi. That's two full waves.

\[ -2 \le 2\sin x \le 2, \qquad -1 \le 3 - 4\cos 2x \le 7 \]

Label each curve, and mark the maximum and minimum values on the y-axis.

Watch for: starting the second curve at a maximum. The minus sign flips the cosine upside down, so it starts at \(3 - 4 = -1\).

(a)

Consecutive maxima are one period apart: period \(= \dfrac{9\pi}{2} - \dfrac{7\pi}{2} = \pi\).

\[ \frac{2\pi}{b} = \pi \;\Rightarrow\; b = 2 \]

At \(x = \dfrac{7\pi}{2}\): \(10 = a\cos 7\pi = -a\), so \(a = -10\).

\(a = -10,\ b = 2\)
Teaching script for parts (a)
(a)

Two maximum points next to each other are exactly one period apart. Nine pi over two minus seven pi over two is pi, so the period is pi.

\[ \frac{2\pi}{b} = \pi \;\Rightarrow\; b = 2 \]

For a cos bx, the period is two pi over b, so b is two. Now use the maximum point. At x equals seven pi over two, the cosine part is cos seven pi, which is minus one. So ten equals minus a, which makes a minus ten.

Don't panic about a being negative. It flips the cosine curve upside down, so the peaks sit where cos bx is minus one.

Watch for: saying a is ten because the maximum value is ten. Substitute the point and let the algebra decide.
