109/2162024 P1 Q10Whole question
Model solution
(a)
Amplitude \(= 4\); period \(= \dfrac{2\pi}{2} = \pi\)
(b)

Two complete cycles between \(0\) and \(2\pi\): maximum points \((0, 4), (\pi, 4), (2\pi, 4)\); minimum points \(\left(\frac{\pi}{2}, -4\right), \left(\frac{3\pi}{2}, -4\right)\); \(x\)-intercepts at \(\frac{\pi}{4}, \frac{3\pi}{4}, \frac{5\pi}{4}, \frac{7\pi}{4}\).

(c)
\[ \pi\cos 2x + x = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; \cos 2x = -\frac{x}{\pi} \;\Rightarrow\; 4\cos 2x = -\frac{4x}{\pi} \]

Draw the line \(y = -\dfrac{4x}{\pi}\), which passes through \((0, 0)\) and \((\pi, -4)\). For \(x \gt \pi\) the line lies below \(y = -4\), so it cannot meet the curve again.

The line meets the curve at two points.

Number of solutions \(= 2\)
Teaching script
(a)

For a cos bx, the amplitude is the size of a, and the period is two pi over b. Here a is four and b is two.

\[ \text{Amplitude} = 4, \qquad \text{period} = \frac{2\pi}{2} = \pi \]

Watch for: writing two pi for the period. That's plain cos x; the two in front of x squashes it to pi.

(b)

The period is pi, so between zero and two pi you get two full waves. A cosine curve starts at the top, so your first point is zero, four.

The peaks are at zero, pi and two pi, all at height four. The troughs are at pi over two and three pi over two, down at minus four. The curve crosses the x-axis halfway between each peak and trough: pi over four, three pi over four, five pi over four and seven pi over four.

\[ \max: (0,4), (\pi,4), (2\pi,4); \quad \min: \left(\tfrac{\pi}{2},-4\right), \left(\tfrac{3\pi}{2},-4\right) \]

Label the axes, mark four and minus four on the y-axis, and draw it smooth. A cosine curve is a wave, not a zigzag.

Watch for: drawing only one wave, or a curve that doesn't reach exactly four and minus four.

(c)

The idea is to rewrite the equation as "curve equals line", so you can count crossings on the sketch you've already drawn. Rearrange to cos 2x equals minus x over pi, then times by four so the left side matches your curve.

\[ 4\cos 2x = -\frac{4x}{\pi} \]

Now draw the line y equals minus four x over pi. It goes through the origin and through pi, minus four. After x equals pi it drops below minus four, so it can never meet the curve again.

Count the crossings between zero and pi on your sketch. There are two, so the equation has two solutions.

Watch for: drawing the line with the wrong gradient because you forgot to times by four.

G1 Trigonometric functions, identities and equations › Graphs of sine and cosine
110/2162025 P1 Q12Whole question
Model solution
(a)(i)
\[ a\cos kx = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; kx = \frac{\pi}{2},\ \frac{3\pi}{2} \]
\(A\): \(x = \dfrac{\pi}{2k}\); \(\ B\): \(x = \dfrac{3\pi}{2k}\)
(a)(ii)
\[ AB = \frac{3\pi}{2k} - \frac{\pi}{2k} = \frac{\pi}{k} \]

\(C\) is the minimum point, \(\left(\dfrac{\pi}{k},\ -a\right)\), midway between \(A\) and \(B\). The perpendicular height of triangle \(ABC\) from \(C\) to \(AB\) is \(a\).

For an equilateral triangle of side \(\dfrac{\pi}{k}\), the height is \(\dfrac{\pi}{k}\sin 60^\circ\).

\[ a = \frac{\pi}{k} \times \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = \frac{\pi\sqrt{3}}{2k} \]
\(a = \dfrac{\pi\sqrt{3}}{2k}\) (proved)
(b)
\[ \begin{aligned} \tfrac{1}{2} \times \frac{\pi}{k} \times \frac{\pi\sqrt{3}}{2k} &= 1 \\ k^2 &= \frac{\sqrt{3}\,\pi^2}{4} \\ k &= \frac{3^{\frac{1}{4}}\pi}{2} \quad (k \gt 0) \end{aligned} \] \[ \text{Amplitude } a = \frac{\pi\sqrt{3}}{2} \times \frac{2}{3^{\frac{1}{4}}\pi} = 3^{\frac{1}{4}} \approx 1.32 \] \[ \text{Period} = \frac{2\pi}{k} = \frac{4}{3^{\frac{1}{4}}} \approx 3.04 \]
\(k = \dfrac{3^{\frac{1}{4}}\pi}{2}\) (shown); amplitude \(= 3^{\frac{1}{4}} = 1.32\); period \(= \dfrac{4}{3^{\frac{1}{4}}} = 3.04\)
Teaching script
(a)(i)

A and B are where y equals a cos k x crosses the x-axis, so set a cos k x equal to zero. Cosine is zero at pi over two and three pi over two, the first two crossings after the origin.

\[ kx = \frac{\pi}{2},\ \frac{3\pi}{2} \]

Now divide by k. A is at pi over two k, and B is at three pi over two k.

Watch for: stopping at pi over two and three pi over two. Those are values of k x, not x, so you still have to divide by k.

(a)(ii)

The length AB is the gap between the two x-values, which is pi over k.

\[ AB = \frac{3\pi}{2k} - \frac{\pi}{2k} = \frac{\pi}{k} \]

C is the lowest point, halfway between A and B, sitting at negative a. So the height of triangle ABC, from C up to AB, is a. In an equilateral triangle, the height is the side times sine sixty, and sine sixty is root three over two.

\[ a = \frac{\pi}{k} \times \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = \frac{\pi\sqrt{3}}{2k} \]

And that's the result they wanted.

Watch for: using negative a as the height. Heights are lengths, so it's a.

(b)

The triangle's area is one square unit. Half base times height, with pi over k as the base and part (ii)'s expression for a as the height.

\[ \tfrac{1}{2} \times \frac{\pi}{k} \times \frac{\pi\sqrt{3}}{2k} = 1 \;\Rightarrow\; k^2 = \frac{\sqrt{3}\,\pi^2}{4} \]

Take the positive root. Root of root three is three to the power one quarter, so k is three to the quarter, times pi, over two.

\[ k = \frac{3^{\frac{1}{4}}\pi}{2} \]

For the amplitude, put this k into a equals pi root three over two k. The pi and the two cancel, and root three over three to the quarter leaves three to the quarter, about one point three two. The period is two pi over k, which is four over three to the quarter, about three point nought four.

Watch for: giving pi over k as the period. That's AB, which is only half a wave.

Exact values and compound angles

4 questions
G1 Trigonometric functions, identities and equations › Exact values and compound angles
111/2162020 P1 Q12Whole questionSyllabus 4047 paper
Model solution
(i)
\[ \begin{aligned} \text{LHS} &= \frac{(\sin\alpha\cos\beta + \cos\alpha\sin\beta) + (\sin\alpha\cos\beta - \cos\alpha\sin\beta)}{(\cos\alpha\cos\beta - \sin\alpha\sin\beta) + (\cos\alpha\cos\beta + \sin\alpha\sin\beta)} \\ &= \frac{2\sin\alpha\cos\beta}{2\cos\alpha\cos\beta} = \frac{\sin\alpha}{\cos\alpha} = \tan\alpha = \text{RHS} \end{aligned} \]
(ii)

Both angles are acute, so \(-90^\circ \lt A - B \lt 90^\circ\). Since \(\sin(A - B) \lt 0\), \(A - B \lt 0\).

\(B\) is larger than \(A\)
(iii)

Since \(-90^\circ \lt A - B \lt 0^\circ\), \(\cos(A-B) \gt 0\).

\[ \cos(A-B) = \sqrt{1 - \left(-\tfrac{3}{5}\right)^2} = \sqrt{\tfrac{16}{25}} = \tfrac{4}{5} \]
\(\cos(A-B) = \dfrac{4}{5}\)
(iv)
\[ \sin(A+B) = \tan(A+B)\cos(A+B) = \frac{24}{7}\times\frac{7}{25} = \frac{24}{25} \]
\(\sin(A+B) = \dfrac{24}{25}\)
(v)

Put \(\alpha = A\), \(\beta = B\) in the identity from part (i):

\[ \tan A = \frac{\sin(A+B) + \sin(A-B)}{\cos(A+B) + \cos(A-B)} = \frac{\frac{24}{25} - \frac{15}{25}}{\frac{7}{25} + \frac{20}{25}} = \frac{9}{27} = \frac{1}{3} \]
(vi)
\[ \begin{aligned} \tan(A+B) &= \frac{\tan A + \tan B}{1 - \tan A\tan B} \\ \frac{24}{7} &= \frac{\frac{1}{3} + \tan B}{1 - \frac{1}{3}\tan B} \\ \frac{24}{7} - \frac{8}{7}\tan B &= \frac{1}{3} + \tan B \\ \frac{65}{21} &= \frac{15}{7}\tan B \\ \tan B &= \frac{65}{45} = \frac{13}{9} \end{aligned} \]
\(\tan B = \dfrac{13}{9}\)
Teaching script
(i)

Start with the left-hand side and expand all four compound angles with the addition formulae.

\[ \frac{(\sin\alpha\cos\beta + \cos\alpha\sin\beta) + (\sin\alpha\cos\beta - \cos\alpha\sin\beta)}{(\cos\alpha\cos\beta - \sin\alpha\sin\beta) + (\cos\alpha\cos\beta + \sin\alpha\sin\beta)} \]

On top, the cos alpha sin beta terms cancel. Underneath, the sin alpha sin beta terms cancel.

\[ = \frac{2\sin\alpha\cos\beta}{2\cos\alpha\cos\beta} = \frac{\sin\alpha}{\cos\alpha} = \tan\alpha \]

The two cos beta cancels, and you're left with tan alpha, the right-hand side.

Watch for: the sign inside cos of alpha plus beta. It's cos cos minus sin sin.

(ii)

Both angles are acute, so A minus B is somewhere between minus ninety and ninety degrees. In that range, sine is only negative when the angle is negative. sin of A minus B is negative, so A minus B is negative, which means B is bigger than A.

Watch for: writing "B is larger" with no reason. Give the range of A minus B and the sign argument.

(iii)

A minus B is between minus ninety and nought, the fourth quadrant, where cosine is positive. Use sine squared plus cosine squared equals one.

\[ \cos(A-B) = \sqrt{1 - \tfrac{9}{25}} = \tfrac45 \]

cos of A minus B is four fifths, the positive root.

Watch for: writing minus four fifths. Sine is negative in this quadrant, but cosine isn't.

(iv)

You know tan of A plus B and cos of A plus B. Sine is tan times cos.

\[ \sin(A+B) = \tfrac{24}{7}\times\tfrac{7}{25} = \tfrac{24}{25} \]

The sevens cancel, giving twenty-four over twenty-five.

Watch for: dividing instead of multiplying. tan is sin over cos, so sin is tan times cos.

(v)

Use the identity from part (i) with alpha as A and beta as B. You now know every value you need.

\[ \tan A = \frac{\tfrac{24}{25} - \tfrac{15}{25}}{\tfrac{7}{25} + \tfrac{20}{25}} = \frac{9}{27} = \tfrac13 \]

sin of A minus B is minus three fifths, which is minus fifteen over twenty-five. cos of A minus B is twenty over twenty-five. The answer is a third.

Watch for: losing the minus sign on sin of A minus B. It's been with you since part (ii), don't abandon it now.

(vi)

Use the tan addition formula with tan A equals a third and tan of A plus B equals twenty-four sevenths. Solve for tan B.

\[ \frac{24}{7} = \frac{\tfrac13 + \tan B}{1 - \tfrac13\tan B} \]

Cross-multiply, then collect the tan B terms on one side.

\[ \tfrac{24}{7} - \tfrac87\tan B = \tfrac13 + \tan B \;\Rightarrow\; \tfrac{65}{21} = \tfrac{15}{7}\tan B \;\Rightarrow\; \tan B = \tfrac{13}{9} \]

Watch for: expanding twenty-four sevenths times one minus a third tan B. Both terms in the bracket get multiplied.

G1 Trigonometric functions, identities and equations › Exact values and compound angles
112/2162022 P2 Q5Whole question
Model solution
(a)
\[ \begin{aligned} \tan 75^\circ &= \tan(45^\circ + 30^\circ) = \frac{\tan 45^\circ + \tan 30^\circ}{1 - \tan 45^\circ\tan 30^\circ} \\ &= \frac{1 + \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}}{1 - \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}} = \frac{\sqrt{3}+1}{\sqrt{3}-1} \\ &= \frac{(\sqrt{3}+1)^2}{(\sqrt{3}-1)(\sqrt{3}+1)} = \frac{4 + 2\sqrt{3}}{2} \\ &= 2 + \sqrt{3}. \end{aligned} \]
\(\tan 75^\circ = 2 + \sqrt{3}\) (shown)
(b)
\[ \sec^2 75^\circ = 1 + \tan^2 75^\circ = 1 + (2+\sqrt{3})^2 = 1 + 4 + 4\sqrt{3} + 3 = 8 + 4\sqrt{3}. \]
\(\sec^2 75^\circ = 8 + 4\sqrt{3}\)
(c)
\[ \cos^2 75^\circ = \frac{1}{8 + 4\sqrt{3}} = \frac{8 - 4\sqrt{3}}{(8+4\sqrt{3})(8-4\sqrt{3})} = \frac{8 - 4\sqrt{3}}{64 - 48} = \frac{8 - 4\sqrt{3}}{16}. \]
\(\cos^2 75^\circ = \dfrac{1}{2} - \dfrac{1}{4}\sqrt{3}\)
Teaching script
(a)

No calculator, so break seventy-five into two angles whose tangents you know by heart: forty-five and thirty. Then the addition formula for tan takes over.

\[ \tan 75^\circ = \frac{\tan 45^\circ + \tan 30^\circ}{1 - \tan 45^\circ\tan 30^\circ} = \frac{1 + \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}}{1 - \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}} = \frac{\sqrt{3}+1}{\sqrt{3}-1} \]

Multiply top and bottom by root three over root three to get rid of the little fractions. Then rationalise by multiplying top and bottom by root three plus one.

\[ \frac{(\sqrt{3}+1)^2}{3 - 1} = \frac{4 + 2\sqrt{3}}{2} = 2 + \sqrt{3} \]

Watch for: expanding root three plus one, squared, as four. The middle term, two root three, loves to go missing.

(b)

Secant squared connects to tan squared through sec squared equals one plus tan squared. Square your part (a) answer, slowly.

\[ \sec^2 75^\circ = 1 + (2+\sqrt{3})^2 = 1 + 4 + 4\sqrt{3} + 3 = 8 + 4\sqrt{3} \]

Watch for: writing sec squared as tan squared minus one. It's plus one.

(c)

Cos squared is one over sec squared, so flip part (b) upside down and rationalise using the conjugate, eight minus four root three.

\[ \cos^2 75^\circ = \frac{8 - 4\sqrt{3}}{64 - 48} = \frac{8 - 4\sqrt{3}}{16} = \frac{1}{2} - \frac{1}{4}\sqrt{3} \]

The denominator becomes sixty-four minus forty-eight, which is sixteen. Then split the fraction into the form the question asks for.

Watch for: squaring four root three as twelve. It's sixteen times three, forty-eight.

G1 Trigonometric functions, identities and equations › Exact values and compound angles
113/2162023 P2 Q4Whole question
Model solution
(a)

Let \(t = \tan 15^\circ\). Using \(\tan 2A = \dfrac{2\tan A}{1 - \tan^2 A}\) with \(A = 15^\circ\):

\[ \begin{aligned} \tan 30^\circ = \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} &= \frac{2t}{1 - t^2} \\ 1 - t^2 &= 2\sqrt{3}\,t \\ t^2 + 2\sqrt{3}\,t - 1 &= 0 \\ t &= \frac{-2\sqrt{3} \pm \sqrt{12 + 4}}{2} = -\sqrt{3} \pm 2. \end{aligned} \]

Since \(15^\circ\) is acute, \(\tan 15^\circ \gt 0\), so reject \(-\sqrt{3} - 2\).

\(\tan 15^\circ = 2 - \sqrt{3}\) (shown)
(b)
\[ \begin{aligned} \tan 105^\circ &= \tan(60^\circ + 45^\circ) = \frac{\sqrt{3} + 1}{1 - \sqrt{3}} \\ &= \frac{(\sqrt{3} + 1)(1 + \sqrt{3})}{(1 - \sqrt{3})(1 + \sqrt{3})} = \frac{4 + 2\sqrt{3}}{-2} = -2 - \sqrt{3}. \end{aligned} \] \[ \tan 15^\circ - \tan 105^\circ = (2 - \sqrt{3}) - (-2 - \sqrt{3}) = 4. \]
\(\tan 15^\circ - \tan 105^\circ = 4\) (proved)
Teaching script
(a)

Fifteen degrees is half of thirty, and you know tan thirty exactly. So use the double-angle formula, with t standing for tan fifteen.

\[ \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} = \frac{2t}{1 - t^2} \;\Rightarrow\; t^2 + 2\sqrt{3}\,t - 1 = 0 \]

That's a quadratic in t. Use the formula: b squared minus four a c is twelve plus four, which is sixteen.

\[ t = \frac{-2\sqrt{3} \pm 4}{2} = -\sqrt{3} \pm 2 \]

Fifteen degrees is acute, so its tangent is positive. Reject minus root three minus two. Tan fifteen is two minus root three.

Watch for: rejecting the negative root without a reason. One line saying fifteen degrees is acute covers it.

(b)

Write one hundred and five as sixty plus forty-five and use the tan addition formula.

\[ \tan 105^\circ = \frac{\sqrt{3} + 1}{1 - \sqrt{3}} = \frac{4 + 2\sqrt{3}}{-2} = -2 - \sqrt{3} \]

Rationalise by multiplying top and bottom by one plus root three, and the bottom becomes one minus three, which is minus two. Then subtract.

\[ (2 - \sqrt{3}) - (-2 - \sqrt{3}) = 4 \]

Watch for: ending up with a positive tan one hundred and five. It's in the second quadrant, so tan is negative.

G1 Trigonometric functions, identities and equations › Exact values and compound angles
114/2162024 P1 Q3Whole question
Model solution

In right-angled triangles \(PXQ\) and \(PXR\):

\[ \tan\alpha = \frac{2}{x}, \qquad \tan\beta = \frac{3}{x} \] \[ \begin{aligned} \tan(\alpha + \beta) &= \frac{\tan\alpha + \tan\beta}{1 - \tan\alpha\tan\beta} \\ 2 &= \frac{\frac5x}{1 - \frac{6}{x^2}} = \frac{5x}{x^2 - 6} \\ 2x^2 - 12 &= 5x \\ 2x^2 - 5x - 12 &= 0 \\ (2x + 3)(x - 4) &= 0 \end{aligned} \]

\(x = -\tfrac32\) is rejected since \(x\) is a length.

\(x = 4\)
Teaching script

They give you tan of alpha plus beta, and the diagram has two right-angled triangles sharing the side PX. So the plan is to write tan alpha and tan beta in terms of x, then use the compound-angle formula.

In triangle PXQ, tan alpha is opposite over adjacent, two over x. In triangle PXR, tan beta is three over x.

\[ \tan\alpha = \frac{2}{x}, \qquad \tan\beta = \frac{3}{x} \]

Tan alpha plus beta is the two tans added, over one minus the two tans multiplied. On top that's five over x; underneath it's one minus six over x squared. Fractions inside fractions are no fun, so multiply top and bottom by x squared.

\[ 2 = \frac{5x}{x^2 - 6} \;\Rightarrow\; 2x^2 - 5x - 12 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; (2x + 3)(x - 4) = 0 \]

X is a length, so minus three halves is out. The answer is x equals four.

Watch for: putting a plus sign underneath in the tan addition formula. It's one minus the product.

Ratios of an acute angle from one given ratio

1 question
G1 Trigonometric functions, identities and equations › Ratios of an acute angle from one given ratio
115/2162019 P1 Q1Whole questionSyllabus 4047 paper
Model solution
(i)

Since \(\theta\) is acute, \(\sin\theta \gt 0\).

\[ \begin{aligned} \sin^2\theta &= 1-\cos^2\theta = 1-c^2 \\ \sin\theta &= \sqrt{1-c^2} \\ \tan\theta &= \frac{\sin\theta}{\cos\theta} = \frac{\sqrt{1-c^2}}{c} \end{aligned} \]
\(\tan\theta = \dfrac{\sqrt{1-c^2}}{c}\)
(ii)
\[ \operatorname{cosec}\theta = \frac{1}{\sin\theta} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-c^2}} \]
\(\operatorname{cosec}\theta = \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{1-c^2}}\)
Teaching script
(i)

They give you cos theta as c and want tan theta. Tan is sine over cosine, so the only thing missing is sine. We get that from the old favourite, sine squared plus cosine squared equals one.

\[ \sin^2\theta = 1-\cos^2\theta = 1-c^2 \]

Square root it. Now, the question tells you theta is acute, so sine is positive and you keep the positive root only.

\[ \sin\theta = \sqrt{1-c^2} \]

Then divide by cosine, which is c.

\[ \tan\theta = \frac{\sin\theta}{\cos\theta} = \frac{\sqrt{1-c^2}}{c} \]

So tan theta is root of one minus c squared, all over c.

Watch for: putting plus or minus in front of the root. The word "acute" is there to kill off the negative sign, so write one line saying so.

(ii)

Cosec is one over sine, and you already found sine in part (i). One line and you're done.

\[ \operatorname{cosec}\theta = \frac{1}{\sin\theta} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-c^2}} \]

The answer is one over root of one minus c squared.

Watch for: mixing up cosec and sec. A quick way to remember: cosec goes with sine, sec goes with cosine. The "co" words pair with the non-"co" functions.

Trigonometric models

2 questions
G1 Trigonometric functions, identities and equations › Trigonometric models
116/2162022 P1 Q12Whole question
Model solution
(a)

The dot moves between \(A\) and \(B\), with \(O\) the midpoint of \(AB = 120\) cm. The greatest displacement from \(O\) is \(60\) cm, and the greatest value of \(a\sin nt\) is \(a\), so \(a = 60\).

One oscillation takes 6 s, so the period of \(\sin nt\) is 6:

\[ \frac{2\pi}{n} = 6 \;\Rightarrow\; n = \frac{\pi}{3} \]
\(a = 60\); \(n = \tfrac13\pi\) (shown)
(b)
\[ x = 60\sin\frac{\pi t}{3} \;\Rightarrow\; v = \frac{dx}{dt} = 60\left(\frac\pi3\right)\cos\frac{\pi t}{3} = 20\pi\cos\frac{\pi t}{3} \]

Since \(-1 \le \cos\frac{\pi t}{3} \le 1\), the maximum speed is \(20\pi\).

\(v = 20\pi\cos\dfrac{\pi t}{3}\) cm/s; maximum speed \(= 20\pi \approx 62.8\) cm/s
(c)
\[ \text{acceleration} = \frac{dv}{dt} = -\frac{20\pi^2}{3}\sin\frac{\pi t}{3} \]

At \(A\), \(x = -60\), so \(\sin\frac{\pi t}{3} = -1\):

\[ |\text{acceleration}| = \frac{20\pi^2}{3} = 65.79\ldots \]
\(\dfrac{20\pi^2}{3} \approx 65.8\) cm/s²
Teaching script
(a)

The dot swings between A and B, which are a hundred and twenty centimetres apart, with O right in the middle. So the furthest it ever gets from O is sixty. The biggest value of a sine n t is a, so a is sixty.

\[ a = 60 \]

One full swing takes six seconds, so the period of sine n t is six. And period is two pi over n.

\[ \frac{2\pi}{n} = 6 \;\Rightarrow\; n = \frac{\pi}{3} \]

Watch for: taking \(a = 120\). The amplitude is measured from the centre, so it's half of \(AB\).

(b)

Velocity is the derivative of displacement. Sine becomes cos, and the chain rule brings out a pi over three. Sixty times pi over three is twenty pi.

\[ v = 20\pi\cos\frac{\pi t}{3} \]

Cos never goes above one in size, so the top speed is twenty pi, about sixty-two point eight centimetres per second.

\[ 20\pi \approx 62.8 \text{ cm/s} \]

Watch for: leaving out the factor \(\frac\pi3\) from the chain rule.

(c)

Differentiate again for acceleration. Cos becomes minus sine, and another pi over three comes out.

\[ \frac{dv}{dt} = -\frac{20\pi^2}{3}\sin\frac{\pi t}{3} \]

At A, x is minus sixty, so sine pi t over three is minus one. The size of the acceleration is twenty pi squared over three, about sixty-five point eight centimetres per second squared.

\[ \frac{20\pi^2}{3} \approx 65.8 \text{ cm/s}^2 \]

Watch for: giving a negative magnitude. They asked for the size, so it's positive.

G1 Trigonometric functions, identities and equations › Trigonometric models
117/2162023 P2 Q7Parts (b)
Model solution for parts (b)
(b)(i)

The particle never changes direction if \(v\) never becomes negative. The minimum value of \(5\sin 0.2t\) is \(-5\), so the minimum velocity is \(c - 5\).

\[ c - 5 \ge 0 \;\Rightarrow\; c \ge 5 \]
Smallest value of \(c = 5\)
(b)(ii)

With \(c = 8\), \(v \ge 8 - 5 = 3 \gt 0\) for all \(t\), so the particle does not change direction. The third second is from \(t = 2\) to \(t = 3\).

\[ \begin{aligned} \text{Distance} &= \int_2^3 (5\sin 0.2t + 8)\,dt \\ &= \Big[ -25\cos 0.2t + 8t \Big]_2^3 \\ &= (-25\cos 0.6 + 24) - (-25\cos 0.4 + 16) \\ &= 25\cos 0.4 - 25\cos 0.6 + 8 \\ &= 10.393\ldots \end{aligned} \]
Total distance \(= 10.4\) m (3 s.f.)
Teaching script for parts (b)
(b)(i)

A particle only changes direction when its velocity changes sign. So "never changes direction" really means v never goes negative.

The lowest five sin zero point two t can go is minus five. That makes the lowest velocity c minus five, and we need that to be zero or more.

\[ c - 5 \ge 0 \;\Rightarrow\; c \ge 5 \]

So the smallest c can be is five.

Watch for: trying to solve v equals zero for t. You don't need t at all here; you need the lowest value of the sine part.

(b)(ii)

With c equals eight, the velocity never drops below three, so the particle never turns round. That means distance is the integral of v, no splitting needed.

Careful with the wording: the third second runs from t equals two to t equals three, not from zero to three. Five sin zero point two t integrates to minus twenty-five cos zero point two t, and eight integrates to eight t.

\[ \int_2^3 (5\sin 0.2t + 8)\,dt = \Big[ -25\cos 0.2t + 8t \Big]_2^3 = 25\cos 0.4 - 25\cos 0.6 + 8 \]

Calculator in radians, please. It comes to ten point three nine three, so the distance is ten point four metres to three significant figures.

Watch for: using 0 to 3 as the limits, or working it out with the calculator still in degrees.

G2 Coordinate geometry

Circle: centre and radius

3 questions
G2 Coordinate geometry › Circle: centre and radius
118/2162020 P1 Q9Parts (i)Syllabus 4047 paper
Model solution for parts (i)
(i)

Complete the square in \(x\) and in \(y\).

\[ \begin{aligned} (x^2 + 4x) + (y^2 - 6y) &= 12 \\ (x + 2)^2 - 4 + (y - 3)^2 - 9 &= 12 \\ (x + 2)^2 + (y - 3)^2 &= 25 \end{aligned} \]
Centre \((-2,\ 3)\), radius \(= 5\) units
Teaching script for parts (i)
(i)

To read off the centre and radius, we need the circle in the form x minus a, squared, plus y minus b, squared, equals r squared. So complete the square, once for x and once for y.

\[ (x + 2)^2 - 4 + (y - 3)^2 - 9 = 12 \]

Move the minus four and the minus nine across to the right. Twelve plus four plus nine is twenty-five.

\[ (x + 2)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = 25 \]

The centre has the opposite signs to the brackets, so it's minus two, three. The radius is the square root of twenty-five, which is five units.

Watch for: writing the centre as (2, −3). If the bracket says x plus two, the x-coordinate is minus two. The signs flip.

Centre \((8, 1)\), radius \(= 5\)

Complete the square in x and in y. Half of minus sixteen is minus eight, so add sixty-four. Half of minus two is minus one, so add one. Whatever you add on the left, add on the right too.

Centre eight, one, and radius five.

Circle: equation from conditions

3 questions
G2 Coordinate geometry › Circle: equation from conditions
121/2162019 P2 Q6Whole questionSyllabus 4047 paper
Model solution
(i)
\[ \text{Gradient of tangent through } (0,0) \text{ and } (5,5) = \frac{5-0}{5-0} = 1 \]

The radius at \((5, 5)\) is perpendicular to the tangent, so its gradient is \(-1\):

\[ y - 5 = -(x - 5) \;\Rightarrow\; y = -x + 10 \]

A normal passes through the centre, so the centre lies on both \(y = -x + 10\) and \(3y = x + 2\):

\[ \begin{aligned} 3(-x + 10) &= x + 2 \\ 28 &= 4x \\ x = 7,\quad y &= 3 \end{aligned} \] \[ r^2 = (7-5)^2 + (3-5)^2 = 8 \]
\((x-7)^2 + (y-3)^2 = 8\)
(ii)

The nearest point to the \(x\)-axis lies vertically below the centre \((7, 3)\), a distance \(r = \sqrt{8} = 2\sqrt{2}\) from it. Since \(2\sqrt{2} \lt 3\), the circle does not reach the \(x\)-axis.

\((7,\ 3 - 2\sqrt{2})\)
Teaching script
(i)

You need the centre and the radius. The tangent goes through the origin and touches at five, five, so its gradient is one.

\[ m_{\text{tangent}} = \tfrac{5-0}{5-0} = 1 \]

The radius to the point of contact is perpendicular to the tangent, so its gradient is minus one. Its equation is y equals minus x plus ten.

\[ y = -x + 10 \]

Every normal passes through the centre, so the centre is where this line meets three y equals x plus two. Substitute.

\[ 3(-x + 10) = x + 2 \;\Rightarrow\; x = 7,\ y = 3 \]

Radius squared is the distance squared from seven, three to five, five: four plus four, which is eight.

\[ (x-7)^2 + (y-3)^2 = 8 \]

Watch for: square rooting the eight and then putting it back in unsquared. The right-hand side of the circle equation is r squared.

(ii)

The lowest point of a circle is straight below the centre, one radius down. The centre is at height three and the radius is root eight, two root two, which is less than three. So the circle never reaches the x-axis.

\((7,\ 3 - 2\sqrt2)\)

The nearest point is seven, three minus two root two.

Watch for: answering seven, nought. The circle doesn't touch the axis, so the nearest point is on the circle, not on the axis.

G2 Coordinate geometry › Circle: equation from conditions
122/2162024 P2 Q7Parts (a)(b)
Model solution for parts (a)(b)
(a)

The centre is the midpoint of the diameter \(PQ\).

\[ \text{Centre} = \left(\frac{3+(-5)}{2},\ \frac{1+7}{2}\right) = (-1,\ 4) \] \[ PQ = \sqrt{(3+5)^2 + (1-7)^2} = \sqrt{64+36} = 10, \qquad r = \tfrac{1}{2}PQ = 5 \]
Centre \((-1,\ 4)\), radius \(5\) units
(b)
\[ \begin{aligned} (x+1)^2 + (y-4)^2 &= 25 \\ x^2 + 2x + 1 + y^2 - 8y + 16 - 25 &= 0 \end{aligned} \]
\(x^2 + y^2 + 2x - 8y - 8 = 0\)
Teaching script for parts (a)(b)
(a)

The word that does all the work here is "diameter". If PQ is a diameter, the centre sits right in the middle of P and Q. So add the x-coordinates and halve them, then do the same with the y's.

\[ \text{Centre} = \left(\frac{3+(-5)}{2},\ \frac{1+7}{2}\right) = (-1,\ 4) \]

Now the radius. Find the full length PQ first. Across, the gap is eight; down, it's six. Root of sixty-four plus thirty-six is ten, and the radius is half of that.

\[ PQ = \sqrt{64+36} = 10,\qquad r = 5 \]

So the centre is negative one, four, and the radius is five units.

Watch for: writing ten as the radius. That's the whole diameter. Halve it.

(b)

Write it in centre-radius form first, since that's the easy one. Centre negative one, four means the brackets are x plus one and y minus four, and the right side is the radius squared, twenty-five.

\[ (x+1)^2 + (y-4)^2 = 25 \]

But the question wants the long expanded version. So multiply out both brackets and move the twenty-five over. One plus sixteen minus twenty-five comes to negative eight.

\[ x^2 + y^2 + 2x - 8y - 8 = 0 \]

Watch for: the signs inside the brackets. A centre at x equals negative one gives x plus one, not x minus one. It always feels backwards, and it always is.

123/2162025 P1 Q13Parts (a)
Model solution for parts (a)
(a)

\(2y - 3x = 17 \Rightarrow y = \tfrac{3}{2}x + \tfrac{17}{2}\), so the gradient of \(AB\) is \(\tfrac{3}{2}\).

Diameter parallel to \(AB\) through \(\left(-\tfrac{1}{2},\ \tfrac{1}{4}\right)\):

\[ y - \tfrac{1}{4} = \tfrac{3}{2}\left(x + \tfrac{1}{2}\right) \;\Rightarrow\; y = \tfrac{3}{2}x + 1 \]

The centre lies on this diameter and on \(y = -2\):

\[ -2 = \tfrac{3}{2}x + 1 \;\Rightarrow\; x = -2 \]

Centre \((-2,\ -2)\).

\[ r^2 = (-1+2)^2 + (7+2)^2 = 1 + 81 = 82 \]
\((x+2)^2 + (y+2)^2 = 82\), i.e. \(x^2 + y^2 + 4x + 4y - 74 = 0\)
Teaching script for parts (a)
(a)

Start with the chord AB. Rearrange two y minus three x equals seventeen to get y equals three halves x plus seventeen halves, so the gradient is three halves.

The diameter they mention is parallel to AB, so it has the same gradient, and it goes through negative a half, a quarter.

\[ y - \tfrac{1}{4} = \tfrac{3}{2}\left(x + \tfrac{1}{2}\right) \;\Rightarrow\; y = \tfrac{3}{2}x + 1 \]

The centre is on this diameter and also on the line y equals negative two. Put y equals negative two in: three halves x is negative three, so x is negative two. The centre is negative two, negative two.

For r squared, use the point negative one, seven, which is on the circle. The gaps are one and nine, so r squared is one plus eighty-one, eighty-two.

\[ (x+2)^2 + (y+2)^2 = 82 \]

Expanded, that's x squared plus y squared plus four x plus four y minus seventy-four equals zero.

Watch for: switching to the perpendicular gradient out of habit. Parallel means the same gradient, three halves.

Circle with lines and points

G2 Coordinate geometry › Circle with lines and points
124/2162020 P1 Q9Parts (ii)Syllabus 4047 paper
Model solution for parts (ii)
(ii)

Gradient of the radius joining \((-2, 3)\) to \((1, 7)\) is \(\dfrac{7 - 3}{1 - (-2)} = \dfrac{4}{3}\).

The tangent is perpendicular to the radius, so its gradient is \(-\dfrac{3}{4}\).

\[ \begin{aligned} y - 7 &= -\tfrac{3}{4}(x - 1) \\ 4y - 28 &= -3x + 3 \end{aligned} \]
\(3x + 4y = 31\)  (or \(y = -\tfrac{3}{4}x + \tfrac{31}{4}\))
Teaching script for parts (ii)
(ii)

A tangent always meets the radius at a right angle. So we start with the radius: find its gradient from the centre, minus two, three, to the point one, seven.

\[ m_{\text{radius}} = \frac{7 - 3}{1 - (-2)} = \frac{4}{3} \]

The tangent's gradient is the negative reciprocal, so minus three quarters. Put that and the point one, seven into y minus y one equals m, x minus x one. Then multiply through by four to clear the fraction.

\[ y - 7 = -\tfrac{3}{4}(x - 1) \;\Rightarrow\; 3x + 4y = 31 \]

Watch for: using four thirds as the tangent's gradient. That's the radius. The tangent needs the perpendicular one.

G2 Coordinate geometry › Circle with lines and points
125/2162021 P2 Q9Whole question
Model solution
(a)

With \(k = 4\): \((x + 2a)^2 + (y - a)^2 = 4a^2\). Centre \((-2a, a)\), radius \(2a\) (since \(a \gt 0\)).

The perpendicular distance from the centre to the \(y\)-axis is \(|-2a| = 2a\), which equals the radius. Hence the circle touches the \(y\)-axis at exactly one point, \((0, a)\), so the \(y\)-axis is a tangent.

Distance from centre to \(y\)-axis \(= 2a =\) radius, so the \(y\)-axis is a tangent.
(b)

Tangents parallel to the \(x\)-axis touch the circle vertically above and below the centre, at distance \(2a\):

\[ (-2a,\; a + 2a) \text{ and } (-2a,\; a - 2a) \]
\((-2a, 3a)\) and \((-2a, -a)\)
(c)

With \(k = 5\), substitute \((0, 0)\):

\[ (0 + 2a)^2 + (0 - a)^2 = 4a^2 + a^2 = 5a^2 \]
\((0,0)\) satisfies the equation, so the circle passes through \(O\).
(d)

The centre \((-2a, a)\) is the midpoint of the diameter \(OP\):

\[ P = \big(2(-2a) - 0,\; 2(a) - 0\big) = (-4a, 2a) \] \[ m_{OP} = \frac{2a - 0}{-4a - 0} = -\frac12 \]

The tangent at \(P\) is perpendicular to the radius (and hence to \(OP\)), so its gradient is \(2\).

\[ y - 2a = 2(x + 4a) \]

At the \(x\)-axis, \(y = 0\):

\[ -2a = 2x + 8a \;\Rightarrow\; x = -5a \]
\((-5a, 0)\)
Teaching script
(a)

Put k equals four and read off the centre and radius. The centre is minus two a, a, and the radius is root four a squared, which is two a.

\[ \text{centre } (-2a, a), \quad r = 2a \]

A line is a tangent if its distance from the centre equals the radius. The distance from the centre to the y-axis is the size of the x-coordinate, two a. That's the radius, so the y-axis is a tangent, touching at nought, a.

Watch for: writing the distance as \(-2a\). Distances are never negative: \(|-2a| = 2a\).

(b)

Tangents parallel to the x-axis touch at the very top and bottom of the circle, one radius above and below the centre. So keep x as minus two a and move y by two a either way.

\[ (-2a, 3a) \text{ and } (-2a, -a) \]

Watch for: moving sideways instead of up and down. Horizontal tangents touch above and below the centre.

(c)

To show a point is on the circle, substitute it in. With k equals five, put x and y both as nought: four a squared plus a squared is five a squared, which matches the right-hand side. So the circle passes through the origin.

\[ (2a)^2 + (-a)^2 = 5a^2 \]

Watch for: substituting into the \(k = 4\) equation from part (a). New part, new k.

(d)

OP is a diameter, so the centre is its midpoint. Double the centre and subtract O, and P is minus four a, two a.

\[ P(-4a, 2a) \]

The gradient of OP is two a over minus four a, which is minus a half. The tangent at P is perpendicular to the radius, so its gradient is two.

\[ y - 2a = 2(x + 4a) \]

It meets the x-axis where y is nought: minus two a equals two x plus eight a, so x is minus five a. The point is minus five a, nought.

\[ (-5a, 0) \]

Watch for: using the gradient of \(OP\) for the tangent. The tangent is perpendicular, so flip it and change the sign.

126/2162022 P2 Q9Parts (b)(c)
Model solution for parts (b)(c)
(b)

The perpendicular from the centre to \(y = 2x\) has gradient \(-\tfrac{1}{2}\):

\[ y - 1 = -\frac{1}{2}(x - 8) \quad\Rightarrow\quad y = -\frac{1}{2}x + 5. \]

Intersect with \(y = 2x\): \(2x = -\tfrac{1}{2}x + 5\), so \(x = 2\), \(y = 4\). Foot of perpendicular is \((2, 4)\).

\[ \text{Shortest distance} = \sqrt{(8-2)^2 + (1-4)^2} = \sqrt{45} = 3\sqrt{5} \approx 6.71. \]
Shortest distance \(= 3\sqrt{5} \approx 6.71\) units. Since \(6.71 \gt 5\) (the radius), every point of the line lies outside the circle, so the circle does not intersect \(y = 2x\).
(c)

The centre \((8, 1)\) is the midpoint of \(CD\). Let \(D = (p, q)\):

\[ \frac{11 + p}{2} = 8,\quad \frac{5 + q}{2} = 1 \quad\Rightarrow\quad p = 5,\ q = -3. \]
\(D(5, -3)\)
Teaching script for parts (b)(c)
(b)

Find the shortest distance from the centre to the line y equals two x, then compare it with the radius. The shortest route is along the perpendicular, with gradient minus a half through the centre.

\[ y - 1 = -\tfrac{1}{2}(x - 8) \;\Rightarrow\; y = -\tfrac{1}{2}x + 5 \]

Solve that with y equals two x: two and a half x equals five, so x is two and y is four. The foot of the perpendicular is at two, four.

\[ d = \sqrt{6^2 + 3^2} = \sqrt{45} = 3\sqrt{5} \approx 6.71 \]

Six point seven one is bigger than the radius of five, so even the closest point of the line is outside the circle. They never meet.

Watch for: working out the distance and then not saying what it means. The final sentence has to mention the radius.

(c)

CD is a diameter, so the centre sits halfway between C and D. Call D p, q and use the midpoint formula with C at eleven, five.

\[ \frac{11+p}{2} = 8, \quad \frac{5+q}{2} = 1 \;\Rightarrow\; D(5, -3) \]

Watch for: averaging C and the centre. That gives you a point between them, not D.

(c)

The circle goes through O, so OC is a radius, thirteen. X is on the line OC with CX equal to thirty-nine, which is three radii. The step from O to C is minus five, twelve, and X is three of those steps from C, in either direction.

128/2162024 P1 Q4Whole question
Model solution

From the line, \(x = 1 - 2y\). Substituting into the curve:

\[ \begin{aligned} (1 - 2y)^2 + y^2 - 2(1 - 2y) &= 4 \\ 1 - 4y + 4y^2 + y^2 - 2 + 4y &= 4 \\ 5y^2 - 5 &= 0 \\ y &= 1 \ \text{ or } \ y = -1 \end{aligned} \]

When \(y = 1\), \(x = -1\); when \(y = -1\), \(x = 3\). So \(A(-1, 1)\) and \(B(3, -1)\).

\[ \text{Midpoint of } AB = \left( \frac{-1 + 3}{2}, \frac{1 + (-1)}{2} \right) = (1, 0) \]
Midpoint of \(AB = (1, 0)\)
Teaching script

To find where a line meets a curve, make one letter the subject of the line and substitute it in. Here x equals one minus two y is the tidiest option.

\[ (1 - 2y)^2 + y^2 - 2(1 - 2y) = 4 \]

Expand it: one minus four y plus four y squared, plus y squared, minus two plus four y. The y terms cancel each other out, which is nice of them, and you're left with five y squared minus five equals zero. So y is one or minus one.

\[ 5y^2 - 5 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; y = \pm 1 \]

Use the line to get x. When y is one, x is minus one; when y is minus one, x is three. So A is minus one, one, and B is three, minus one.

\[ \text{Midpoint} = \left( \frac{-1 + 3}{2}, \frac{1 - 1}{2} \right) = (1, 0) \]

Watch for: going back to the curve to find x. You'll pick up extra values that aren't on the line. Use the line.

G2 Coordinate geometry › Circle with lines and points
129/2162024 P2 Q7Parts (c)(d)
Model solution for parts (c)(d)
(c)

Gradient of \(QP = \dfrac{7-1}{-5-3} = -\dfrac{3}{4}\). Gradient of the line \(3y = 4x - 34\) is \(\dfrac{4}{3}\).

\[ -\frac{3}{4} \times \frac{4}{3} = -1 \]
\(QP\) is perpendicular to the line and passes through the centre. The point on the line closest to the circle is the foot of the perpendicular from the centre to the line, so \(R\) lies on \(QP\) extended.
(d)

Equation of \(QP\):

\[ y - 1 = -\tfrac{3}{4}(x-3) \;\Rightarrow\; 3x + 4y = 13 \]

Solve simultaneously with \(4x - 3y = 34\):

\[ \begin{aligned} 9x + 12y &= 39 \\ 16x - 12y &= 136 \\ 25x &= 175 \;\Rightarrow\; x = 7,\quad y = \tfrac{13 - 21}{4} = -2 \end{aligned} \]
\(R(7,\ -2)\)
Teaching script for parts (c)(d)
(c)

They want you to explain why the closest point R is on QP extended. The way in is gradients. QP goes six over negative eight, so negative three quarters. Rearrange three y equals four x minus thirty-four and you get a gradient of four thirds.

\[ -\frac{3}{4} \times \frac{4}{3} = -1 \]

Multiply them and you get negative one, so QP is perpendicular to the line. QP also goes through the centre, because it's a diameter. The shortest path from the centre to a line is always the perpendicular one, so the nearest point has to sit on QP extended.

Watch for: proving the perpendicular bit and stopping there. You also need to say QP passes through the centre. Both facts, or the argument has a hole in it.

(d)

R is where QP crosses the given line, so this is a simultaneous equations job. First, the equation of QP: gradient negative three quarters, through P, three, one.

\[ y - 1 = -\tfrac{3}{4}(x-3) \;\Rightarrow\; 3x + 4y = 13 \]

Write the given line as four x minus three y equals thirty-four. To get rid of y, times the first equation by three and the second by four, then add them.

\[ 9x + 12y = 39,\quad 16x - 12y = 136 \;\Rightarrow\; 25x = 175 \]

So x is seven. Put that back into three x plus four y equals thirteen: four y is thirteen minus twenty-one, negative eight, so y is negative two. R is seven, negative two.

Watch for: giving QP the gradient of the given line, four thirds. QP is the perpendicular one.

130/2162025 P1 Q13Parts (b)
Model solution for parts (b)
(b)

Inside the circle means distance from centre is less than the radius:

\[ \begin{aligned} (7+2)^2 + (\alpha+2)^2 &\lt 82 \\ (\alpha+2)^2 &\lt 1 \\ -1 \lt \alpha + 2 &\lt 1 \end{aligned} \]
\(\{\alpha : -3 \lt \alpha \lt -1\}\)
Teaching script for parts (b)
(b)

A point is inside the circle if it's closer to the centre than the radius. Compare the squared distances, so you don't have to deal with square roots.

\[ (7+2)^2 + (\alpha+2)^2 \lt 82 \;\Rightarrow\; (\alpha+2)^2 \lt 1 \]

Something squared is less than one when that something is between negative one and one. Take away two all the way through, and alpha is between negative three and negative one.

Watch for: writing only alpha plus two less than one. That loses the lower limit, and the answer leaks out the bottom.

Model solution
(i)

\(AB = 4\) and \(A\) is \((0, 2)\) with \(B\) below \(A\) on the \(y\)-axis, so \(B\) is \((0, -2)\).

In a kite with \(AB = BC\) and \(AD = DC\), the diagonal \(BD\) is the perpendicular bisector of \(AC\), so \(P\) is the midpoint of \(AC\). Since \(P\) lies on the \(x\)-axis, its \(y\)-coordinate is 0:

\[ \frac{2 + y_C}{2} = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; y_C = -2 \]

\(B\) and \(C\) both have \(y\)-coordinate \(-2\), so \(BC\) is parallel to the \(x\)-axis.

(ii)

\(BC = AB = 4\) and \(BC\) is horizontal from \(B(0, -2)\).

\(C(4, -2)\)
(iii)
\[ P = \left(\frac{0+4}{2}, \frac{2-2}{2}\right) = (2, 0) \] \[ \text{Gradient of } BD = \frac{0-(-2)}{2-0} = 1 \;\Rightarrow\; BD:\ y = x - 2 \] \[ AC = \sqrt{4^2 + 4^2} = 4\sqrt{2} \] \[ \begin{aligned} \text{Area} = \tfrac{1}{2} \times AC \times BD &= 28 \\ \tfrac{1}{2}(4\sqrt{2})\,BD &= 28 \\ BD &= 7\sqrt{2} \end{aligned} \]

Let \(D = (t, t-2)\), \(t \gt 0\):

\[ \begin{aligned} t^2 + (t - 2 + 2)^2 &= (7\sqrt{2})^2 \\ 2t^2 &= 98 \\ t &= 7 \end{aligned} \]
\(D(7, 5)\)
Teaching script
(i)

A is nought, two and AB is four, with B below A on the y-axis. So B is nought, minus two.

In a kite with AB equal to BC and AD equal to DC, the diagonal BD is the perpendicular bisector of AC. So P is the midpoint of AC. P is on the x-axis, so its y-coordinate is zero.

\[ \frac{2 + y_C}{2} = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; y_C = -2 \]

B and C both have y-coordinate minus two, so BC is horizontal, parallel to the x-axis.

Watch for: saying BC is horizontal because it looks horizontal. You need the midpoint argument to show C has the same y-coordinate as B.

(ii)

BC equals AB, which is four, and BC runs horizontally from B at nought, minus two. Move four units to the right.

\(C(4, -2)\)

Watch for: moving left. C is on the right of the y-axis in the diagram.

(iii)

First find P as the midpoint of A and C, then get the equation of BD.

\[ P = (2, 0), \quad m_{BD} = \frac{0-(-2)}{2-0} = 1, \quad BD:\ y = x - 2 \]

For a kite, area is half the product of the diagonals. AC is root of sixteen plus sixteen, which is four root two. Set the area to twenty-eight.

\[ \tfrac12(4\sqrt2)\,BD = 28 \;\Rightarrow\; BD = 7\sqrt2 \]

D is on y equals x minus two, so call it t, t minus two. Its distance from B at nought, minus two is seven root two.

\[ t^2 + t^2 = 98 \;\Rightarrow\; t = 7 \]

Take the positive value because D is on the right. D is seven, five.

Watch for: measuring BD from P instead of from B. The diagonal runs the whole way from B to D.

Gradient of \(BE = \dfrac{6 - 5}{5 - 7} = -\dfrac12\). Equation of \(BD\): \(y - 5 = -\tfrac12(x - 7)\), i.e. \(y = \dfrac{17 - x}{2}\).

So \(D = \left(x, \dfrac{17 - x}{2}\right)\).

\(AB = \sqrt{12^2 + 5^2} = 13\). Perimeter \(= 2(AB + AD) = 46\), so \(AD = 23 - 13 = 10\).

\[ \begin{aligned} (x + 5)^2 + \left(\frac{17 - x}{2}\right)^2 &= 100 \\ 4(x^2 + 10x + 25) + (289 - 34x + x^2) &= 400 \\ 5x^2 + 6x - 11 &= 0 \end{aligned} \]
\(5x^2 + 6x = 11\)  (shown)
(ii)
\[ (5x + 11)(x - 1) = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x = 1 \text{ or } x = -\tfrac{11}{5} \]

Both values give \(AD = 10\). The diagram shows \(D\) to the right of the \(y\)-axis, so \(x \gt 0\) and \(x = 1\). Then \(y = \dfrac{17 - 1}{2} = 8\).

\(D(1,\ 8)\). The diagram is needed to reject \(x = -\tfrac{11}{5}\).

\(C(13,\ 13)\)
Teaching script
(i)

D sits on the line through B and E, so find that line first. The gradient of BE is six minus five over five minus seven, so minus a half. Through B, the equation is y equals seventeen minus x, over two.

\[ D = \left(x, \frac{17 - x}{2}\right) \]

Now use the perimeter. AB is the square root of twelve squared plus five squared, which is thirteen. Half the perimeter is twenty-three, so AD must be ten.

Write AD squared equals one hundred, using A at minus five, nought. Multiply through by four to clear the fraction, expand, and collect terms.

\[ (x + 5)^2 + \left(\frac{17 - x}{2}\right)^2 = 100 \;\Rightarrow\; 5x^2 + 6x - 11 = 0 \]

Watch for: setting AD equal to 46 − 13. The perimeter has two of each side, so AB + AD is 23.

(ii)

Factorise: five x plus eleven, times x minus one. So x is one or minus eleven fifths. Both give AD equal to ten, so the algebra alone can't choose.

\[ (5x + 11)(x - 1) = 0 \]

The diagram can. It shows D to the right of the y-axis, so x is positive, which means x is one. Then y is sixteen over two, which is eight. D is one, eight, and we needed the diagram to throw out the negative value.

Watch for: giving both points. The question wants you to explain your choice using the diagram.

(iii)

The diagonals of a parallelogram bisect each other, so A plus C equals B plus D, one coordinate at a time. C is seven plus one minus minus five, and five plus eight minus zero.

\[ C(13,\ 13) \]

Watch for: slipping on the double negative. −(−5) is +5.

133/2162021 P1 Q9Whole question
Model solution
(a)

Since \(BC = CD\), \(BC^2 = CD^2\):

\[ \begin{aligned} (6-0)^2 + (6-k)^2 &= (6-h)^2 + (6-0)^2 \\ (6-k)^2 &= (6-h)^2 \\ 6 - k &= \pm(6-h) \end{aligned} \]

Taking \(6 - k = 6 - h\) gives \(k = h\), which is rejected since \(k \ne h\). Hence

\[ 6 - k = -(6 - h) \;\Rightarrow\; h + k = 12 \]
\(h + k = 12\) (shown)
(b)

\(h = 4 \Rightarrow k = 8\), so \(B(0, 8)\) and \(D(4, 0)\). Let \(A\) be \((a, 0)\). Since \(AB = AD\):

\[ \begin{aligned} a^2 + 8^2 &= (4 - a)^2 \\ a^2 + 64 &= 16 - 8a + a^2 \\ 8a &= -48 \;\Rightarrow\; a = -6 \end{aligned} \]
\(A(-6, 0)\)
(c)

Using the vertices \(A(-6,0)\), \(B(0,8)\), \(C(6,6)\), \(D(4,0)\) in order:

\[ \text{Area} = \tfrac12 \left| \begin{matrix} -6 & 0 & 6 & 4 & -6 \\ 0 & 8 & 6 & 0 & 0 \end{matrix} \right| = \tfrac12\left|(-48 + 0 + 0 + 0) - (0 + 48 + 24 + 0)\right| = \tfrac12(120) = 60 \]

Check: \(\tfrac12 \times AC \times BD = \tfrac12\sqrt{180}\sqrt{80} = \tfrac12(120) = 60\).

Area of kite \(= 60\) units²
Teaching script
(a)

You're told BC equals CD. Lengths come with square roots, and nobody wants to carry those around, so square both sides and compare BC squared with CD squared using the distance formula.

\[ (6-0)^2 + (6-k)^2 = (6-h)^2 + (6-0)^2 \]

The thirty-six on each side cancels out. What's left is six minus k, all squared, equals six minus h, all squared. When you square-root that, you get six minus k equals plus or minus six minus h.

\[ 6 - k = \pm(6 - h) \]

The plus sign gives k equals h, but the question says they're different, so throw that one out. The minus sign gives six minus k equals h minus six, and that rearranges to h plus k equals twelve.

\[ h + k = 12 \]

Watch for: forgetting the \(\pm\). Without the negative case you only get \(k = h\), and you'll be stuck.

(b)

Now h is four, so k is eight. That makes B the point nought, eight, and D the point four, nought. A sits on the x-axis, so call it a, nought.

In a kite, the two short sides coming out of A are equal. So AB equals AD. Square both and expand.

\[ a^2 + 64 = (4 - a)^2 = 16 - 8a + a^2 \]

The a squared terms cancel nicely, leaving eight a equals minus forty-eight. So a is minus six.

\[ a = -6 \]

A is the point minus six, nought.

Watch for: expanding \((4 - a)^2\) as \(16 - a^2\). The middle term is still there, whether you like it or not.

(c)

For the area, use the shoelace method. Go round the kite in order: A, B, C, D, and back to A.

\[ \tfrac12 \left| \begin{matrix} -6 & 0 & 6 & 4 & -6 \\ 0 & 8 & 6 & 0 & 0 \end{matrix} \right| \]

The downward products add up to minus forty-eight. The upward products are forty-eight plus twenty-four, so seventy-two. The difference is minus one hundred and twenty. Take the size, halve it, and you get sixty.

\[ \tfrac12\left|-48 - 72\right| = 60 \]

Quick check if you like: half the product of the diagonals AC and BD. That's a half of root one-eighty times root eighty, which is sixty again. So the area is sixty square units.

Watch for: listing the vertices out of order. Go round the shape once, in one direction, or the area comes out wrong.

(a)

A and B both have y-coordinate two, so AB is flat. Its length is ten minus four, which is six. That makes AB a handy base, and the height is the vertical distance from C to that line: q minus two, in size.

\[ \tfrac12(6)|q - 2| = 15 \]

So the size of q minus two is five, which gives q equals seven or minus three. The question says q is positive, so q is seven.

135/2162023 P1 Q13Whole question
Model solution
(a)
\[ \text{Gradient of } AB = \frac{h - (-7)}{2 - (-8)} = \frac{h + 7}{10}. \]
Gradient of perpendicular bisector \(= -\dfrac{10}{h + 7}\)
(b)

Midpoint of \(AB\): \(M = \left(\dfrac{2 - 8}{2}, \dfrac{h - 7}{2}\right) = \left(-3, \dfrac{h-7}{2}\right)\).

The bisector passes through \(M\) and \(X\left(h, -\tfrac{13}{2}\right)\):

\[ \text{Gradient of } MX = \frac{-\frac{13}{2} - \frac{h-7}{2}}{h + 3} = \frac{-(h + 6)}{2(h + 3)}. \]

Equating with part (a):

\[ \begin{aligned} \frac{h + 6}{2(h + 3)} &= \frac{10}{h + 7} \\ (h + 6)(h + 7) &= 20(h + 3) \\ h^2 + 13h + 42 &= 20h + 60 \\ h^2 - 7h - 18 &= 0 \\ (h - 9)(h + 2) &= 0. \end{aligned} \]
\(h = 9\) or \(h = -2\)
Teaching script
(a)

A is minus eight, minus seven and B is two, h. Gradient is change in y over change in x.

\[ m_{AB} = \frac{h + 7}{2 + 8} = \frac{h + 7}{10} \]

The perpendicular bisector is at right angles to AB, so its gradient is the negative reciprocal: minus ten over h plus seven.

Watch for: putting a minus in front but forgetting to flip the fraction. You need both.

(b)

The bisector goes through the midpoint of AB and through X. So work out its gradient a second way, from those two points, and set it equal to your part (a) answer.

\[ M = \left(-3, \tfrac{h-7}{2}\right), \quad X\left(h, -\tfrac{13}{2}\right) \]
\[ m_{MX} = \frac{-\frac{13}{2} - \frac{h-7}{2}}{h + 3} = \frac{-(h+6)}{2(h+3)} \]

Both gradients are negative, so drop the minus signs and cross-multiply.

\[ (h+6)(h+7) = 20(h+3) \;\Rightarrow\; h^2 - 7h - 18 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; (h-9)(h+2) = 0 \]

So h is nine or minus two. Both work, so give both.

Watch for: sign slips in the numerator. Minus thirteen over two, minus h minus seven over two, is minus h minus six over two.

(v)
\[ \begin{aligned} 0 &= 12.1e^{-0.126t} - 3 \\ e^{-0.126t} &= \frac{3}{12.1} \\ t &= \frac{\ln(12.1/3)}{0.126} \approx 11.1 \end{aligned} \]

Teaching script
(i)

There's an exponential in the model, so take logs to get a straight line. Add three to both sides first so the exponential is on its own, then take natural logs.

\[ v + 3 = Ae^{-kt} \;\Rightarrow\; \ln(v+3) = -kt + \ln A \]

Now it's Y equals m X plus c, with Y as ln of v plus three, X as t, gradient minus k and intercept ln A. Work out ln of v plus three for each value in the table.

\(\ln(v+3)\): 2.24, 2.00, 1.74, 1.48 at \(t = 2, 4, 6, 8\)

Plot to the scales they give you: one centimetre per unit of t, five centimetres per unit of ln of v plus three. Rule one straight line of best fit and extend it back to the vertical axis, because you'll need the intercept.

Watch for: taking logs before adding three. ln of v plus three is not ln v plus ln three.

(ii)

Read the gradient from two points on your line that are far apart. Using the end points gives about minus nought point one two six.

\[ \text{gradient} = \frac{1.48 - 2.24}{8 - 2} \approx -0.126 \;\Rightarrow\; k \approx 0.126 \]

The gradient is minus k, so k is about nought point one two six. If the paper prints the power as plus k t instead of minus k t, then k is minus nought point one two six.

The intercept is about two point four nine. That's ln A, so A is e to the two point four nine.

\[ \ln A \approx 2.49 \;\Rightarrow\; A \approx 12.1 \]

A is about twelve and k about nought point one three. Values close to these from a decent line are accepted.

Watch for: giving the intercept as A. The intercept is ln A, so you still have to exponentiate it.

(iii)

At O, t is zero, so e to the nought is one and v is A minus three.

\[ v = A - 3 \approx 12.1 - 3 = 9.1 \]

The speed at O is about nine point one metres per second.

Watch for: giving A as the speed. The minus three is part of the model.

(iv)

This one asks you to explain, not calculate. When v is five, ln of v plus three is ln eight, about two point nought eight.

Go to two point nought eight on the vertical axis, across to your line, then down to the t-axis and read off t. It comes to about three point three.

Watch for: looking for five on the vertical axis. That axis is ln of v plus three, so convert first.

(v)

At rest, v is zero. Put that into the model with your A and k.

\[ 0 = 12.1e^{-0.126t} - 3 \;\Rightarrow\; e^{-0.126t} = \frac{3}{12.1} \]

Take natural logs of both sides and solve for t.

\[ t = \frac{\ln(12.1/3)}{0.126} \approx 11.1 \]

The vehicle stops at about eleven point one seconds.

Watch for: a sign slip when you take logs. ln of three over twelve point one is negative, and dividing by minus nought point one two six makes t positive. Time doesn't run backwards here.

Teaching script
(a)

For a straight line, you want the form Y equals m X plus c. Divide the whole equation by v and you get d over v equals a v plus b.

\[ \frac{d}{v} = av + b \]

So plot d over v on the vertical axis against v on the horizontal axis. The gradient is a, and the vertical intercept is b.

That's about seventy-three tigers. Anything from roughly seventy to seventy-eight is fine, depending on how you drew your line.

Teaching script
(a)(i)

R equals A v to the k is a power law. Take lg of both sides and it turns into a straight line: lg R equals k lg v plus lg A.

\[ \lg R = k\lg v + \lg A \]

That's Y equals m X plus c, with lg R as Y and lg v as X. So you plot lg R against lg v. The gradient is k, and where the line cuts the lg R axis is lg A.

Make a table of lg v and lg R to three decimal places, plot the five points on a sensible scale, and draw the line of best fit through the four that line up nicely.

\(\lg v\): 0.699, 1.000, 1.176, 1.301, 1.398   |   \(\lg R\): 1.322, 1.740, 2.041, 2.164, 2.301

(a)(iii)

(a)
\[ \begin{aligned} y &= Ax^b \\ \ln y &= \ln A + b\ln x \end{aligned} \]

This is of the form \(Y = mX + c\) with \(Y = \ln y\).

\(X = \ln x\)
(b)
\[ b = \text{gradient} = \frac{12.1 - 6.3}{4.1 - 1.2} = \frac{5.8}{2.9} = 2 \] \[ \ln A = 6.3 - 2(1.2) = 3.9 \;\Rightarrow\; A = e^{3.9} = 49.40 \]
\(A = 49.4\), \(b = 2\)
Teaching script
(a)

y equals A x to the b is a power law. To turn it into a straight line, take natural logs of both sides. The log of a product splits into a sum, and the power b drops down to the front.

\[ \ln y = \ln A + b\ln x \]

Match it up with capital Y equals m capital X plus c. Capital Y is ln y, so capital X has to be ln x.

Watch for: choosing X as plain x. Whatever b multiplies is what goes on the horizontal axis, and that's ln x.

(b)

The gradient of the line is b. Use the two points they give: twelve point one minus six point three, over four point one minus one point two.

\[ A = e^{3.9} = 49.4 \]

G3 Proofs in plane geometry

Angle and length proofs in circles
