G3 Proofs in plane geometry › Angle and length proofs in circles
142/2162019 P1 Q6Whole questionSyllabus 4047 paper
Model solution
(i)

Let \(\angle PQR = x\).

\(\angle PRQ = \angle PQR = x\) (base angles of isosceles triangle \(PQR\), \(PQ = PR\)).

\(\angle PTQ = 90^\circ\) (angle in a semicircle, \(PQ\) is a diameter).

\(\angle PTR = 180^\circ - 90^\circ = 90^\circ\) (angles on a straight line \(QTR\)).

\(\angle STP = \angle TQP = x\) (alternate segment theorem: angle between tangent \(TS\) and chord \(TP\)).

\(\angle STR = \angle PTR - \angle STP = 90^\circ - x\).

In triangle \(TSR\):

\[ \angle TSR = 180^\circ - \angle STR - \angle SRT = 180^\circ - (90^\circ - x) - x = 90^\circ \]

(angle sum of triangle). Hence \(\angle TSR = 90^\circ\).

(ii)

Since \(\angle TSR = 90^\circ\), the chord \(TR\) subtends a right angle at \(S\) on the circle through \(S\), \(R\) and \(T\). By the converse of the angle in a semicircle, \(TR\) is a diameter of this circle, so its centre is the midpoint of \(TR\).

Teaching script
(i)

You want to show angle TSR is ninety degrees. The plan: get the other two angles of triangle TSR in terms of one letter, then use the angle sum. Call angle PQR x.

PQ equals PR, so the triangle is isosceles and the base angles match. Angle PRQ is x too.

\(\angle PRQ = \angle PQR = x\) (base angles, isosceles triangle)

PQ is a diameter, so the angle at T is ninety degrees, angle in a semicircle. QTR is a straight line, so angle PTR is ninety as well.

\(\angle PTQ = 90^\circ\) (angle in semicircle); \(\angle PTR = 90^\circ\) (angles on a straight line)

TS is a tangent and TP is a chord, so the alternate segment theorem gives angle STP equal to angle TQP, which is x.

\(\angle STP = x\) (alternate segment theorem); \(\angle STR = 90^\circ - x\)

So triangle TSR has ninety minus x at T and x at R. The third angle is one eighty minus those two, which comes out as ninety degrees.

\[ \angle TSR = 180^\circ - (90^\circ - x) - x = 90^\circ \]

Watch for: statements with no reason, or writing "alternate angles" when you mean the alternate segment theorem. Every line needs its own named reason.

(ii)

Now use part (i). Angle TSR is ninety degrees and it sits on the circle through S, R and T. A right angle on a circle stands on a diameter. That's the converse of the angle in a semicircle.

So TR is a diameter of that circle, and the centre is the midpoint of any diameter. The centre is the midpoint of TR.

Watch for: leaving out the word "converse". You're going from the right angle to the diameter, which is the usual theorem backwards.

G3 Proofs in plane geometry › Angle and length proofs in circles
143/2162020 P2 Q4Whole questionSyllabus 4047 paper
Model solution

\(\angle TAC = \angle ABC\) (angle between tangent and chord equals angle in alternate segment)

\(\angle ABC = \angle DBC\) (same angle, since \(D\) lies on \(AB\))

\(\angle AED = \angle DBC\) (exterior angle of cyclic quadrilateral \(BCED\) equals interior opposite angle)

Hence \(\angle AED = \angle TAC = \angle TAE\), since \(E\) lies on \(AC\).

\(\angle AED\) and \(\angle TAE\) are alternate angles formed by the transversal \(AE\) with \(DE\) and \(ST\).

Alternate angles are equal, so \(DE\) is parallel to \(ST\). (proved)
Teaching script

To prove two lines are parallel, show that a pair of alternate angles are equal. The transversal here is AE, so what we want is angle AED equals angle TAE. The bridge between them is angle ABC, which connects the tangent to the cyclic quadrilateral.

Start at the tangent. The angle between the tangent AT and the chord AC equals the angle in the alternate segment, which is angle ABC.

\(\angle TAC = \angle ABC\) (alternate segment theorem)

D lies on AB, so angle ABC is the same angle as DBC. Now bring in the cyclic quadrilateral BCED. Its exterior angle at E, angle AED, equals the interior opposite angle at B, angle DBC.

\(\angle AED = \angle DBC\) (exterior angle of cyclic quadrilateral)

Now chain them together. Angle AED equals angle DBC, which equals angle TAC. And since E lies on AC, angle TAC is the same as angle TAE.

\(\angle AED = \angle TAE\) (alternate angles), so \(DE \parallel ST\)

Watch for: missing reasons. Every line needs its reason written out in full, and the last line has to say alternate angles.

G3 Proofs in plane geometry › Angle and length proofs in circles
144/2162021 P1 Q11Whole question
Model solution
(a)

\(\angle TCA = \angle CBA\) (angle between tangent and chord equals angle in alternate segment)

\(\angle TCA = \angle CAB\) (alternate angles, \(BA \parallel CT\))

Hence \(\angle CBA = \angle CAB\).

Two angles of triangle \(ABC\) are equal, so triangle \(ABC\) is isosceles with \(CA = CB\). (proved)
(b)

\(TA = TC\) (tangents from an external point), so \(\angle TAC = \angle TCA\) (base angles of isosceles triangle \(TAC\)).

In triangle \(TAC\): \(\angle CTA = 180^\circ - 2\angle TCA\) (angle sum of triangle).

In triangle \(ABC\): \(\angle BCA = 180^\circ - 2\angle CAB\) (angle sum of triangle, using (a)).

From (a), \(\angle TCA = \angle CAB\).

Hence \(\angle BCA = \angle CTA\). (proved)
Teaching script
(a)

To show a triangle is isosceles, show two of its angles are equal. We've got a tangent at C and a pair of parallel lines, and both of them lead to angle TCA.

By the alternate segment theorem, the angle between the tangent CT and the chord CA equals angle CBA. And since BA is parallel to CT, angle TCA also equals angle CAB, as alternate angles.

\(\angle TCA = \angle CBA\) (alt. segment), \(\angle TCA = \angle CAB\) (alt. angles, \(BA \parallel CT\))

So angle CBA equals angle CAB. Two equal angles means the sides opposite them are equal too, CA equals CB, so the triangle is isosceles.

Watch for: writing "alternate angles" without saying which lines are parallel. Name BA ∥ CT.

(b)

TA and TC are tangents drawn from the same outside point, so they're the same length. That makes triangle TAC isosceles, with base angles TAC and TCA equal.

\(TA = TC\) (tangents from external point)

Angles in triangle TAC add to one eighty, so angle CTA is one eighty minus two times angle TCA. Do the same in triangle ABC, using the equal base angles from (a): angle BCA is one eighty minus two times angle CAB.

\(\angle CTA = 180^\circ - 2\angle TCA, \quad \angle BCA = 180^\circ - 2\angle CAB\)

Part (a) already showed angle TCA equals angle CAB, so those two expressions are the same. Angle BCA equals angle CTA.

Watch for: skipping the reason for TA = TC. It's the whole reason triangle TAC is isosceles, so it has to be there.

G3 Proofs in plane geometry › Angle and length proofs in circles
145/2162022 P1 Q7Whole question
Model solution
(i)

\(SP = SR\) (tangents from an external point are equal), so triangle \(PSR\) is isosceles.

\(\angle SRP = \angle SPR = x\) (base angles of isosceles triangle).

\(\angle RST = \angle SPR + \angle SRP\) (exterior angle of triangle \(PSR\) equals the sum of the interior opposite angles).

\[ \angle RST = x + x = 2x \]
\(\angle RST = 2x\) (proved)
(ii)

\(\angle QRP = 90^\circ\) (angle in a semicircle). Since \(QRT\) is a straight line, \(\angle PRT = 180^\circ - 90^\circ = 90^\circ\) (adjacent angles on a straight line).

\(\angle SRT = \angle PRT - \angle PRS = 90^\circ - x\).

In triangle \(PRT\): \(\angle RTP = 180^\circ - 90^\circ - x = 90^\circ - x\) (angle sum of triangle).

So \(\angle STR = \angle SRT = 90^\circ - x\), hence \(SR = ST\) (sides opposite equal angles).

Triangle \(RST\) is isosceles with \(SR = ST\) (proved).
Teaching script
(i)

Start with the two tangents drawn from S. Tangents from the same outside point are equal, so SP equals SR, and triangle PSR is isosceles.

\(SP = SR\) (tangents from an external point)

That means the base angles are equal: angle SRP equals angle SPR, which is x.

\(\angle SRP = \angle SPR = x\) (base angles of isosceles triangle)

Angle RST is an exterior angle of triangle PSR, so it equals the two interior opposite angles added together: x plus x, which is two x.

\(\angle RST = x + x = 2x\) (exterior angle of triangle)

Watch for: writing a statement with no reason. Every line in a geometry proof needs its property in brackets.

(ii)

Now you need two equal angles in triangle RST. PQ is a diameter, so the angle in the semicircle at R, angle QRP, is ninety degrees. QRT is a straight line, so angle PRT is ninety as well.

\(\angle QRP = 90^\circ\) (angle in a semicircle), so \(\angle PRT = 90^\circ\) (angles on a straight line)

Angle SRT is angle PRT minus angle PRS, so ninety minus x.

\(\angle SRT = 90^\circ - x\)

In triangle PRT the angles add up to a hundred and eighty, so angle RTP is a hundred and eighty minus ninety minus x. That's also ninety minus x.

\(\angle RTP = 90^\circ - x\) (angle sum of triangle)

Angles STR and SRT are equal, so the sides opposite them are equal: SR equals ST. Triangle RST is isosceles.

Watch for: concluding from the equal angles without naming the equal sides \(SR = ST\).

G3 Proofs in plane geometry › Angle and length proofs in circles
146/2162024 P1 Q6Whole question
Model solution

Let \(\angle AXB = x\).

\(\angle ABC = 90^\circ\) (angle in a semicircle, \(AC\) is a diameter).

\(\angle ABX = 180^\circ - 90^\circ = 90^\circ\) (adjacent angles on a straight line \(XBC\)).

In triangle \(ABX\): \(\angle XAB = 180^\circ - 90^\circ - x = 90^\circ - x\) (angle sum of triangle). Since \(T\) lies on \(XA\), \(\angle TAB = 90^\circ - x\).

\(TA = TB\) (tangents from an external point are equal), so triangle \(ATB\) is isosceles and \(\angle TBA = \angle TAB = 90^\circ - x\) (base angles of isosceles triangle).

\[ \begin{aligned} \angle ATB &= 180^\circ - 2(90^\circ - x) \quad \text{(angle sum of triangle)} \\ &= 2x \end{aligned} \]
\(\angle ATB = 2 \times \angle AXB\) (shown)
Teaching script

Call angle AXB x, then work towards angle ATB in terms of x. Every step needs a reason in brackets; in a proof, a fact without a reason is only a rumour.

AC is a diameter, so angle ABC is ninety degrees, the angle in a semicircle. X, B and C are on a straight line, so angle ABX is ninety degrees too, because adjacent angles on a straight line add up.

\(\angle ABC = 90^\circ\) (angle in a semicircle); \(\angle ABX = 90^\circ\) (adj. angles on a str. line)

The angles in triangle ABX add to one hundred and eighty, so angle XAB is ninety minus x. T is on XA, so angle TAB is also ninety minus x.

TA and TB are tangents from the same outside point, so they're equal. That makes triangle ATB isosceles, and angle TBA equals angle TAB, ninety minus x.

\[ \angle ATB = 180^\circ - 2(90^\circ - x) = 2x \]

So angle ATB is double angle AXB, which is what you had to prove.

Watch for: writing TA equals TB with no reason. Say "tangents from an external point are equal".

G3 Proofs in plane geometry › Angle and length proofs in circles
147/2162025 P2 Q10Parts (b)(i)
Model solution for parts (b)(i)
(b)(i)

\(\angle ADB = \angle FBA = y\) (alternate segment theorem: angle between tangent \(BF\) and chord \(BA\)).

\(\angle BDC = \angle BAC = x\) (angles in the same segment, subtended by chord \(BC\)).

\[ \angle ADC = \angle ADB + \angle BDC = y + x \]
\(\angle ADC = x + y\) (proved)
Teaching script for parts (b)(i)
(b)(i)

Cut angle ADC into two pieces using the chord DB: angle ADB and angle BDC.

Angle ADB comes from the tangent BF and the chord BA. By the alternate segment theorem it equals angle FBA, which is y. Angle BDC and angle BAC both stand on the chord BC, so they're angles in the same segment and both equal x.

\(\angle ADB = y\) (alt. segment), \(\angle BDC = x\) (same segment)

Add the two pieces, and angle ADC is x plus y.

Watch for: giving a reason without saying which chord the angles stand on.

Similar triangles and classifying a figure

2 questions
G3 Proofs in plane geometry › Similar triangles and classifying a figure
148/2162023 P2 Q2Whole question
Model solution
(a)

In triangles \(ABD\) and \(AFB\):

\(\angle BAD = \angle FAB\) (common angle).

\(\angle ABF = \angle BDF = \angle ADB\) (alternate segment theorem: the angle between tangent \(AB\) and chord \(BF\) equals the angle subtended by \(BF\) in the alternate segment).

Two pairs of corresponding angles are equal, so the triangles are similar (AA).

Triangle \(ABD\) is similar to triangle \(AFB\) (proved).
(b)

\(\angle BXC = 90^\circ\) (angle in a semicircle, \(BC\) is a diameter of the circle through \(B\), \(C\), \(X\)). So \(EC \perp BD\).

\(\angle FBD = 90^\circ\) (angle in a semicircle, \(FD\) is a diameter). So \(BF \perp BD\).

Since \(BF\) and \(EC\) are both perpendicular to \(BD\), \(BF \parallel EC\).

\(EB\) lies on line \(AE\) and \(FC\) lies on line \(AD\); these lines meet at \(A\), so \(EB\) is not parallel to \(FC\).

\(EBFC\) is a trapezium: exactly one pair of opposite sides (\(BF\) and \(EC\)) is parallel.
Teaching script
(a)

For similar triangles you need two pairs of equal angles. Name the triangles in the order the question gives, ABD and AFB, so A matches A, B matches F, and D matches B.

\[ \angle BAD = \angle FAB \quad \text{(common angle)} \]

AB is a tangent and BF is a chord. The alternate segment theorem says the angle between them equals the angle BF makes in the other segment, which is angle BDF, the same as angle ADB.

\[ \angle ABF = \angle ADB \quad \text{(alternate segment theorem)} \]

Two pairs of equal angles, so the triangles are similar.

Watch for: writing "tangent-chord" and leaving it there. The reason has to say which chord and which segment.

(b)

To classify a quadrilateral, check which sides are parallel. The two circles hand you two right angles.

B, C and X lie on a circle with BC as diameter, so angle BXC is ninety degrees, and EC is perpendicular to BD. FD is a diameter of the big circle, so angle FBD is ninety, and BF is perpendicular to BD.

\[ EC \perp BD,\; BF \perp BD \;\Rightarrow\; BF \parallel EC \]

The other pair, EB and FC, lie on lines AE and AD, which meet at A, so they aren't parallel. One pair of parallel sides makes EBFC a trapezium.

Watch for: naming the shape without showing the other pair isn't parallel. Otherwise it could be a parallelogram as far as the examiner knows.

G3 Proofs in plane geometry › Similar triangles and classifying a figure
149/2162025 P2 Q10Parts (a)(b)(ii)
Model solution for parts (a)(b)(ii)
(a)

In triangles \(ABE\) and \(BCE\):

\(\angle AEB = \angle BEC\) (common angle).

\(\angle CBE = \angle BAC = x\) (alternate segment theorem: angle between tangent \(BE\) and chord \(BC\) equals the angle in the alternate segment), so \(\angle BAE = \angle CBE\).

Two pairs of corresponding angles are equal, so triangle \(ABE\) is similar to triangle \(BCE\) (AA).
(b)(ii)

\(\angle ACD = \angle BAC = x\) (alternate angles, \(DC \parallel AB\)).

\(\angle ABD = \angle ACD = x\) (angles in the same segment, subtended by chord \(AD\)).

\(\angle ACB = \angle FBA = y\) (alternate segment theorem), and \(\angle ADB = y\) from (b)(i).

In triangles \(ABD\) and \(BAC\):

\(\angle ABD = \angle BAC = x\); \(\angle ADB = \angle BCA = y\); \(AB\) is common.

Triangles \(ABD\) and \(BAC\) are congruent (AAS).

Hence \(AD = BC\) (corresponding sides of congruent triangles).
Teaching script for parts (a)(b)(ii)
(a)

To prove two triangles similar, you need two pairs of equal angles. Look at triangles ABE and BCE. They share the angle at E, so that's pair number one.

\(\angle AEB = \angle BEC\) (common angle)

For the second pair: BE is a tangent and BC is a chord. The alternate segment theorem says the angle between them equals the angle in the alternate segment, which is angle BAC, the one marked x.

\(\angle CBE = \angle BAC = x\) (alternate segment theorem)

Two pairs of angles match, so triangle ABE is similar to triangle BCE.

Watch for: writing "tangent-chord" as your reason and moving on. Write out the alternate segment theorem by name.

(b)(ii)

To show AD equals BC, prove triangles ABD and BAC congruent, then match up the sides. Gather your angles first.

DC is parallel to AB, so angle ACD equals angle BAC, x, as alternate angles. Angle ABD also stands on chord AD, the same chord as angle ACD, so it's x as well. On the other side, angle ACB is y by the alternate segment theorem, and angle ADB is y from part (i).

\(\angle ABD = \angle BAC = x,\ \angle ADB = \angle BCA = y,\ AB\) common

Two angles and a side match, so the triangles are congruent by A A S. AD and BC are corresponding sides, so AD equals BC.

Watch for: calling it A S A. The side AB isn't sandwiched between the two equal angles, so it's A A S.

3. Calculus

C1 Differentiation and integration

“Show that” a derivative

3 questions
C1 Differentiation and integration › “Show that” a derivative
150/2162020 P2 Q7Parts (i)Syllabus 4047 paper
Model solution for parts (i)
(i)

By the product rule and the chain rule:

\[ \begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dx}\left\{x(3x - 5)^{\frac53}\right\} &= (3x - 5)^{\frac53} + x \cdot \tfrac53(3x - 5)^{\frac23}(3) \\ &= (3x - 5)^{\frac23}\left[(3x - 5) + 5x\right] \\ &= (8x - 5)(3x - 5)^{\frac23} \end{aligned} \]
\(\dfrac{d}{dx}\left\{x(3x - 5)^{\frac53}\right\} = (8x - 5)(3x - 5)^{\frac23}\)  (shown)
Teaching script for parts (i)
(i)

x times three x minus five to the five thirds is a product, so product rule. The second factor also needs the chain rule: bring down the five thirds, drop the power to two thirds, and multiply by three, which is the derivative of the inside.

\[ (3x - 5)^{\frac53} + x \cdot \tfrac53(3x - 5)^{\frac23}(3) \]

Now take out the lower power, three x minus five to the two thirds. What's left in the bracket is three x minus five plus five x, which tidies up to eight x minus five.

\[ (8x - 5)(3x - 5)^{\frac23} \]

Watch for: forgetting the 3 from the chain rule. Without it, the bracket refuses to become 8x − 5.

C1 Differentiation and integration › “Show that” a derivative
151/2162021 P2 Q3Parts (a)
Model solution for parts (a)
(a)

Write \(y = x(2x+1)^{-\frac12}\) and use the product rule.

\[ \begin{aligned} \frac{dy}{dx} &= (2x+1)^{-\frac12} + x\left(-\tfrac12\right)(2x+1)^{-\frac32}(2) \\ &= (2x+1)^{-\frac32}\left[(2x+1) - x\right] \\ &= \frac{x+1}{(2x+1)^{\frac32}} \end{aligned} \]
\(\dfrac{dy}{dx} = \dfrac{x+1}{(2x+1)^{\frac32}}\) (shown)
Teaching script for parts (a)
(a)

Turn the fraction into a product first: x times two x plus one to the power minus a half. Then it's a straightforward product rule.

\[ y = x(2x+1)^{-\frac12} \]

First times derivative of the second: x times minus a half, times two x plus one to the minus three halves, times two from the chain rule. Then add second times derivative of the first, which is two x plus one to the minus a half.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = (2x+1)^{-\frac12} - x(2x+1)^{-\frac32} \]

Take out two x plus one to the minus three halves as a common factor. Inside, you're left with two x plus one minus x, which is x plus one.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{x+1}{(2x+1)^{\frac32}} \]

Watch for: forgetting the factor 2 from the chain rule on \(2x + 1\). It's small, but it changes everything after it.

C1 Differentiation and integration › “Show that” a derivative
152/2162022 P2 Q8Parts (a)
Model solution for parts (a)
(a)

By the product rule:

\[ \begin{aligned} \frac{dy}{dx} &= \sqrt{2x+1} + (x-3)\cdot\frac{1}{2}(2x+1)^{-\frac{1}{2}}(2) \\ &= \sqrt{2x+1} + \frac{x-3}{\sqrt{2x+1}} \\ &= \frac{(2x+1) + (x-3)}{\sqrt{2x+1}} = \frac{3x-2}{\sqrt{2x+1}}. \end{aligned} \]
\(\dfrac{dy}{dx} = \dfrac{3x-2}{\sqrt{2x+1}}\) (shown)
Teaching script for parts (a)
(a)

It's a product: x minus three times root of two x plus one. So, product rule. The root needs the chain rule as well: bring down a half, drop the power to minus a half, and multiply by two.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = \sqrt{2x+1} + (x-3)\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}(2x+1)^{-\frac{1}{2}}(2) = \sqrt{2x+1} + \frac{x-3}{\sqrt{2x+1}} \]

Put everything over the common denominator, root two x plus one. The first term becomes two x plus one on top.

\[ \frac{(2x+1) + (x-3)}{\sqrt{2x+1}} = \frac{3x-2}{\sqrt{2x+1}} \]

Watch for: forgetting to multiply the first term by root two x plus one when you combine. It's the easiest line on the page to rush.

Differentiate, then “hence integrate”

7 questions
C1 Differentiation and integration › Differentiate, then “hence integrate”
153/2162019 P2 Q1Whole questionSyllabus 4047 paper
Model solution
(i)
\[ \frac{d}{dx}\left(x^2\ln x\right) = 2x\ln x + x^2 \cdot \frac{1}{x} = 2x\ln x + x \]
\(2x\ln x + x\)
(ii)
\[ \begin{aligned} \int (2x\ln x + x)\,dx &= x^2\ln x \\ 2\int x\ln x\,dx &= x^2\ln x - \int x\,dx = x^2\ln x - \frac{x^2}{2} \\ \int x\ln x\,dx &= \frac{1}{2}x^2\ln x - \frac{x^2}{4} + c \end{aligned} \]
\(\dfrac{1}{2}x^2\ln x - \dfrac{1}{4}x^2 + c\)
Teaching script
(i)

x squared ln x is a product, so it's the product rule. Differentiate x squared to get two x, times ln x, plus x squared times the derivative of ln x, which is one over x.

\[ \frac{d}{dx}(x^2\ln x) = 2x\ln x + x^2\cdot\frac1x = 2x\ln x + x \]

Watch for: leaving x squared over x as it is. It's x.

(ii)

"Hence" means use part (i) in reverse. Integrating two x ln x plus x takes you back to x squared ln x.

\[ \int (2x\ln x + x)\,dx = x^2\ln x \]

Split the integral. The x on its own integrates to x squared over two, so move it to the other side.

\[ 2\int x\ln x\,dx = x^2\ln x - \frac{x^2}{2} \]

Divide by two and add the constant.

\[ \int x\ln x\,dx = \tfrac12x^2\ln x - \tfrac14x^2 + c \]

Watch for: forgetting to halve the x squared over two term when you divide by two.

C1 Differentiation and integration › Differentiate, then “hence integrate”
154/2162020 P2 Q7Parts (ii)Syllabus 4047 paper
Model solution for parts (ii)
(ii)

From (i), \(\displaystyle\int (8x - 5)(3x - 5)^{\frac23}\,dx = x(3x - 5)^{\frac53}\). Write \(x = \tfrac18(8x - 5) + \tfrac58\).

\[ \begin{aligned} \int x(3x - 5)^{\frac23}\,dx &= \tfrac18\int (8x - 5)(3x - 5)^{\frac23}\,dx + \tfrac58\int (3x - 5)^{\frac23}\,dx \\ &= \tfrac18 x(3x - 5)^{\frac53} + \tfrac58 \cdot \frac{(3x - 5)^{\frac53}}{\frac53 \times 3} + c \\ &= \tfrac18 x(3x - 5)^{\frac53} + \tfrac18(3x - 5)^{\frac53} + c \\ &= \tfrac18(x + 1)(3x - 5)^{\frac53} + c \end{aligned} \]
\(k = \tfrac18\)
Teaching script for parts (ii)
(ii)

"Hence" means run part (i) backwards: integrating eight x minus five, times the bracket to the two thirds, gives x times the bracket to the five thirds. But our integrand only has x in front, not eight x minus five. So we write x as one eighth of eight x minus five, plus five eighths.

\[ x = \tfrac18(8x - 5) + \tfrac58 \]

The first piece integrates straight from part (i) to one eighth x times the bracket to the five thirds. The second piece is an ordinary power: raise it to five thirds and divide by five thirds times three. That gives one eighth of the bracket to the five thirds.

\[ \tfrac18 x(3x - 5)^{\frac53} + \tfrac18(3x - 5)^{\frac53} + c = \tfrac18(x + 1)(3x - 5)^{\frac53} + c \]

So k is one eighth.

Watch for: dividing by 5/3 and stopping there. The inner derivative, 3, has to go into the denominator too.

C1 Differentiation and integration › Differentiate, then “hence integrate”
155/2162021 P2 Q3Parts (b)
Model solution for parts (b)
(b)

From (a), \(\displaystyle\int \frac{x+1}{(2x+1)^{\frac32}}\,dx = \frac{x}{(2x+1)^{\frac12}} + c\). Split the integrand:

\[ \frac{x}{(2x+1)^{\frac32}} = \frac{x+1}{(2x+1)^{\frac32}} - (2x+1)^{-\frac32} \] \[ \begin{aligned} \int_0^4 \frac{x}{(2x+1)^{\frac32}}\,dx &= \left[\frac{x}{(2x+1)^{\frac12}}\right]_0^4 - \int_0^4 (2x+1)^{-\frac32}\,dx \\ &= \frac{4}{3} - \left[\frac{(2x+1)^{-\frac12}}{\left(-\frac12\right)(2)}\right]_0^4 \\ &= \frac43 - \left[-(2x+1)^{-\frac12}\right]_0^4 \\ &= \frac43 - \left(-\frac13 + 1\right) \\ &= \frac23 \end{aligned} \]
\(\dfrac23\)
Teaching script for parts (b)
(b)

"Hence" means use part (a). Part (a) told us that x plus one over two x plus one to the three halves integrates back to x over root two x plus one.

But here there's only x on top, not x plus one. So split it: x is x plus one, minus one. That gives the part-(a) fraction, minus two x plus one to the minus three halves.

\[ \frac{x}{(2x+1)^{\frac32}} = \frac{x+1}{(2x+1)^{\frac32}} - (2x+1)^{-\frac32} \]

The first piece, from nought to four, is four over root nine, so four thirds. The second piece integrates to minus two x plus one to the minus a half. Between nought and four, that's minus a third plus one, which is two thirds.

\[ \frac43 - \left(-\frac13 + 1\right) = \frac23 \]

So the answer is two thirds.

Watch for: the sign when you subtract the second integral. Its value is \(+\frac23\), so the total is \(\frac43 - \frac23\).

C1 Differentiation and integration › Differentiate, then “hence integrate”
156/2162022 P2 Q8Parts (c)
Model solution for parts (c)
(c)

From (a), \(\displaystyle\int \frac{3x-2}{\sqrt{2x+1}}\,dx = (x-3)\sqrt{2x+1} + c\). So

\[ \begin{aligned} 3\int_0^4 \frac{x}{\sqrt{2x+1}}\,dx - 2\int_0^4 \frac{1}{\sqrt{2x+1}}\,dx &= \Big[(x-3)\sqrt{2x+1}\Big]_0^4 \\ &= (1)(3) - (-3)(1) = 6. \end{aligned} \] \[ \int_0^4 \frac{1}{\sqrt{2x+1}}\,dx = \Big[\sqrt{2x+1}\Big]_0^4 = 3 - 1 = 2. \] \[ 3\int_0^4 \frac{x}{\sqrt{2x+1}}\,dx = 6 + 2(2) = 10. \]
\(\displaystyle\int_0^4 \frac{x}{\sqrt{2x+1}}\,dx = \frac{10}{3}\)
Teaching script for parts (c)
(c)

"Hence" means run part (a) backwards. The integral of three x minus two over root two x plus one is x minus three times root two x plus one. Split it into three times the integral you want, minus two times a simpler one.

\[ 3\int_0^4 \frac{x}{\sqrt{2x+1}}\,dx - 2\int_0^4 \frac{dx}{\sqrt{2x+1}} = \Big[(x-3)\sqrt{2x+1}\Big]_0^4 = 3 - (-3) = 6 \]

The second integral is root two x plus one between nought and four: three minus one, which is two.

\[ 3\int_0^4 \frac{x}{\sqrt{2x+1}}\,dx = 6 + 4 = 10 \;\Rightarrow\; \int_0^4 \frac{x}{\sqrt{2x+1}}\,dx = \frac{10}{3} \]

Watch for: the lower limit. At x equals nought, x minus three is minus three, so you're subtracting minus three.

C1 Differentiation and integration › Differentiate, then “hence integrate”
157/2162023 P1 Q4Whole question
Model solution
(a)

By the product rule:

\[ \frac{d}{dx}(xe^{-2x}) = e^{-2x} + x(-2e^{-2x}) = e^{-2x} - 2xe^{-2x}. \]
\(e^{-2x} - 2xe^{-2x}\)
(b)

Integrating the result of (a):

\[ \begin{aligned} \int (e^{-2x} - 2xe^{-2x})\,dx &= xe^{-2x} \\ 2\int xe^{-2x}\,dx &= \int e^{-2x}\,dx - xe^{-2x} \\ &= -\frac{1}{2}e^{-2x} - xe^{-2x} + c_1. \end{aligned} \]
\(\displaystyle\int xe^{-2x}\,dx = -\frac{1}{4}e^{-2x} - \frac{1}{2}xe^{-2x} + c\)
Teaching script
(a)

x times e to the minus two x is a product. Differentiate x to get one, and e to the minus two x to get minus two e to the minus two x.

\[ \frac{d}{dx}(xe^{-2x}) = e^{-2x} - 2xe^{-2x} \]

Watch for: losing the minus two from the chain rule.

(b)

"Hence" means integrate both sides of part (a). The integral of a derivative is the thing you started with, x e to the minus two x. Then move the easy integral, e to the minus two x, over to the other side.

\[ 2\int xe^{-2x}\,dx = \int e^{-2x}\,dx - xe^{-2x} = -\tfrac{1}{2}e^{-2x} - xe^{-2x} + c_1 \]

Divide everything by two.

\[ \int xe^{-2x}\,dx = -\tfrac{1}{4}e^{-2x} - \tfrac{1}{2}xe^{-2x} + c \]

Watch for: halving most of it but not the minus a half, which leaves minus a half e to the minus two x behind.

C1 Differentiation and integration › Differentiate, then “hence integrate”
158/2162024 P1 Q2Whole question
Model solution
(a)
\[ \frac{d}{dx}\left(x^2 + x + 1\right)^{\frac12} = \tfrac12\left(x^2 + x + 1\right)^{-\frac12}(2x + 1) \]
\(\dfrac{2x + 1}{2\sqrt{x^2 + x + 1}}\)
(b)
\[ \begin{aligned} \int \frac{16x + 8}{\sqrt{x^2 + x + 1}}\,dx &= 16 \int \frac{2x + 1}{2\sqrt{x^2 + x + 1}}\,dx \\ &= 16\sqrt{x^2 + x + 1} + c \end{aligned} \]
\(16\sqrt{x^2 + x + 1} + c\)
Teaching script
(a)

Write the square root as a power of a half and use the chain rule. Bring the half down, drop the power by one, and multiply by the derivative of what's inside.

\[ \tfrac12\left(x^2 + x + 1\right)^{-\frac12}(2x + 1) = \frac{2x + 1}{2\sqrt{x^2 + x + 1}} \]

Watch for: forgetting the two x plus one from the inside. The chain rule always wants its cut.

(b)

"Hence" means use part (a) backwards. Sixteen x plus eight is eight lots of two x plus one, and the answer to (a) has a two underneath. So what you're integrating is sixteen times the derivative from (a).

\[ \int \frac{16x + 8}{\sqrt{x^2 + x + 1}}\,dx = 16\int \frac{2x + 1}{2\sqrt{x^2 + x + 1}}\,dx = 16\sqrt{x^2 + x + 1} + c \]

Watch for: ending up with eight instead of sixteen because you ignored the two underneath from part (a).

C1 Differentiation and integration › Differentiate, then “hence integrate”
159/2162025 P1 Q4Whole question
Model solution
(a)
\[ \frac{d}{dx}\left(x^{n+1}\ln x\right) = (n+1)x^n \ln x + x^{n+1} \cdot \frac{1}{x} \]
\((n+1)x^n \ln x + x^n\)
(b)

Integrating the result of (a):

\[ \begin{aligned} \int \left[(n+1)x^n\ln x + x^n\right] dx &= x^{n+1}\ln x \\ (n+1)\int x^n \ln x\,dx &= x^{n+1}\ln x - \int x^n\,dx \\ &= x^{n+1}\ln x - \frac{x^{n+1}}{n+1} + c' \end{aligned} \]
\(\displaystyle\int x^n \ln x\,dx = \frac{x^{n+1}\ln x}{n+1} - \frac{x^{n+1}}{(n+1)^2} + c\)
Teaching script
(a)

x to the n plus one times ln x is a product, so product rule. Differentiate the first part and keep ln x, then keep the first part and differentiate ln x, which gives one over x.

\[ (n+1)x^n \ln x + x^{n+1} \cdot \frac{1}{x} \]

x to the n plus one over x tidies up to x to the n. So the derivative is n plus one, x to the n, ln x, plus x to the n.

Watch for: leaving x to the n plus one over x as it is. Part (b) needs it as x to the n, so simplify now and thank yourself later.

(b)

"Hence" means integrate what you got in part (a). Integrating a derivative takes you straight back to where you started, so the integral of the whole expression is x to the n plus one ln x.

\[ (n+1)\int x^n \ln x\,dx = x^{n+1}\ln x - \int x^n\,dx \]

The integral of x to the n is x to the n plus one over n plus one. Then divide everything by n plus one.

\[ \int x^n \ln x\,dx = \frac{x^{n+1}\ln x}{n+1} - \frac{x^{n+1}}{(n+1)^2} + c \]

Watch for: dividing only the first term by n plus one. The second term ends up with n plus one squared underneath.

Stationary points and their nature

9 questions
C1 Differentiation and integration › Stationary points and their nature
160/2162019 P1 Q8Whole questionSyllabus 4047 paper
Model solution
(i)
\[ \begin{aligned} \frac{dy}{dx} &= 1 + \frac{2(x-2) - (2x+5)(1)}{(x-2)^2} = 1 - \frac{9}{(x-2)^2} \\ \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} &= \frac{d}{dx}\left[1 - 9(x-2)^{-2}\right] = 18(x-2)^{-3} = \frac{18}{(x-2)^3} \end{aligned} \]
\(\dfrac{dy}{dx} = 1 - \dfrac{9}{(x-2)^2}\), \(\quad \dfrac{d^2y}{dx^2} = \dfrac{18}{(x-2)^3}\)
(ii)
\[ \begin{aligned} 1 - \frac{9}{(x-2)^2} &= 0 \\ (x-2)^2 &= 9 \\ x - 2 &= \pm 3 \end{aligned} \]
\(x = 5\) and \(x = -1\)
(iii)
\[ x = 5:\ \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = \frac{18}{27} = \frac{2}{3} \gt 0 \qquad x = -1:\ \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = \frac{18}{-27} = -\frac{2}{3} \lt 0 \]
Minimum point at \(x = 5\); maximum point at \(x = -1\)
Teaching script
(i)

The curve is x plus a fraction. The x differentiates to one. For the fraction, two x plus five over x minus two, use the quotient rule.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = 1 + \frac{2(x-2) - (2x+5)(1)}{(x-2)^2} = 1 - \frac{9}{(x-2)^2} \]

The top tidies up to two x minus four minus two x minus five, which is minus nine. Nice and small.

For the second derivative, rewrite the fraction as minus nine times x minus two to the power minus two, then differentiate with the chain rule.

\[ \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = 18(x-2)^{-3} = \frac{18}{(x-2)^3} \]

Watch for: the order in the quotient rule numerator. Bottom times derivative of top, minus top times derivative of bottom.

(ii)

For stationary points, set dy by dx equal to zero.

\[ 1 - \frac{9}{(x-2)^2} = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; (x-2)^2 = 9 \]

Square root both sides and keep the plus and the minus. x minus two is three or minus three.

\[ x - 2 = \pm 3 \;\Rightarrow\; x = 5 \text{ or } x = -1 \]

Watch for: taking only the positive root and losing x equals minus one.

(iii)

Put each x into the second derivative and look at the sign.

\[ x = 5:\ \tfrac{18}{27} = \tfrac{2}{3} \gt 0 \qquad x = -1:\ \tfrac{18}{-27} = -\tfrac{2}{3} \lt 0 \]

Positive means minimum, negative means maximum. So x equals five gives a minimum point and x equals minus one gives a maximum point.

Watch for: getting the rule backwards. A positive second derivative means the curve bends upwards like a smile, so it's a minimum.

C1 Differentiation and integration › Stationary points and their nature
161/2162020 P1 Q6Whole questionSyllabus 4047 paper
Model solution
\[ y = x^2 + 4x^{-2} \qquad \frac{dy}{dx} = 2x - 8x^{-3} = 2x - \frac{8}{x^3} \] \[ \begin{aligned} 2x - \frac{8}{x^3} &= 0 \\ x^4 &= 4 \\ x &= \pm\sqrt{2} \end{aligned} \] \[ x = \pm\sqrt{2}:\quad y = 2 + \frac{4}{2} = 4 \] \[ \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = 2 + \frac{24}{x^4} = 2 + \frac{24}{4} = 8 \gt 0 \text{ at both points} \]
\((\sqrt{2}, 4)\) and \((-\sqrt{2}, 4)\) are both minimum points
Teaching script

Write the four over x squared as four x to the minus two, then differentiate.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = 2x - \frac{8}{x^3} \]

For stationary points, set this to zero. Multiply through by x cubed.

\[ 2x^4 = 8 \;\Rightarrow\; x^4 = 4 \;\Rightarrow\; x = \pm\sqrt2 \]

Both values give x squared equal to two, so y is two plus four over two, which is four, for both.

\[ (\sqrt2, 4) \text{ and } (-\sqrt2, 4) \]

The second derivative is two plus twenty-four over x to the four. At both points that's two plus six, eight, which is positive. So both are minimum points.

Watch for: taking only the positive fourth root. x to the four equals four has two real solutions.

C1 Differentiation and integration › Stationary points and their nature
162/2162021 P1 Q8Whole question
Model solution
(a)

Subtract the three unplanted right-angled triangles from the rectangle. \(PD = 50 - x\) and \(CQ = 80 - 2x\).

\[ \begin{aligned} \text{Area of } \triangle ABP &= \tfrac12(80)(x) = 40x \\ \text{Area of } \triangle PDQ &= \tfrac12(50-x)(2x) = 50x - x^2 \\ \text{Area of } \triangle BCQ &= \tfrac12(50)(80-2x) = 2000 - 50x \\ A &= 4000 - 40x - (50x - x^2) - (2000 - 50x) \\ &= x^2 - 40x + 2000 \end{aligned} \]
\(A = x^2 - 40x + 2000\) (shown)
(b)
\[ \frac{dA}{dx} = 2x - 40 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x = 20 \] \[ A = 20^2 - 40(20) + 2000 = 1600 \] \[ \frac{d^2A}{dx^2} = 2 \gt 0, \text{ so } A \text{ is a minimum.} \]
Stationary value \(A = 1600\) m², which is a minimum.
Teaching script
(a)

This one asks you to show an area formula, and measuring the planted bit directly is a pain. So go the other way round: start with the whole rectangle and take away the three right-angled triangles that aren't planted.

First we need the missing sides. PD is fifty minus x, and CQ is eighty minus two x.

\[ PD = 50 - x, \qquad CQ = 80 - 2x \]

Each triangle is half base times height. Triangle ABP is a half of eighty times x, so forty x. Triangle PDQ is a half of fifty minus x, times two x, which comes to fifty x minus x squared. Triangle BCQ is a half of fifty times eighty minus two x, so two thousand minus fifty x.

\[ 40x, \qquad 50x - x^2, \qquad 2000 - 50x \]

The rectangle is four thousand. Now take away all three, and put brackets round each one so the signs behave themselves.

\[ A = 4000 - 40x - (50x - x^2) - (2000 - 50x) = x^2 - 40x + 2000 \]

And that's exactly the expression they gave you, so we're done with this part.

Watch for: dropping the brackets when you subtract \(50x - x^2\). If you do, the \(x^2\) term comes out negative and the whole thing falls apart.

(b)

Stationary value means the first derivative is zero. Differentiate A with respect to x and you get two x minus forty. Set that to zero and x is twenty.

\[ \frac{dA}{dx} = 2x - 40 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x = 20 \]

Put that back into A: four hundred, minus eight hundred, plus two thousand. That's one thousand six hundred.

\[ A = 20^2 - 40(20) + 2000 = 1600 \]

Is it a max or a min? Check the second derivative. It's two, which is positive, so it's a minimum.

\[ \frac{d^2A}{dx^2} = 2 \gt 0 \]

So the stationary value of A is sixteen hundred square metres, and it's a minimum.

Watch for: stopping at \(x = 20\). They want the stationary value of \(A\) itself, and whether it's a max or min.

163/2162022 P2 Q4Parts (b)(c)
Model solution for parts (b)(c)
(b)
\[ \frac{dA}{dx} = 12x - \frac{16000}{3x^2}. \]

For a stationary value, \(\dfrac{dA}{dx} = 0\):

\[ 12x = \frac{16000}{3x^2} \quad\Rightarrow\quad x^3 = \frac{16000}{36} = \frac{4000}{9} \quad\Rightarrow\quad x = \sqrt[3]{\frac{4000}{9}}. \]
\(x = 7.63\) (3 s.f.)
(c)
\[ \frac{d^2A}{dx^2} = 12 + \frac{32000}{3x^3}. \]

At \(x = 7.63\), \(\dfrac{d^2A}{dx^2} = 12 + \dfrac{32000}{3}\cdot\dfrac{9}{4000} = 36 \gt 0\).

The total surface area is a minimum.
Teaching script for parts (b)(c)
(b)

Write the second term as sixteen thousand over three, times x to the minus one. Now it's a power you can differentiate without fuss.

\[ \frac{dA}{dx} = 12x - \frac{16000}{3x^2} \]

Set the derivative to zero. Multiply through by three x squared and you're left with thirty-six x cubed equals sixteen thousand.

\[ x^3 = \frac{4000}{9} \;\Rightarrow\; x = \sqrt[3]{\tfrac{4000}{9}} = 7.63 \]

Give x to three significant figures: seven point six three.

Watch for: dropping the minus sign when you differentiate x to the minus one.

(c)

Now the second derivative test. Differentiating minus sixteen thousand over three x squared gives plus thirty-two thousand over three x cubed.

\[ \frac{d^2A}{dx^2} = 12 + \frac{32000}{3x^3} \]

At the stationary point, x cubed is four thousand over nine, so that fraction comes out to exactly twenty-four, and the second derivative is thirty-six. It's positive, so you've got a minimum.

Watch for: writing "minimum" without the value or the sign of the second derivative. The examiner wants to see why.

164/2162023 P1 Q9Whole question
Model solution
(a)
\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = 1 - x - 6x^2. \]

At stationary points, \(\dfrac{dy}{dx} = 0\):

\[ 6x^2 + x - 1 = 0 \quad\Rightarrow\quad (3x - 1)(2x + 1) = 0. \]
\(x = \dfrac{1}{3}\) and \(x = -\dfrac{1}{2}\)
(b)

Let \(g = \dfrac{dy}{dx} = 1 - x - 6x^2\). For the gradient to be a maximum, \(\dfrac{dg}{dx} = 0\):

\[ \frac{dg}{dx} = -1 - 12x = 0 \quad\Rightarrow\quad x = -\frac{1}{12}. \]

\(\dfrac{d^2g}{dx^2} = -12 \lt 0\), so the gradient is a maximum at \(x = -\tfrac{1}{12}\).

\[ x\text{-coordinate of midpoint of } AB = \frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{3} - \frac{1}{2}\right) = \frac{1}{2}\left(-\frac{1}{6}\right) = -\frac{1}{12}. \]
Both \(x\)-coordinates equal \(-\dfrac{1}{12}\) (shown).
Teaching script
(a)

Stationary points are where the gradient is zero. Differentiate term by term.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = 1 - x - 6x^2 \]

Set it to zero and multiply through by minus one, so the x squared term is positive. Factorising is much friendlier that way.

\[ 6x^2 + x - 1 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; (3x-1)(2x+1) = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x = \tfrac{1}{3},\; -\tfrac{1}{2} \]

Watch for: signs in the brackets. Expand them back out to check you get plus x in the middle.

(b)

This part wants where the gradient itself is largest. Treat dy by dx as a brand-new function and find its stationary point, which means differentiating again.

\[ \frac{d}{dx}\left(1 - x - 6x^2\right) = -1 - 12x = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x = -\tfrac{1}{12} \]

The next derivative is minus twelve, which is negative, so that point is a maximum of the gradient. Now find the midpoint of A and B by averaging a third and minus a half.

\[ \tfrac{1}{2}\left(\tfrac{1}{3} - \tfrac{1}{2}\right) = -\tfrac{1}{12} \]

The two x-coordinates match, and that's what you had to show.

Watch for: setting dy by dx to zero again. That gets you A and B, not the steepest point.

Model solution
(a)
\[ \begin{aligned} 2x^2 + 4xy &= 600 \\ y &= \frac{600 - 2x^2}{4x} = \frac{300 - x^2}{2x} \end{aligned} \] \[ V = x^2 y = x^2 \cdot \frac{300 - x^2}{2x} = \frac{300x - x^3}{2} = 150x - \frac{x^3}{2} \quad \text{(shown)} \]
(b)
\[ \frac{dV}{dx} = 150 - \frac{3x^2}{2} \]

For stationary \(V\): \(150 - \dfrac{3x^2}{2} = 0 \Rightarrow x^2 = 100 \Rightarrow x = 10\) (since \(x \gt 0\)).

\[ V = 150(10) - \frac{10^3}{2} = 1500 - 500 = 1000 \] \[ \frac{d^2V}{dx^2} = -3x = -30 \lt 0 \ \text{ when } x = 10 \]
Stationary value of \(V = 1000\ \text{cm}^3\); it is a maximum.
Teaching script
(a)

Use the surface area to get y in terms of x, then put that into the volume. The two square ends give two x squared, and the four rectangular sides give four x y.

\[ 2x^2 + 4xy = 600 \;\Rightarrow\; y = \frac{300 - x^2}{2x} \]

Volume is base area times height, so x squared times y. One of the x's cancels.

\[ V = x^2 \cdot \frac{300 - x^2}{2x} = 150x - \frac{x^3}{2} \]

Watch for: counting only one square face, which gives x squared plus four x y. A closed box has a lid.

(b)

Stationary value means dV by dx equals zero. Differentiate: one hundred and fifty minus three x squared over two.

\[ 150 - \frac{3x^2}{2} = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x^2 = 100 \;\Rightarrow\; x = 10 \]

Take x equals ten, because a length can't be negative. Then V is fifteen hundred minus five hundred, which is one thousand.

To check what kind it is, the second derivative is minus three x, and at x equals ten that's minus thirty. Negative means the curve is frowning, so it's a maximum.

\[ V = 1000\ \text{cm}^3, \qquad \frac{d^2V}{dx^2} = -30 \lt 0 \;\Rightarrow\; \text{maximum} \]

Watch for: stopping at x equals ten. The question wants the stationary value of V, so finish with the one thousand.

166/2162024 P2 Q5Parts (a)
Model solution for parts (a)
(a)
\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = e^x - e^{-x}; \qquad \text{at } x = 0:\ \frac{dy}{dx} = e^0 - e^0 = 1 - 1 = 0 \]
\(\dfrac{dy}{dx} = 0\) at \(x = 0\), so the curve has a stationary point at \(x = 0\).
Teaching script for parts (a)
(a)

To show there's a stationary point, show that dy by dx is zero there. E to the x differentiates to itself, and e to the minus x differentiates to minus e to the minus x.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = e^x - e^{-x}; \quad x = 0: \ 1 - 1 = 0 \]

At x equals zero both exponentials are one, so the gradient is one minus one, zero. That's your stationary point at x equals zero.

Watch for: differentiating e to the minus x as plus e to the minus x. The minus in the power comes down in front.

167/2162024 P2 Q11Parts (b)
Model solution for parts (b)
(b)
\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = -2e^{-2x}\sin x + e^{-2x}\cos x = e^{-2x}(\cos x - 2\sin x) \]

At a stationary point, \(\dfrac{dy}{dx} = 0\). Since \(e^{-2x} \neq 0\):

\[ \cos x - 2\sin x = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; \tan x = \tfrac12 \]

For \(0 \lt x \lt \pi\), \(\tan x \gt 0\) only in the first quadrant, so the only solution is \(x = \tan^{-1}\tfrac12\) (shown).

\(\dfrac{dy}{dx} = e^{-2x}(\cos x - 2\sin x)\)
Teaching script for parts (b)
(b)

Product rule. E to the minus two x differentiates to minus two e to the minus two x, and sin x differentiates to cos x.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = e^{-2x}(\cos x - 2\sin x) \]

At a stationary point this is zero. E to the minus two x is never zero, so the bracket has to be. That means cos x equals two sin x, so tan x is a half.

Between zero and pi, tan is positive only in the first quadrant. So there's exactly one solution: x equals tan inverse of a half.

Watch for: dividing out e to the minus two x without saying why. Say it's never zero.

C1 Differentiation and integration › Stationary points and their nature
168/2162025 P1 Q6Whole question
Model solution
(a)
\[ y = \tfrac{1}{2}x^2 - 3x^{\frac{1}{2}} + 1 \;\Rightarrow\; \frac{dy}{dx} = x - \tfrac{3}{2}x^{-\frac{1}{2}} \]

At a stationary point, \(\dfrac{dy}{dx} = 0\):

\[ \begin{aligned} x - \frac{3}{2\sqrt{x}} &= 0 \\ x^{\frac{3}{2}} &= \frac{3}{2} \\ x &= \left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{\frac{2}{3}} \end{aligned} \]
\(x = \left(\tfrac{3}{2}\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}\) (shown)
(b)
\[ \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = 1 + \tfrac{3}{4}x^{-\frac{3}{2}} \]

At \(x = \left(\tfrac{3}{2}\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}\), \(x^{-\frac{3}{2}} = \tfrac{2}{3}\):

\[ \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = 1 + \tfrac{3}{4}\left(\tfrac{2}{3}\right) = \tfrac{3}{2} \gt 0 \]
The stationary point is a minimum point.
Teaching script
(a)

Stationary point means d y by d x equals zero. First rewrite the root as a power, minus three x to the half, then differentiate each term.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = x - \tfrac{3}{2}x^{-\frac{1}{2}} \]

Set it to zero. Move the second term across and multiply both sides by root x. x times root x is x to the three halves, so x to the three halves equals three halves.

\[ x^{\frac{3}{2}} = \frac{3}{2} \;\Rightarrow\; x = \left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{\frac{2}{3}} \]

Raise both sides to the power two thirds and you've got the x they asked for.

Watch for: differentiating minus three root x as minus three halves x to the half. The power goes down by one, to negative a half.

(b)

To find out what kind of point it is, take the second derivative. x goes to one. Minus three halves x to the negative half gives plus three quarters x to the negative three halves.

\[ \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = 1 + \tfrac{3}{4}x^{-\frac{3}{2}} \]

From part (a), x to the three halves is three halves, so x to the negative three halves is two thirds. The second derivative is one plus a half, three halves. Positive, so it's a minimum.

Watch for: getting the sign of that second term wrong. Minus times minus gives plus.

Increasing and decreasing functions; no stationary point

C1 Differentiation and integration › Increasing and decreasing functions; no stationary point
169/2162020 P1 Q4Whole questionSyllabus 4047 paper
Model solution
\[ \begin{aligned} \frac{dy}{dx} &= \frac{2(x^2+4) - (2x-3)(2x)}{(x^2+4)^2} \\ &= \frac{-2x^2 + 6x + 8}{(x^2+4)^2} = \frac{-2(x-4)(x+1)}{(x^2+4)^2} \end{aligned} \]

Since \((x^2+4)^2 \gt 0\) for all \(x\), \(y\) is increasing when \(-2(x-4)(x+1) \gt 0\):

\[ (x-4)(x+1) \lt 0 \;\Rightarrow\; -1 \lt x \lt 4 \]
\(-1 \lt x \lt 4\)
Teaching script

"Increasing" means dy by dx is positive. It's a quotient, so use the quotient rule.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{2(x^2+4) - (2x-3)(2x)}{(x^2+4)^2} \]

Expand the top: two x squared plus eight, minus four x squared plus six x. That's minus two x squared plus six x plus eight. Factorise.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{-2(x-4)(x+1)}{(x^2+4)^2} \]

The denominator is a square, so it's always positive. The sign depends on the top. Minus two times the bracket is positive when the bracket is negative.

\[ (x-4)(x+1) \lt 0 \;\Rightarrow\; -1 \lt x \lt 4 \]

y is increasing for x between minus one and four.

Watch for: forgetting to flip the inequality when you divide by minus two.

C1 Differentiation and integration › Increasing and decreasing functions; no stationary point
170/2162022 P1 Q9Parts (a)
Model solution for parts (a)
(a)
\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = 3x^2 - 2ax + b \]

\(y\) is always increasing when \(\dfrac{dy}{dx} \gt 0\) for all \(x\). The quadratic \(3x^2 - 2ax + b\) has positive leading coefficient, so this requires discriminant \(\lt 0\):

\[ (-2a)^2 - 4(3)(b) \lt 0 \;\Rightarrow\; 4a^2 - 12b \lt 0 \;\Rightarrow\; a^2 \lt 3b \]
\(a^2 \lt 3b\) (shown)
Teaching script for parts (a)
(a)

Always increasing means the gradient is positive for every x. Differentiate: three x squared minus two a x plus b.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = 3x^2 - 2ax + b \]

That's a quadratic with a positive leading coefficient. It stays above zero for every x only if it never touches the x-axis, so the discriminant must be negative.

\[ 4a^2 - 12b \lt 0 \;\Rightarrow\; a^2 \lt 3b \]

Watch for: using \(\le 0\). A zero discriminant gives a point where the gradient is 0.

C1 Differentiation and integration › Increasing and decreasing functions; no stationary point
171/2162022 P1 Q13Parts (a)
Model solution for parts (a)
(a)
\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{4(x+1) - (4x+2)(1)}{(x+1)^2} = \frac{2}{(x+1)^2} \]

The numerator is the constant 2, so \(\dfrac{dy}{dx} \ne 0\) for any \(x\) (in fact \(\dfrac{dy}{dx} \gt 0\) for all \(x \gt -1\)). Hence the curve has no stationary point.

\(\dfrac{dy}{dx} = \dfrac{2}{(x+1)^2} \ne 0\) for all \(x\), so there is no stationary point.
Teaching script for parts (a)
(a)

To show there's no stationary point, find d y by d x and show it can never be zero. Use the quotient rule: bottom times derivative of top, minus top times derivative of bottom, all over bottom squared.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{4(x+1) - (4x+2)}{(x+1)^2} = \frac{2}{(x+1)^2} \]

The numerator comes out as the constant two, so the gradient can never be zero. In fact it's always positive. So the curve has no stationary point.

Watch for: doing the quotient rule subtraction the wrong way round, which gives \(-2\) on top.

C1 Differentiation and integration › Increasing and decreasing functions; no stationary point
172/2162023 P1 Q6Whole question
Model solution
(a)

By the quotient rule:

\[ f'(x) = \frac{2ax(x - a) - ax^2(1)}{(x-a)^2} = \frac{ax^2 - 2a^2x}{(x-a)^2}. \]
\(f'(x) = \dfrac{ax(x - 2a)}{(x-a)^2}\)
(b)
\[ g'(x) = (x-a)^2\cdot\frac{ax(x - 2a)}{(x-a)^2} = ax(x - 2a). \]

For \(x \gt a\), \(a \gt 0\) and \(x \gt 0\), so \(g'(x) \lt 0\) exactly when \(x - 2a \lt 0\), i.e. \(a \lt x \lt 2a\).

Since \(g\) decreases for \(a \lt x \lt 8\), \(2a = 8\).

\(a = 4\)
Teaching script
(a)

f is a quotient, a x squared over x minus a, so it's the quotient rule: bottom times derivative of top, minus top times derivative of bottom, all over bottom squared. Treat a like any other number.

\[ f'(x) = \frac{2ax(x-a) - ax^2}{(x-a)^2} = \frac{ax^2 - 2a^2x}{(x-a)^2} = \frac{ax(x-2a)}{(x-a)^2} \]

Watch for: swapping the order on top. The quotient rule is fussy about order, and swapping it flips the sign.

(b)

Multiply part (a) by x minus a, squared. The denominator cancels, and g dash is a x times x minus two a.

\[ g'(x) = ax(x - 2a) \]

For x bigger than a, both a and x are positive, so the sign comes down to x minus two a alone. It's negative between a and two a. You're told g decreases from a to eight, so two a is eight.

\[ a = 4 \]

Watch for: setting a equal to eight. Eight is the top end of the interval, and that's two a.

173/2162024 P2 Q4Parts (a)(ii)
Model solution for parts (a)(ii)
(a)(ii)

\(\dfrac{dy}{dx} = \dfrac{20}{(5 - 3x)^2}\). The numerator is 20, which is never zero, so \(\dfrac{dy}{dx} \neq 0\) for all \(x\) and the curve has no stationary points.

Teaching script for parts (a)(ii)
(a)(ii)

For a stationary point, dy by dx has to be zero. Our derivative is twenty over something squared. A fraction is only zero when the top is zero, and the top here is twenty. Twenty is never going to be zero, however hard it tries.

\(\dfrac{dy}{dx} = \dfrac{20}{(5 - 3x)^2} \neq 0\) for all \(x\)

So the curve has no stationary points.

Watch for: arguing about the bottom of the fraction. It's the constant twenty on top that settles it.

C1 Differentiation and integration › Increasing and decreasing functions; no stationary point
174/2162025 P2 Q5Whole question
Model solution

By the quotient rule:

\[ \begin{aligned} \frac{dy}{dx} &= \frac{(1 + e^{-x})(e^{-x}) - (1 - e^{-x})(-e^{-x})}{(1 + e^{-x})^2} \\ &= \frac{e^{-x} + e^{-2x} + e^{-x} - e^{-2x}}{(1 + e^{-x})^2} \\ &= \frac{2e^{-x}}{(1 + e^{-x})^2} \end{aligned} \]

For all real \(x\), \(e^{-x} \gt 0\), so \(2e^{-x} \gt 0\) and \((1 + e^{-x})^2 \gt 0\).

\(\dfrac{dy}{dx} \gt 0\) for all real \(x\), so \(y\) is an increasing function for all real values of \(x\).
Teaching script

To show y is increasing for every x, you show d y by d x is positive for every x. It's a fraction, so quotient rule: bottom times derivative of top, minus top times derivative of bottom, all over bottom squared.

The top, one minus e to the minus x, differentiates to plus e to the minus x. The bottom, one plus e to the minus x, differentiates to minus e to the minus x.

\[ \frac{(1 + e^{-x})(e^{-x}) - (1 - e^{-x})(-e^{-x})}{(1 + e^{-x})^2} \]

Expand the top. The e to the minus two x terms cancel, and the e to the minus x terms add up.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{2e^{-x}}{(1 + e^{-x})^2} \]

Now explain the sign, in words. e to the minus x is positive for every real x, so the top is positive. The bottom is a positive number squared, so that's positive too. So d y by d x is positive for every x, and y is increasing for all real x.

Watch for: getting the derivative perfectly right and then saying nothing about the sign. That one sentence about e to the minus x being positive is the last mark.

Connected rates of change

7 questions
C1 Differentiation and integration › Connected rates of change
175/2162019 P1 Q4Whole questionSyllabus 4047 paper
Model solution
\[ A = 6x^2 \qquad \frac{dA}{dx} = 12x \] \[ \text{When } x = 10,\ \frac{dA}{dx} = 120, \qquad \frac{dA}{dt} = -48 \] \[ \begin{aligned} \frac{dA}{dt} &= \frac{dA}{dx}\times\frac{dx}{dt} \\ -48 &= 120 \times \frac{dx}{dt} \\ \frac{dx}{dt} &= -0.4 \end{aligned} \]
\(x\) is decreasing at \(0.4\) cm/s (rate of change \(= -0.4\) cm/s)
Teaching script

This is connected rates of change. You know how fast the surface area is changing and you want how fast the edge is changing. The chain rule links them through dA by dx.

A cube has six square faces, so the area is six x squared. Differentiate.

\[ A = 6x^2, \qquad \frac{dA}{dx} = 12x \]

At x equals ten, dA by dx is one hundred and twenty. The area is decreasing at forty-eight, so dA by dt is minus forty-eight.

\[ \frac{dA}{dt} = \frac{dA}{dx}\times\frac{dx}{dt} \;\Rightarrow\; -48 = 120\,\frac{dx}{dt} \]

Divide, and dx by dt is minus nought point four.

So the edge is shrinking at nought point four centimetres per second.

Watch for: using plus forty-eight. "Decreasing" makes the rate negative, and that sign should follow you all the way to the answer.

C1 Differentiation and integration › Connected rates of change
176/2162020 P2 Q11Whole questionSyllabus 4047 paper
Model solution
(i)

The empty part at the top is a cone of height \(x\) similar to the container. Its radius \(r\) satisfies \(\dfrac{r}{x} = \dfrac{15}{25}\), so \(r = \dfrac{3x}{5}\).

\[ \begin{aligned} V &= \tfrac13\pi(15)^2(25) - \tfrac13\pi\left(\tfrac{3x}{5}\right)^2 x \\ &= 1875\pi - \frac{3\pi x^3}{25} \\ &= 3\pi\left(625 - \frac{x^3}{25}\right) \end{aligned} \]
\(V = 3\pi\left(625 - \dfrac{x^3}{25}\right)\)  (shown)
(ii)
\[ \frac{dV}{dx} = -\frac{9\pi x^2}{25}, \qquad \frac{dV}{dt} = kx^2 \] \[ \frac{dt}{dx} = \frac{dV}{dx} \div \frac{dV}{dt} = -\frac{9\pi x^2}{25} \times \frac{1}{kx^2} \]
\(\dfrac{dt}{dx} = -\dfrac{9\pi}{25k}\)
(iii)
\[ t = \int -\frac{9\pi}{25k}\,dx = -\frac{9\pi x}{25k} + C \]

At \(t = 0\) the container is empty, so \(x = 25\): \(0 = -\dfrac{9\pi}{k} + C\), giving \(C = \dfrac{9\pi}{k}\).

\[ t = \frac{9\pi(25 - x)}{25k} \]

Height of liquid \(25 - x = 12\) when \(t = 72\pi\).

\[ 72\pi = \frac{9\pi(12)}{25k} \;\Rightarrow\; k = \frac{108}{1800} \]
\(k = 0.06\)  \(\left(=\tfrac{3}{50}\right)\)
Teaching script
(i)

Picture the liquid filling the cone, with an empty little cone sitting at the top, height x. That empty cone is similar to the whole cone, so its radius is in the same ratio, fifteen to twenty-five. That gives a radius of three x over five.

\[ \frac{r}{x} = \frac{15}{25} \;\Rightarrow\; r = \frac{3x}{5} \]

Volume of liquid is the whole cone minus the empty bit. The whole cone is one third pi times fifteen squared times twenty-five, which is eighteen seventy-five pi. Pull out a factor of three pi and you're at the form they gave.

\[ V = 1875\pi - \frac{3\pi x^3}{25} = 3\pi\left(625 - \frac{x^3}{25}\right) \]

Watch for: treating x as the depth of the liquid. Here x is the height of the empty part at the top.

(ii)

Differentiate V with respect to x: minus nine pi x squared over twenty-five. The rate of filling, dV by dt, is given as k x squared. So use the chain rule this way round: dt by dx is dV by dx divided by dV by dt.

\[ \frac{dt}{dx} = -\frac{9\pi x^2}{25} \times \frac{1}{kx^2} = -\frac{9\pi}{25k} \]

The x squared cancels out, which leaves dt by dx as a constant. Rather tidy.

Watch for: multiplying the two rates instead of dividing. Check the units: you should end up with time over length.

(iii)

Integrating a constant is the easy kind: t equals minus nine pi x over twenty-five k, plus C. At the start the container is empty, so x is twenty-five when t is zero. That gives C equals nine pi over k.

\[ t = \frac{9\pi(25 - x)}{25k} \]

Twenty-five minus x is the depth of the liquid. A depth of twelve centimetres at time seventy-two pi gives us an equation for k.

\[ 72\pi = \frac{9\pi(12)}{25k} \;\Rightarrow\; k = 0.06 \]

Watch for: putting x equals twelve. Twelve is the depth, so x, the empty height, is thirteen.

After 60 s, \(V = 500 \times 60 = 30\,000\) cm\(^3\).

\[ \tfrac43\pi r^3 = 30\,000 \;\Rightarrow\; r^3 = \frac{22\,500}{\pi} \;\Rightarrow\; r = 19.28 \]
\(r = 19.3\) cm (3 s.f.)
(a)(ii)

\(\dfrac{dr}{dt} = 0.107\) cm/s (3 s.f.)
(b)

\(k = 1.2 \times 2 = 2.4\), so \(V = \dfrac{2.4}{P}\).

\[ \frac{dV}{dP} = -\frac{2.4}{P^2} \]

When \(V = 2\), \(P = 1.2\):

\[ \frac{dV}{dP} = -\frac{2.4}{1.44} = -\frac53 \]

Teaching script
(a)(i)

Air goes in at five hundred cubic centimetres per second, so after sixty seconds the volume is thirty thousand. Set the sphere formula equal to that and solve for r.

\[ \tfrac43\pi r^3 = 30\,000 \;\Rightarrow\; r = 19.28 \]

The radius is nineteen point three centimetres. Keep nineteen point two eight in your calculator for the next part.

Watch for: using the rounded 19.3 in part (ii). Carry the unrounded value forward.

(a)(ii)

Connected rates. We know dV by dt, and we want dr by dt. Differentiate the volume formula to get dV by dr, four pi r squared, then divide.

\[ \frac{dr}{dt} = \frac{500}{4\pi(19.28)^2} = 0.107 \]

So the radius is growing at zero point one zero seven centimetres per second.

Watch for: multiplying 500 by 4πr² instead of dividing. It's dV/dt divided by dV/dr.

(b)

P V equals k. Use the pair they gave, P equals one point two and V equals two, and k comes out as two point four. So V equals two point four over P.

\[ V = \frac{2.4}{P}, \qquad \frac{dV}{dP} = -\frac{2.4}{P^2} \]

When V is two, P is one point two. Substitute that in: minus two point four over one point four four, which is minus five thirds.

So V is decreasing at one and two thirds litres per atmosphere.

Watch for: losing the minus sign. It's telling you V goes down as P goes up, which is the whole point.

Model solution
(a)

When \(t = 0\), \(\dfrac{dr}{dt} = 0.5\): \(\dfrac{k}{2(0) + 1} = 0.5\), so \(k = 0.5\).

\(k = 0.5\) (shown)
(b)
\[ r = \int \frac{0.5}{2t+1}\,dt = \frac{1}{4}\ln(2t+1) + c. \]

When \(t = 0\), \(r = 1\): \(1 = \tfrac{1}{4}\ln 1 + c\), so \(c = 1\).

\(r = \dfrac{1}{4}\ln(2t+1) + 1\)
(c)

\(A = \pi r^2\), so \(\dfrac{dA}{dr} = 2\pi r\).

At \(t = 3\): \(r = \tfrac{1}{4}\ln 7 + 1 = 1.4865\) and \(\dfrac{dr}{dt} = \dfrac{0.5}{7} = \dfrac{1}{14}\).

\[ \frac{dA}{dt} = \frac{dA}{dr}\times\frac{dr}{dt} = 2\pi(1.4865)\times\frac{1}{14} = 0.667. \]
Rate of increase of area \(= 0.667\) cm\(^2\)/s (3 s.f.)
Teaching script
(a)

The radius grows at nought point five centimetres per second at the start. Put t equals nought into the rate: the denominator becomes one, so k equals nought point five.

\[ \frac{k}{2(0)+1} = 0.5 \;\Rightarrow\; k = 0.5 \]

Watch for: writing only "k = 0.5" with no substitution shown. It's a show-that, so the working is the answer.

(b)

Integrate the rate to get r. It's one over a linear expression, so out comes a natural log divided by the coefficient of t, which is two. A half divided by two is a quarter.

\[ r = \tfrac{1}{4}\ln(2t+1) + c \]

Now use the starting radius, one centimetre at t equals nought. Log of one is zero, so c is one.

\[ r = \tfrac{1}{4}\ln(2t+1) + 1 \]

Watch for: leaving out the constant of integration. Without it you can't use the starting radius at all.

(c)

Chain the rates together: dA by dt is dA by dr times dr by dt. Area is pi r squared, so dA by dr is two pi r.

At t equals three, r is a quarter log seven plus one, about one point four eight six five. The rate dr by dt is nought point five over seven, which is one fourteenth.

\[ \frac{dA}{dt} = 2\pi(1.4865) \times \tfrac{1}{14} = 0.667 \text{ cm}^2/\text{s} \]

Watch for: using r equals one. That's the radius at the start, not at t equals three.

Model solution
\[ A = \pi r^2 \;\Rightarrow\; \frac{dA}{dr} = 2\pi r \] \[ \begin{aligned} \frac{dA}{dt} &= \frac{dA}{dr} \times \frac{dr}{dt} \\ 250 &= 2\pi(40) \times \frac{dr}{dt} \\ \frac{dr}{dt} &= \frac{250}{80\pi} = \frac{25}{8\pi} = 0.9947\ldots \end{aligned} \]

Teaching script

This is connected rates of change. You know how fast the area is growing, and you want how fast the radius is growing. The chain rule joins them up: dA by dt equals dA by dr times dr by dt.

The oil patch is a circle, so A is pi r squared, and dA by dr is two pi r.

\[ \frac{dA}{dt} = \frac{dA}{dr} \times \frac{dr}{dt} \]

At eight o'clock the radius is forty metres and dA by dt is two hundred and fifty. Put those in: two hundred and fifty equals two pi times forty, times dr by dt.

\[ 250 = 80\pi \times \frac{dr}{dt} \;\Rightarrow\; \frac{dr}{dt} = \frac{25}{8\pi} = 0.9947\ldots \]

So the radius is growing at zero point nine nine five metres per hour, to three significant figures. A bit under a metre an hour.

181/2162025 P1 Q11Whole question
Model solution
(a)

By Pythagoras' theorem, \(AB = \sqrt{x^2 + 9} = (x^2+9)^{\frac{1}{2}}\).

\[ \frac{d(AB)}{dx} = \tfrac{1}{2}(x^2+9)^{-\frac{1}{2}}(2x) = \frac{x}{\sqrt{x^2+9}} \] \[ x = 4:\quad \frac{d(AB)}{dx} = \frac{4}{\sqrt{25}} = \frac{4}{5} \] \[ \frac{d(AB)}{dt} = \frac{d(AB)}{dx} \times \frac{dx}{dt} = \frac{4}{5} \times 2 = 1.6 \]
\(AB\) is increasing at \(1.6\) units/s
(b)

Area of square \(S = AB^2 = x^2 + 9\).

\[ \frac{dS}{dt} = \frac{dS}{dx} \times \frac{dx}{dt} = 2x \times 2 = 4x \] \[ 4x \le 80 \;\Rightarrow\; x \le 20 \]
Maximum \(x = 20\)
Teaching script
(a)

This is a rates of change question. You know how fast x is changing and you want how fast AB is changing, so first get AB in terms of x. Pythagoras gives AB as root of x squared plus nine.

\[ AB = (x^2+9)^{\frac{1}{2}},\qquad \frac{d(AB)}{dx} = \frac{x}{\sqrt{x^2+9}} \]

At x equals four, root twenty-five is five, so the derivative is four fifths. Then chain the rates together: d AB by d t equals d AB by d x times d x by d t.

\[ \frac{d(AB)}{dt} = \frac{4}{5} \times 2 = 1.6 \]

AB is increasing at one point six units per second.

Watch for: putting x equals four in before differentiating. Differentiate first, substitute second. Do it the other way round and you're differentiating a constant, which gets you nowhere.

(b)

The square has side AB, so its area is AB squared, which is x squared plus nine. Nice, the square root has gone.

\[ \frac{dS}{dt} = 2x \times 2 = 4x \]

The area can't grow faster than eighty square units per second. So four x is at most eighty, and x is at most twenty. The largest x is twenty.

Watch for: forgetting to multiply by d x by d t. That gives two x at most eighty, and the wrong answer, forty.

182/2162019 P2 Q11Parts (iii)(iv)Syllabus 4047 paper
Model solution for parts (iii)(iv)
(iii)
\[ \begin{aligned} A &= \tfrac{1}{2}(KN + LM) \times KL \\ &= \tfrac{1}{2}(5 + 12\cos\theta + 5)(12\sin\theta) \\ &= 60\sin\theta + 72\sin\theta\cos\theta \\ &= 60\sin\theta + 36(2\sin\theta\cos\theta) \\ &= 60\sin\theta + 36\sin 2\theta \end{aligned} \]
(iv)
\[ \frac{dA}{d\theta} = 60\cos\theta + 72\cos 2\theta \] \[ \begin{aligned} 60\cos\theta + 72(2\cos^2\theta - 1) &= 0 \\ 144\cos^2\theta + 60\cos\theta - 72 &= 0 \\ 12\cos^2\theta + 5\cos\theta - 6 &= 0 \\ \cos\theta &= \frac{-5 \pm \sqrt{25 + 288}}{24} \end{aligned} \]

Since \(\theta\) is acute, \(\cos\theta \gt 0\):

\[ \cos\theta = \frac{-5 + \sqrt{313}}{24} = 0.52883 \;\Rightarrow\; \theta = 1.0136 \] \[ \frac{d^2A}{d\theta^2} = -60\sin\theta - 144\sin 2\theta \lt 0 \text{ for acute } \theta, \text{ so } A \text{ is a maximum.} \]
\(\theta = 1.01\) rad (3 s.f.)
Teaching script for parts (iii)(iv)
(iii)

Area of a trapezium is half the sum of the parallel sides times the gap between them. The parallel sides are KN and LM, and the height is KL.

\[ A = \tfrac12(5 + 12\cos\theta + 5)(12\sin\theta) = 60\sin\theta + 72\sin\theta\cos\theta \]

The target has sin two theta in it. Write seventy-two as thirty-six times two and use the double angle formula, two sin theta cos theta equals sin two theta.

\[ A = 60\sin\theta + 36\sin2\theta \]

Watch for: taking KN plus LM as twelve cos theta plus five. KN already has its own five, so the bracket holds two fives.

(iv)

For a maximum, differentiate A with respect to theta and set it to zero.

\[ \frac{dA}{d\theta} = 60\cos\theta + 72\cos2\theta \]

You can't solve that with mixed angles. Swap cos two theta for two cos squared theta minus one so everything is in cos theta, then divide through by twelve.

\[ 12\cos^2\theta + 5\cos\theta - 6 = 0 \]

Quadratic formula time. Theta is acute, so cos theta is positive and you take the plus root.

\[ \cos\theta = \frac{-5 + \sqrt{313}}{24} = 0.52883 \;\Rightarrow\; \theta = 1.0136 \]

Check the nature: the second derivative is minus sixty sin theta minus one forty-four sin two theta, which is negative for an acute angle. So it's a maximum, at theta equals one point nought one radians.

Watch for: having the calculator in degrees. The answer is in radians, and 1.01 degrees would be a very flat trapezium.

183/2162021 P1 Q8Whole question
Model solution
(a)

Subtract the three unplanted right-angled triangles from the rectangle. \(PD = 50 - x\) and \(CQ = 80 - 2x\).

\[ \begin{aligned} \text{Area of } \triangle ABP &= \tfrac12(80)(x) = 40x \\ \text{Area of } \triangle PDQ &= \tfrac12(50-x)(2x) = 50x - x^2 \\ \text{Area of } \triangle BCQ &= \tfrac12(50)(80-2x) = 2000 - 50x \\ A &= 4000 - 40x - (50x - x^2) - (2000 - 50x) \\ &= x^2 - 40x + 2000 \end{aligned} \]
\(A = x^2 - 40x + 2000\) (shown)
(b)
\[ \frac{dA}{dx} = 2x - 40 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x = 20 \] \[ A = 20^2 - 40(20) + 2000 = 1600 \] \[ \frac{d^2A}{dx^2} = 2 \gt 0, \text{ so } A \text{ is a minimum.} \]
Stationary value \(A = 1600\) m², which is a minimum.
Teaching script
(a)

This one asks you to show an area formula, and measuring the planted bit directly is a pain. So go the other way round: start with the whole rectangle and take away the three right-angled triangles that aren't planted.

First we need the missing sides. PD is fifty minus x, and CQ is eighty minus two x.

\[ PD = 50 - x, \qquad CQ = 80 - 2x \]

Each triangle is half base times height. Triangle ABP is a half of eighty times x, so forty x. Triangle PDQ is a half of fifty minus x, times two x, which comes to fifty x minus x squared. Triangle BCQ is a half of fifty times eighty minus two x, so two thousand minus fifty x.

\[ 40x, \qquad 50x - x^2, \qquad 2000 - 50x \]

The rectangle is four thousand. Now take away all three, and put brackets round each one so the signs behave themselves.

\[ A = 4000 - 40x - (50x - x^2) - (2000 - 50x) = x^2 - 40x + 2000 \]

And that's exactly the expression they gave you, so we're done with this part.

Watch for: dropping the brackets when you subtract \(50x - x^2\). If you do, the \(x^2\) term comes out negative and the whole thing falls apart.

(b)

Stationary value means the first derivative is zero. Differentiate A with respect to x and you get two x minus forty. Set that to zero and x is twenty.

\[ \frac{dA}{dx} = 2x - 40 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x = 20 \]

Put that back into A: four hundred, minus eight hundred, plus two thousand. That's one thousand six hundred.

\[ A = 20^2 - 40(20) + 2000 = 1600 \]

Is it a max or a min? Check the second derivative. It's two, which is positive, so it's a minimum.

\[ \frac{d^2A}{dx^2} = 2 \gt 0 \]

So the stationary value of A is sixteen hundred square metres, and it's a minimum.

Watch for: stopping at \(x = 20\). They want the stationary value of \(A\) itself, and whether it's a max or min.

Model solution
(a)

Volume: \(3x \cdot x \cdot h = 2000\), so \(h = \dfrac{2000}{3x^2}\).

\[ \begin{aligned} A &= 2(3x)(x) + 2(x)(h) + 2(3x)(h) = 6x^2 + 8xh \\ &= 6x^2 + 8x\cdot\frac{2000}{3x^2} = 6x^2 + \frac{16000}{3x}. \end{aligned} \]
Total surface area \(= 6x^2 + \dfrac{16000}{3x}\) (shown)
(b)
\[ \frac{dA}{dx} = 12x - \frac{16000}{3x^2}. \]

For a stationary value, \(\dfrac{dA}{dx} = 0\):

\[ 12x = \frac{16000}{3x^2} \quad\Rightarrow\quad x^3 = \frac{16000}{36} = \frac{4000}{9} \quad\Rightarrow\quad x = \sqrt[3]{\frac{4000}{9}}. \]
\(x = 7.63\) (3 s.f.)
(c)
\[ \frac{d^2A}{dx^2} = 12 + \frac{32000}{3x^3}. \]

At \(x = 7.63\), \(\dfrac{d^2A}{dx^2} = 12 + \dfrac{32000}{3}\cdot\dfrac{9}{4000} = 36 \gt 0\).

The total surface area is a minimum.
Teaching script
(a)

The box measures three x by x by h, and the volume is two thousand. Get h in terms of x first, because the final answer is only allowed to have x in it.

\[ 3x \cdot x \cdot h = 2000 \;\Rightarrow\; h = \frac{2000}{3x^2} \]

There are three pairs of faces: three x by x, x by h, and three x by h. Add the three, double the lot, and swap h for what you found.

\[ A = 6x^2 + 8xh = 6x^2 + 8x\cdot\frac{2000}{3x^2} = 6x^2 + \frac{16000}{3x} \]

Watch for: counting each face once instead of in pairs. That halves the area, and every answer after it goes wrong.

(b)

Write the second term as sixteen thousand over three, times x to the minus one. Now it's a power you can differentiate without fuss.

\[ \frac{dA}{dx} = 12x - \frac{16000}{3x^2} \]

Set the derivative to zero. Multiply through by three x squared and you're left with thirty-six x cubed equals sixteen thousand.

\[ x^3 = \frac{4000}{9} \;\Rightarrow\; x = \sqrt[3]{\tfrac{4000}{9}} = 7.63 \]

Give x to three significant figures: seven point six three.

Watch for: dropping the minus sign when you differentiate x to the minus one.

(c)

Now the second derivative test. Differentiating minus sixteen thousand over three x squared gives plus thirty-two thousand over three x cubed.

\[ \frac{d^2A}{dx^2} = 12 + \frac{32000}{3x^3} \]

At the stationary point, x cubed is four thousand over nine, so that fraction comes out to exactly twenty-four, and the second derivative is thirty-six. It's positive, so you've got a minimum.

Watch for: writing "minimum" without the value or the sign of the second derivative. The examiner wants to see why.

(a)

\(BX = 15 - x\). By Pythagoras' theorem in triangle \(BXC\):

\[ XC = \sqrt{8^2 + (15-x)^2} = \sqrt{x^2 - 30x + 289} \]

Time = distance \(\div\) speed:

\[ T = \frac{x}{5} + \frac{\sqrt{x^2 - 30x + 289}}{3} \quad \text{(shown)} \]
(b)
\[ \frac{dT}{dx} = \frac15 + \frac{1}{3}\cdot\frac{2x - 30}{2\sqrt{x^2 - 30x + 289}} = \frac15 + \frac{x - 15}{3\sqrt{x^2 - 30x + 289}} \]

For minimum time, \(\dfrac{dT}{dx} = 0\):

\[ \begin{aligned} \frac{15 - x}{3\sqrt{x^2 - 30x + 289}} &= \frac15 \\ 5(15 - x) &= 3\sqrt{x^2 - 30x + 289} \\ 25(x^2 - 30x + 225) &= 9(x^2 - 30x + 289) \\ 16x^2 - 480x + 3024 &= 0 \\ x^2 - 30x + 189 &= 0 \quad \text{(shown)} \end{aligned} \]
\(\dfrac{dT}{dx} = \dfrac15 + \dfrac{x - 15}{3\sqrt{x^2 - 30x + 289}}\)
(c)
\[ (x - 9)(x - 21) = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x = 9 \ \text{ or } \ x = 21 \]

\(x = 21\) is rejected since \(X\) lies between \(A\) and \(B\) (\(0 \lt x \lt 15\)).

\[ T = \frac95 + \frac{\sqrt{81 - 270 + 289}}{3} = \frac95 + \frac{10}{3} = \frac{77}{15}\ \text{h} = 5\tfrac{2}{15}\ \text{h} \]

\(\tfrac{2}{15}\) h \(= 8\) minutes.

Minimum time \(= 5\) hours 8 minutes
Teaching script
(a)

AX is x, so BX is fifteen minus x. Triangle BXC has its right angle at B, so XC comes straight from Pythagoras' theorem: the square root of eight squared plus fifteen minus x, all squared.

\[ XC = \sqrt{64 + (15-x)^2} = \sqrt{x^2 - 30x + 289} \]

Time is distance over speed. On the road it's x over five hours; across the rough ground it's XC over three. Add the two and that's T.

\[ T = \frac{x}{5} + \frac{\sqrt{x^2 - 30x + 289}}{3} \]

Watch for: dividing the rough-ground distance by five. Rough ground is the slow bit, so it's three.

(b)

The square root needs the chain rule: one over two root of the inside, times the derivative of the inside, which is two x minus thirty.

\[ \frac{dT}{dx} = \frac15 + \frac{x - 15}{3\sqrt{x^2 - 30x + 289}} \]

For the minimum, set dT by dx to zero. Move the fraction to the other side so both sides are positive, then cross-multiply: five times fifteen minus x equals three times the root.

Now square both sides. Twenty-five times x squared minus thirty x plus two hundred and twenty-five equals nine times x squared minus thirty x plus two hundred and eighty-nine. Collect everything up and you get sixteen x squared minus four hundred and eighty x plus three thousand and twenty-four equals zero.

\[ 16x^2 - 480x + 3024 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x^2 - 30x + 189 = 0 \]

Divide by sixteen and there's the equation they wanted.

Watch for: squaring only part of the left side. The whole of five times fifteen minus x gets squared.

(c)

The quadratic factorises into x minus nine, times x minus twenty-one. X has to be somewhere between A and B, so x is less than fifteen. Twenty-one would put you off the end of the road, so keep nine.

\[ T = \frac95 + \frac{\sqrt{100}}{3} = \frac95 + \frac{10}{3} = \frac{77}{15}\ \text{h} \]

Under the root you get eighty-one minus two hundred and seventy plus two hundred and eighty-nine, which is a lovely one hundred. Seventy-seven fifteenths of an hour is five and two fifteenths hours, and two fifteenths of sixty minutes is eight minutes. So the shortest time is five hours eight minutes.

Watch for: leaving it as 5.13 hours. They asked for hours and minutes, so convert.

To check what kind it is, the second derivative is minus three x, and at x equals ten that's minus thirty. Negative means the curve is frowning, so it's a maximum.

187/2162020 P2 Q10Whole questionSyllabus 4047 paper
Model solution
(i)
\[ f'(x) = \int (48x^2 + 2e^{2x-1})\,dx = 16x^3 + e^{2x-1} + c \]

The normal at the point is the vertical line \(x = \tfrac12\), so the tangent there is horizontal: \(f'\!\left(\tfrac12\right) = 0\).

\[ 16\left(\tfrac12\right)^3 + e^{0} + c = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; 2 + 1 + c = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; c = -3 \]
\(f'(x) = 16x^3 + e^{2x-1} - 3\)
(ii)
\[ f(x) = \int (16x^3 + e^{2x-1} - 3)\,dx = 4x^4 + \tfrac12 e^{2x-1} - 3x + d \]

The curve passes through \(\left(\tfrac12, \tfrac14\right)\).

\[ 4\left(\tfrac{1}{16}\right) + \tfrac12 - \tfrac32 + d = \tfrac14 \;\Rightarrow\; d = 1 \]
\(f(x) = 4x^4 + \tfrac12 e^{2x-1} - 3x + 1\)
(iii)

At \(x = 0\): \(y = \tfrac12 e^{-1} + 1 = \dfrac{1}{2e} + 1\), and gradient \(f'(0) = e^{-1} - 3 = \dfrac1e - 3\).

\[ \begin{aligned} y - \left(1 + \frac{1}{2e}\right) &= \left(\frac1e - 3\right)x \\ y + 3x - 1 &= \frac{x}{e} + \frac{1}{2e} \\ 2e(y + 3x - 1) &= 2x + 1 \end{aligned} \]
\(2e(y + 3x - 1) = 2x + 1\)  (shown)
Teaching script
(i)

We're given f double dash, so integrate once to get f dash. Forty-eight x squared becomes sixteen x cubed. For e to the two x minus one, divide by the coefficient of x, which is two, so two e to the two x minus one becomes e to the two x minus one.

\[ f'(x) = 16x^3 + e^{2x-1} + c \]

Now we need c, and the clue is the normal. The normal at that point is the vertical line x equals a half. If the normal is vertical, the tangent is flat, so the gradient there is zero.

\[ 16\left(\tfrac12\right)^3 + e^{0} + c = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; c = -3 \]

Watch for: forgetting the plus c, or setting the gradient equal to a half. A vertical normal means a gradient of zero.

(ii)

Integrate again. Sixteen x cubed gives four x to the four, e to the two x minus one gives a half e to the two x minus one, and minus three gives minus three x. And a brand new constant, d, turns up.

\[ f(x) = 4x^4 + \tfrac12 e^{2x-1} - 3x + d \]

The curve passes through a half, a quarter. Substitute it in: a quarter plus a half minus one and a half plus d equals a quarter. That makes d equal to one.

\[ f(x) = 4x^4 + \tfrac12 e^{2x-1} - 3x + 1 \]

Watch for: recycling c from part (i). Every integration brings its own constant.

(iii)

For a tangent you need two things: a point and a gradient. The y-coordinate is f of zero, which is a half e to the minus one plus one. The gradient is f dash of zero, which is one over e minus three.

\[ y = 1 + \frac{1}{2e}, \qquad f'(0) = \frac1e - 3 \]

Put those into y minus y one equals m x, then tidy up. Bring the three x and the one across, and multiply everything by two e to clear the fractions. That lands you in the form they asked for.

\[ 2e(y + 3x - 1) = 2x + 1 \]

Watch for: taking the gradient from f double dash. The tangent's gradient comes from f dash.

\[ y - 4 = -\tfrac83(x - 4) \]

At \(Q\), \(y = 0\): \(-4 = -\tfrac83(x - 4) \Rightarrow x - 4 = \tfrac32\).

Teaching script for parts (a)
(a)

For the normal, we need the gradient of the curve at P. Differentiate root of four plus three x using the chain rule: a half, times the bracket to the minus a half, times three.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{3}{2\sqrt{4 + 3x}} \]

At x equals four the root is four, so the gradient is three eighths. The normal's gradient is the negative reciprocal, minus eight thirds. Write the normal through four, four.

\[ y - 4 = -\tfrac83(x - 4) \]

Q is on the x-axis, so put y equal to zero. x minus four is three halves, so Q is five and a half, nought.

Watch for: using 3/8 as the normal's gradient. That's the tangent. The normal is perpendicular to it.

\[ \frac{2}{(x+1)^2} = \frac12 \;\Rightarrow\; (x+1)^2 = 4 \;\Rightarrow\; x + 1 = 2 \quad (x \gt -1) \;\Rightarrow\; x = 1 \] \[ y = \frac{4(1) + 2}{1 + 1} = 3, \qquad P(1, 3) \]

Gradient of normal \(= -2\):

\[ y - 3 = -2(x - 1) \;\Rightarrow\; y = -2x + 5 \]

\(A\): \(y = 0 \Rightarrow x = \tfrac52\). \(B\): \(x = 0 \Rightarrow y = 5\).
