Teaching script for parts (b)
(b)

Now set the gradient equal to a half.

\[ \frac{2}{(x+1)^2} = \frac12 \;\Rightarrow\; (x+1)^2 = 4 \]

Since x is greater than minus one, x plus one is two, so x is one. Then y is six over two, which is three. P is one, three.

The normal is perpendicular to the tangent, so its gradient is minus two.

\[ y - 3 = -2(x - 1) \;\Rightarrow\; y = -2x + 5 \]

It crosses the x-axis at five over two and the y-axis at five. Triangle AOB has area a half times five over two times five, which is twenty-five over four, or six point two five square units.

\[ \tfrac12 \times \tfrac52 \times 5 = 6.25 \]

Watch for: using the tangent gradient \(\frac12\) for the normal.

C1 Differentiation and integration › Tangents and normals
190/2162022 P2 Q10Parts (a)
Model solution for parts (a)
(a)

\(y = 9(3-2x)^{-1}\), so

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = -9(3-2x)^{-2}(-2) = \frac{18}{(3-2x)^2}. \]

At \(P(-3, 1)\): \(\dfrac{dy}{dx} = \dfrac{18}{9^2} = \dfrac{2}{9}\).

Equation of tangent:

\[ y - 1 = \frac{2}{9}(x+3) \quad\Rightarrow\quad y = \frac{2}{9}x + \frac{5}{3}. \]

At \(B\), \(x = 0\): \(B\left(0, \tfrac{5}{3}\right)\). At \(A\), \(x = 0\): \(y = \tfrac{9}{3} = 3\), so \(A(0, 3)\).

\[ OB = \frac{5}{3}, \qquad AB = 3 - \frac{5}{3} = \frac{4}{3}. \]

Since \(\tfrac{4}{3} \lt \tfrac{5}{3}\), \(AB \lt OB\).

\(B\) is nearer to \(A\).
Teaching script for parts (a)
(a)

Two points matter here, both on the y-axis: A, where the curve cuts it, and B, where the tangent at P cuts it. Once you have both, you compare AB with OB. Before differentiating, rewrite the curve as nine times three minus two x, to the power minus one. That way the chain rule does the work for you.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = -9(3-2x)^{-2}(-2) = \frac{18}{(3-2x)^2} \]

Now put x equals minus three into the derivative. The bracket becomes nine, nine squared is eighty-one, and eighteen over eighty-one tidies up to two-ninths. That's your tangent gradient at P.

\[ y - 1 = \tfrac{2}{9}(x+3) \;\Rightarrow\; y = \tfrac{2}{9}x + \tfrac{5}{3} \]

For B, set x to zero in the tangent equation, and you land at nought, five-thirds. For A, set x to zero in the curve: nine over three is three, so A sits at nought, three.

\[ OB = \tfrac{5}{3}, \qquad AB = 3 - \tfrac{5}{3} = \tfrac{4}{3} \]

Four-thirds is smaller than five-thirds, so B is closer to A than to O. Write that comparison out properly in a sentence. The conclusion is what the examiner is looking for.

Watch for: losing the minus two from the chain rule. You'd get a negative gradient, and B would end up below P, which should look wrong on the sketch.

At \(x = 0\), \(y = (0-1)^4 = 1\), so \(Q = (0, 1)\).

(a)

You need where Q and S sit on the y-axis, then compare QS with QO. Q is where the curve meets the y-axis, so put x equals zero: y is minus one to the fourth, which is one.

S comes from the normal at P. Differentiate first: dy by dx is four times x minus one, cubed. At x equals two that's four, so the normal gradient is minus a quarter.

At x equals zero, y is one plus a half, so S is at nought, three halves. That makes QS a half and QO one. So Q is closer to S.

192/2162025 P1 Q10Whole question
Model solution
(a)

At \(x = 0\): \(y = 2\sin 0 - 3\cos 0 = -3\), so \(P(0,\ -3)\).

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = 2a\cos ax + 6\sin 2x \] \[ x = 0:\quad \frac{dy}{dx} = 2a \]

Gradient of normal \(= -\dfrac{1}{2a}\).

\[ y - (-3) = -\frac{1}{2a}(x - 0) \]
\(y = -\dfrac{x}{2a} - 3\)
(b)

At \(Q\), \(y = 0\): \(0 = -\dfrac{x}{2a} - 3 \Rightarrow x = -6a\), so \(Q(-6a,\ 0)\).

\(OQ = 6a\) and \(OP = 3\).

\(a = 3\)
Teaching script
(a)

P is where the curve hits the y-axis, so put x equals zero. Sine nought is zero, cos nought is one, so y is negative three. P is nought, negative three.

For the normal you need the gradient at P, so differentiate. Two sine a x gives two a cos a x, and negative three cos two x gives positive six sine two x.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = 2a\cos ax + 6\sin 2x,\qquad x = 0:\ \frac{dy}{dx} = 2a \]

So the tangent gradient is two a, and the normal is the negative reciprocal, negative one over two a. Use that with the point P.

\[ y = -\frac{x}{2a} - 3 \]

Watch for: forgetting the chain-rule factor. Sine a x gives a cos a x, and cos two x gives negative two sine two x. That inner number always comes out to the front.

(b)

Q is where the normal meets the x-axis, so put y equals zero. You get x equals negative six a, so Q is negative six a, nought.

Triangle OPQ has its right angle at O. The base OQ is six a and the height OP is three.

\[ \tfrac{1}{2}(6a)(3) = 27 \;\Rightarrow\; 9a = 27 \]

So a is three.

Watch for: using negative six a as a length. Lengths aren't negative, so OQ is six a.

Relations between derivatives

3 questions
C1 Differentiation and integration › Relations between derivatives
193/2162019 P1 Q3Whole questionSyllabus 4047 paper
Model solution
\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = 2Ae^{2x} - Be^{-x} \] \[ \begin{aligned} \frac{dy}{dx} + 4y &= 2Ae^{2x} - Be^{-x} + 4Ae^{2x} + 4Be^{-x} \\ &= 6Ae^{2x} + 3Be^{-x} \end{aligned} \]

Comparing with \(e^{2x} - e^{-x}\):

\[ 6A = 1 \;\Rightarrow\; A = \tfrac{1}{6}, \qquad 3B = -1 \;\Rightarrow\; B = -\tfrac{1}{3} \]
\(A = \dfrac{1}{6},\ B = -\dfrac{1}{3}\)
Teaching script

You've got y as A e to the 2x plus B e to the minus x. Differentiate it, then build dy by dx plus four y and match it with the right-hand side.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = 2Ae^{2x} - Be^{-x} \]

Now add four y. Group the e to the 2x terms together and the e to the minus x terms together.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} + 4y = 6Ae^{2x} + 3Be^{-x} \]

This has to equal e to the 2x minus e to the minus x, so compare coefficients. Six A equals one, so A is one sixth. Three B equals minus one, so B is minus one third.

\[ A = \tfrac{1}{6},\qquad B = -\tfrac{1}{3} \]

Watch for: the sign when you differentiate e to the minus x. It gives minus B e to the minus x, and losing that minus changes B.

C1 Differentiation and integration › Relations between derivatives
194/2162023 P2 Q5Whole question
Model solution
(a)
\[ \begin{aligned} y &= 3\cos 3x - 5\sin 3x \\ \frac{dy}{dx} &= -9\sin 3x - 15\cos 3x \\ \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} &= -27\cos 3x + 45\sin 3x \end{aligned} \] \[ \begin{aligned} p\frac{d^2y}{dx^2} + q\frac{dy}{dx} + 14y + 34\sin 3x &= p(-27\cos 3x + 45\sin 3x) + q(-9\sin 3x - 15\cos 3x) \\ &\quad + 14(3\cos 3x - 5\sin 3x) + 34\sin 3x \\ &= (42 - 27p - 15q)\cos 3x + (45p - 9q - 36)\sin 3x \end{aligned} \]

Comparing coefficients of \(\cos 3x\) and \(\sin 3x\):

\(A = 42 - 27p - 15q\) (shown), \(\quad B = 45p - 9q - 36\)
(b)
\[ \begin{aligned} 27p + 15q &= 42 \;\Rightarrow\; 9p + 5q = 14 \quad (1) \\ 45p - 9q &= 36 \;\Rightarrow\; 5p - q = 4 \quad (2) \end{aligned} \]

From (2), \(q = 5p - 4\). Substituting into (1): \(9p + 25p - 20 = 14\), so \(34p = 34\).

\(p = 1,\ q = 1\)
Teaching script
(a)

They give you y, and then ask you to build something out of y, its first derivative and its second derivative. So before anything else, we differentiate twice and keep a close eye on the signs.

Cos 3x differentiates to minus 3 sin 3x, and sin 3x differentiates to 3 cos 3x. That makes dy by dx minus nine sin 3x minus fifteen cos 3x. Do it once more and the second derivative is minus twenty-seven cos 3x plus forty-five sin 3x.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = -9\sin 3x - 15\cos 3x, \qquad \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = -27\cos 3x + 45\sin 3x \]

Now drop all three into p times the second derivative, plus q times the first derivative, plus fourteen y, plus thirty-four sin 3x. Open up every bracket, then sort the terms into two piles: cos 3x in one, sin 3x in the other.

\[ (42 - 27p - 15q)\cos 3x + (45p - 9q - 36)\sin 3x \]

The cos pile has minus twenty-seven p, minus fifteen q, and fourteen times three, which is forty-two. That's exactly the A they told you to show, so you know you're on track. The sin pile has forty-five p, minus nine q, minus seventy from the fourteen y, and that extra plus thirty-four. So B is forty-five p minus nine q minus thirty-six.

Watch for: losing the factor of 3 from the chain rule each time you differentiate. And the plus thirty-four sin 3x tends to get left out when people collect the sin terms.

(b)

A and B both equal to zero gives you two linear equations in p and q. Set each one to zero and tidy it up.

\[ 9p + 5q = 14 \quad (1), \qquad 5p - q = 4 \quad (2) \]

The first one divides through by three, the second by nine. Equation two rearranges nicely to q equals five p minus four. Put that into equation one: nine p plus twenty-five p minus twenty equals fourteen, so thirty-four p equals thirty-four.

So p is one, and when you put it back, q is one too. Tidy answers like that are usually a good sign.

Watch for: sign slips when you rearrange B equals zero. The constant moves across as plus thirty-six.

195/2162024 P2 Q11Parts (c)
Model solution for parts (c)
(c)
\[ \begin{aligned} \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} &= -2e^{-2x}(\cos x - 2\sin x) + e^{-2x}(-\sin x - 2\cos x) \\ &= e^{-2x}(3\sin x - 4\cos x) \end{aligned} \] \[ \begin{aligned} \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} + 4\frac{dy}{dx} + 5y &= e^{-2x}\left[(3\sin x - 4\cos x) + 4(\cos x - 2\sin x) + 5\sin x\right] \\ &= e^{-2x}\left[(3 - 8 + 5)\sin x + (-4 + 4)\cos x\right] \\ &= 0 \quad \text{(shown)} \end{aligned} \]
\(\dfrac{d^2y}{dx^2} = e^{-2x}(3\sin x - 4\cos x)\)
Teaching script for parts (c)
(c)

Differentiate dy by dx once more, again with the product rule, on e to the minus two x times cos x minus two sin x.

\[ \frac{d^2y}{dx^2} = e^{-2x}(3\sin x - 4\cos x) \]

Now put in the second derivative, four times the first derivative and five times y. Every term has e to the minus two x in it, so take that out, then group the sin terms and the cos terms.

\[ e^{-2x}\left[(3 - 8 + 5)\sin x + (-4 + 4)\cos x\right] = 0 \]

The sin numbers add to zero and so do the cos numbers. Everything cancels, so the expression is zero, as required.

Watch for: the sign when you differentiate cos x. It's minus sin x.

Model solution
\[ \begin{aligned} \int\left(\frac{3}{x^2} + \frac{4}{3x - 5}\right)dx &= \int 3x^{-2}\,dx + 4\int\frac{1}{3x - 5}\,dx \\ &= \frac{3x^{-1}}{-1} + \frac43\ln(3x - 5) + c \end{aligned} \]
\(-\dfrac3x + \dfrac43\ln(3x - 5) + c\)
Teaching script

Split it into two integrals you already know how to do. Three over x squared is three x to the minus two. Four over three x minus five is one over a linear bracket, and those integrate to a log.

\[ \int 3x^{-2}\,dx + 4\int\frac{1}{3x - 5}\,dx \]

For three x to the minus two, add one to the power and divide by the new power, minus one. That gives minus three over x. For the log term, divide by three, the coefficient of x, so it becomes four thirds ln of three x minus five. Then add the constant.

\[ -\frac3x + \frac43\ln(3x - 5) + c \]

Watch for: writing 4 ln(3x − 5) and forgetting to divide by the 3 in front of x.

Finding y from dy/dx or d²y/dx²

6 questions
C1 Differentiation and integration › Finding y from dy/dx or d²y/dx²
197/2162019 P2 Q5Whole questionSyllabus 4047 paper
Model solution
\[ f'(x) = \int (3\cos 3x - 4\sin 2x)\,dx = \sin 3x + 2\cos 2x + c \] \[ f(x) = \int (\sin 3x + 2\cos 2x + c)\,dx = -\frac{1}{3}\cos 3x + \sin 2x + cx + d \] \[ f(0) = 0:\quad -\frac{1}{3} + 0 + 0 + d = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; d = \frac{1}{3} \] \[ \begin{aligned} f\left(\tfrac{\pi}{2}\right) = \tfrac{5}{6}:\quad -\tfrac{1}{3}\cos\tfrac{3\pi}{2} + \sin\pi + \tfrac{\pi}{2}c + \tfrac{1}{3} &= \tfrac{5}{6} \\ 0 + 0 + \tfrac{\pi}{2}c &= \tfrac{1}{2} \\ c &= \tfrac{1}{\pi} \end{aligned} \] \[ f(x) = -\frac{1}{3}\cos 3x + \sin 2x + \frac{x}{\pi} + \frac{1}{3} \] \[ \begin{aligned} f\left(\tfrac{\pi}{3}\right) &= -\tfrac{1}{3}\cos\pi + \sin\tfrac{2\pi}{3} + \tfrac{1}{3} + \tfrac{1}{3} \\ &= \tfrac{1}{3} + \tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2} + \tfrac{2}{3} \\ &= 1 + \tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \end{aligned} \]
Teaching script

You're given the second derivative, so integrate twice. Each integration brings its own constant: c the first time, d the second.

\[ f'(x) = \sin3x + 2\cos2x + c \]

Integrating three cos three x gives sin three x. Minus four sin two x gives plus two cos two x. Then integrate again.

\[ f(x) = -\tfrac13\cos3x + \sin2x + cx + d \]

Now use the two given points. f of nought equals nought gives minus a third plus d equals nought, so d is a third.

\[ d = \tfrac13 \]

f of pi over two equals five sixths. Cos three pi over two is zero and sin pi is zero, so pi over two times c equals a half.

\[ c = \tfrac1\pi, \qquad f(x) = -\tfrac13\cos3x + \sin2x + \tfrac{x}{\pi} + \tfrac13 \]

Finally put in pi over three. Cos pi is minus one and sin two pi over three is root three over two.

\[ f\left(\tfrac\pi3\right) = \tfrac13 + \tfrac{\sqrt3}{2} + \tfrac13 + \tfrac13 = 1 + \tfrac{\sqrt3}{2} \]

Watch for: using one constant for both integrations. You need c and d, or the two conditions can't both be satisfied.

Model solution
\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = \int (2e^{-x} + 6e^{2x})\,dx = -2e^{-x} + 3e^{2x} + c \]

At \(P\), \(x = 0\) and \(\dfrac{dy}{dx} = 3\):

\[ -2 + 3 + c = 3 \;\Rightarrow\; c = 2 \] \[ y = \int (-2e^{-x} + 3e^{2x} + 2)\,dx = 2e^{-x} + \tfrac32 e^{2x} + 2x + d \]

The curve passes through \((0, 5)\):

\[ 2 + \tfrac32 + d = 5 \;\Rightarrow\; d = \tfrac32 \]
\(y = 2e^{-x} + \tfrac32 e^{2x} + 2x + \tfrac32\)
Teaching script

You're given the second derivative, so integrate twice and find a constant each time.

First integration: two e to the minus x becomes minus two e to the minus x, and six e to the two x becomes three e to the two x. Add c.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = -2e^{-x} + 3e^{2x} + c \]

At P, x is nought and the gradient is three. e to the nought is one, so minus two plus three plus c equals three, which makes c two.

\[ c = 2 \]

Integrate again. Minus two e to the minus x becomes two e to the minus x. Three e to the two x becomes three halves e to the two x. Two becomes two x. Add d.

\[ y = 2e^{-x} + \tfrac32 e^{2x} + 2x + d \]

The curve goes through nought, five, so two plus three halves plus d equals five, and d is three halves.

\[ y = 2e^{-x} + \tfrac32 e^{2x} + 2x + \tfrac32 \]

Watch for: leaving out the first constant \(c\), or finding it with the point \((0, 5)\) instead of the gradient 3. Each constant has its own piece of information.

202/2162025 P2 Q3Whole question
Model solution
\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = e^x\left(3e^{2x} - e^{-4x}\right) - \cos 2x = 3e^{3x} - e^{-3x} - \cos 2x \] \[ \begin{aligned} y &= \int \left(3e^{3x} - e^{-3x} - \cos 2x\right) dx \\ &= e^{3x} + \tfrac{1}{3}e^{-3x} - \tfrac{1}{2}\sin 2x + c \end{aligned} \]

The curve passes through \((0,\ 0)\):

\[ 0 = 1 + \tfrac{1}{3} - 0 + c \;\Rightarrow\; c = -\tfrac{4}{3} \]
\(y = e^{3x} + \tfrac{1}{3}e^{-3x} - \tfrac{1}{2}\sin 2x - \tfrac{4}{3}\)
Teaching script

You've got d y by d x and a point on the curve, so integrate, then use the point to find the constant. Before integrating, multiply the e to the x into the bracket. Add the powers: e to the x times e to the two x is e to the three x, and e to the x times e to the minus four x is e to the minus three x.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = 3e^{3x} - e^{-3x} - \cos 2x \]

Integrate term by term, dividing by the number in front of x each time. Three e to the three x gives e to the three x. Minus e to the minus three x gives plus a third e to the minus three x. Minus cos two x gives minus a half sine two x.

\[ y = e^{3x} + \tfrac{1}{3}e^{-3x} - \tfrac{1}{2}\sin 2x + c \]

The curve goes through the origin. At x equals nought, both exponentials are one and sine is zero, so nought equals one plus a third plus c, which gives c equals negative four thirds.

\[ y = e^{3x} + \tfrac{1}{3}e^{-3x} - \tfrac{1}{2}\sin 2x - \tfrac{4}{3} \]

Watch for: the sign of the e to the minus three x term. Dividing by negative three turns the minus into a plus.

Model solution
\[ y = 8(3x+2)^{-1} \;\Rightarrow\; \frac{dy}{dx} = -24(3x+2)^{-2} \] \[ \text{At } P(2,1):\ \frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{24}{64} = -\frac{3}{8} \] \[ \text{Tangent: } y - 1 = -\tfrac{3}{8}(x - 2) \;\Rightarrow\; y = -\tfrac{3}{8}x + \tfrac{7}{4}, \qquad B\left(0, \tfrac{7}{4}\right) \] \[ \begin{aligned} \text{Area under curve from } 0 \text{ to } 2 &= \int_0^2 \frac{8}{3x+2}\,dx = \frac{8}{3}\Big[\ln(3x+2)\Big]_0^2 \\ &= \frac{8}{3}(\ln 8 - \ln 2) = \frac{8}{3}\ln 4 = \frac{16}{3}\ln 2 \end{aligned} \] \[ \text{Area of trapezium under } BP = \tfrac{1}{2}\left(\tfrac{7}{4} + 1\right)(2) = \tfrac{11}{4} \] \[ \text{Shaded area} = \frac{16}{3}\ln 2 - \frac{11}{4} \]
\(\left(\dfrac{16}{3}\ln 2 - \dfrac{11}{4}\right)\) units\(^2\)
Teaching script

The shaded region is between the curve and the tangent at P. The plan: find the tangent, then take the area under the tangent away from the area under the curve.

Write the curve as eight times three x plus two to the minus one and differentiate with the chain rule.

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = -24(3x+2)^{-2}, \qquad \text{at } P(2,1):\ -\tfrac{24}{64} = -\tfrac38 \]

The tangent through two, one with gradient minus three eighths hits the y-axis at B, nought, seven quarters.

\[ y = -\tfrac38x + \tfrac74, \qquad B\left(0, \tfrac74\right) \]

Area under the curve from nought to two: eight over three x plus two integrates to eight thirds ln of three x plus two.

\[ \tfrac83(\ln8 - \ln2) = \tfrac83\ln4 = \tfrac{16}{3}\ln2 \]

Under the tangent it's a trapezium with parallel sides seven quarters and one, width two. That's eleven quarters.

\[ \text{Shaded area} = \tfrac{16}{3}\ln2 - \tfrac{11}{4} \]

204/2162020 P1 Q10Whole questionSyllabus 4047 paper
Model solution
(i)
\[ \cos 2x = -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, \qquad 0 \le 2x \le 2\pi \]

Basic angle \(\frac{\pi}{6}\); cosine is negative in the second and third quadrants.

\[ 2x = \pi - \frac{\pi}{6} = \frac{5\pi}{6} \quad\text{or}\quad 2x = \pi + \frac{\pi}{6} = \frac{7\pi}{6} \]
\(A:\ x = \dfrac{5\pi}{12}\); \(\quad B:\ x = \dfrac{7\pi}{12}\)
(ii)

Between \(A\) and \(B\) the line lies above the curve.

\[ \begin{aligned} \text{Area} &= \int_{5\pi/12}^{7\pi/12} \left(-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} - \cos 2x\right) dx \\ &= \left[-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}x - \frac{1}{2}\sin 2x\right]_{5\pi/12}^{7\pi/12} \\ &= -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\left(\frac{7\pi}{12} - \frac{5\pi}{12}\right) - \frac{1}{2}\left(\sin\frac{7\pi}{6} - \sin\frac{5\pi}{6}\right) \\ &= -\frac{\sqrt{3}\pi}{12} - \frac{1}{2}\left(-\frac{1}{2} - \frac{1}{2}\right) \\ &= \frac{1}{2} - \frac{\sqrt{3}\pi}{12} \end{aligned} \]
\(\left(\dfrac{1}{2} - \dfrac{\sqrt{3}\,\pi}{12}\right)\) units\(^2\)
Teaching script
(i)

A and B are where the curve y equals cos two x meets the line y equals minus root three over two. Set them equal.

\[ \cos2x = -\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}, \qquad 0 \le 2x \le 2\pi \]

Double the range, because you're solving for two x. The basic angle is pi over six. Cosine is negative in the second and third quadrants.

\[ 2x = \tfrac{5\pi}{6} \text{ or } \tfrac{7\pi}{6} \]

Halve each one. A is at x equals five pi over twelve and B at seven pi over twelve.

Watch for: halving the angles before you use the quadrants. Find all the values of 2x first, then divide by two.

(ii)

Between A and B the line is above the curve. So integrate top minus bottom: minus root three over two, minus cos two x.

\[ \int_{5\pi/12}^{7\pi/12}\left(-\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2} - \cos2x\right)dx = \left[-\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}x - \tfrac12\sin2x\right]_{5\pi/12}^{7\pi/12} \]

The first part gives minus root three over two times pi over six. For the sine part, sin seven pi over six is minus a half and sin five pi over six is a half. The difference is minus one, and minus a half of minus one is plus a half.

\[ = \tfrac12 - \tfrac{\sqrt3\,\pi}{12} \]

The area is a half minus root three pi over twelve square units.

Watch for: integrating curve minus line, which gives you the negative of the answer. Always top minus bottom. Areas don't come out negative.

205/2162021 P1 Q14Parts (b)
Model solution for parts (b)
(b)

Area = area under the curve from \(x = 0\) to \(x = 4\) + area of the triangle with vertices \((4, 0)\), \(P(4, 4)\) and \(Q\).

\[ \begin{aligned} \int_0^4 (4 + 3x)^{\frac12}\,dx &= \left[\frac{(4 + 3x)^{\frac32}}{\frac32 \times 3}\right]_0^4 = \tfrac29\left(16^{\frac32} - 4^{\frac32}\right) \\ &= \tfrac29(64 - 8) = \tfrac{112}{9} \end{aligned} \] \[ \text{Triangle} = \tfrac12 \times \tfrac32 \times 4 = 3 \] \[ \text{Area} = \tfrac{112}{9} + 3 = \tfrac{139}{9} \]

Teaching script for parts (b)
(b)

The shaded region is really two shapes, so split it at x equals four. On the left is the area under the curve from nought to four. On the right is a triangle under the normal, from four to Q.

For the curve, add one to the power and divide by the new power times three, the coefficient of x. That gives two ninths of the bracket to the three halves.

\[ \tfrac29\left(16^{\frac32} - 4^{\frac32}\right) = \tfrac29(64 - 8) = \tfrac{112}{9} \]

The triangle has base three halves and height four, so its area is three. Add them: one hundred and twelve ninths plus three is one hundred and thirty-nine ninths.

\[ \text{Area} = 15\tfrac49 \text{ units}^2 \]

Watch for: ignoring the lower limit. At x = 0 the bracket is 4, and 4^(3/2) = 8 still has to be subtracted.

C1 Differentiation and integration › Area bounded by a curve and line(s)
206/2162021 P2 Q10Whole question
Model solution

Coordinates of \(M\).

\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = 2\cos\frac{x}{2} - 1 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; \cos\frac{x}{2} = \frac12 \;\Rightarrow\; \frac{x}{2} = \frac{\pi}{3} \;\Rightarrow\; x = \frac{2\pi}{3} \]

\(\dfrac{d^2y}{dx^2} = -\sin\dfrac{x}{2} = -\dfrac{\sqrt3}{2} \lt 0\) at \(x = \dfrac{2\pi}{3}\), confirming a maximum.

\[ y = 4\sin\frac{\pi}{3} - \frac{2\pi}{3} = 2\sqrt3 - \frac{2\pi}{3}, \qquad M\left(\frac{2\pi}{3},\; 2\sqrt3 - \frac{2\pi}{3}\right) \]

Area under the curve from \(O\) to \(M\).

\[ \begin{aligned} \int_0^{2\pi/3} \left(4\sin\frac{x}{2} - x\right) dx &= \left[-8\cos\frac{x}{2} - \frac{x^2}{2}\right]_0^{2\pi/3} \\ &= \left(-8\cdot\frac12 - \frac{2\pi^2}{9}\right) - (-8) \\ &= 4 - \frac{2\pi^2}{9} \end{aligned} \]

Area of the triangle under \(OM\).

\[ \frac12 \cdot \frac{2\pi}{3}\left(2\sqrt3 - \frac{2\pi}{3}\right) = \frac{2\pi\sqrt3}{3} - \frac{2\pi^2}{9} \]

Shaded area.

\[ \left(4 - \frac{2\pi^2}{9}\right) - \left(\frac{2\pi\sqrt3}{3} - \frac{2\pi^2}{9}\right) = 4 - \frac{2\pi\sqrt3}{3} \]
Shaded area \(= 4 - \dfrac{2\pi\sqrt3}{3}\) units² (shown)
Teaching script

A "show that" for a shaded area, so plan before you calculate. The shaded bit is the area under the curve from O to M, minus the triangle under the straight line OM. So we need M first, then two areas.

M is the maximum point, so differentiate and set it to zero. Four sine x over two differentiates to two cos x over two, and minus x gives minus one.

\[ 2\cos\frac{x}{2} - 1 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; \cos\frac{x}{2} = \frac12 \;\Rightarrow\; x = \frac{2\pi}{3} \]

The second derivative is minus sine x over two, which is negative there, so yes, it's a maximum. The y-coordinate is four sine pi over three minus two pi over three, which is two root three minus two pi over three.

\[ M\left(\frac{2\pi}{3},\; 2\sqrt3 - \frac{2\pi}{3}\right) \]

Next, integrate the curve from nought to two pi over three. Four sine x over two becomes minus eight cos x over two, and minus x becomes minus x squared over two.

\[ \left[-8\cos\frac{x}{2} - \frac{x^2}{2}\right]_0^{2\pi/3} = 4 - \frac{2\pi^2}{9} \]

The triangle is half base times height. That's a half of two pi over three, times the y-coordinate of M, which gives two pi root three over three minus two pi squared over nine.

\[ \frac{2\pi\sqrt3}{3} - \frac{2\pi^2}{9} \]

Now subtract. The pi squared terms cancel each other out, and you're left with four minus two pi root three over three. That's the result they wanted.

\[ 4 - \frac{2\pi\sqrt3}{3} \]

Watch for: integrating \(\sin\frac{x}{2}\) as \(-\frac12\cos\frac{x}{2}\). You divide by the coefficient, a half, which doubles it. So it's \(-2\cos\frac{x}{2}\).

Model solution for parts (b)
(b)

Area under the curve from \(x=-3\) to \(x=0\):

\[ \begin{aligned} \int_{-3}^{0} \frac{9}{3-2x}\,dx &= \left[-\frac{9}{2}\ln(3-2x)\right]_{-3}^{0} \\ &= -\frac{9}{2}\ln 3 + \frac{9}{2}\ln 9 \\ &= -\frac{9}{2}\ln 3 + 9\ln 3 = \frac{9}{2}\ln 3. \end{aligned} \]

Area under the tangent from \(x=-3\) to \(x=0\) (trapezium with parallel sides 1 and \(\tfrac{5}{3}\), width 3):

\[ \frac{1}{2}\left(1 + \frac{5}{3}\right)(3) = 4. \] \[ \text{Shaded area} = \frac{9}{2}\ln 3 - 4. \]
\(a = \dfrac{9}{2}\), \(b = -4\)
Teaching script for parts (b)
(b)

The shaded bit is the area under the curve take away the area under the tangent, both from x equals minus three to nought. The curve part is one over a linear expression, so a natural log comes out, and you divide by the x coefficient, minus two.

\[ \int_{-3}^{0} \frac{9}{3-2x}\,dx = \left[-\tfrac{9}{2}\ln(3-2x)\right]_{-3}^{0} = -\tfrac{9}{2}\ln 3 + \tfrac{9}{2}\ln 9 = \tfrac{9}{2}\ln 3 \]

Use log nine equals two log three to pull the logs together. For the tangent, don't bother integrating at all. The region under it is a trapezium with parallel sides one and five-thirds and width three, so the trapezium formula gets you there faster.

\[ \tfrac{1}{2}\left(1 + \tfrac{5}{3}\right)(3) = 4 \qquad \text{Shaded} = \tfrac{9}{2}\ln 3 - 4 \]

So a comes out as nine over two, and b is minus four.

Watch for: putting plus nine-halves in front of the log. You divided by minus two, so it has to be negative.

Model solution for parts (b)
(b)

Tangent at \(P\): \(y - 1 = 4(x - 2)\), i.e. \(y = 4x - 7\). At \(y = 0\), \(x = \tfrac74\), so \(R = \left(\tfrac74, 0\right)\).

The curve touches the \(x\)-axis at \((1, 0)\). Shaded area = area under curve from \(x = 1\) to \(x = 2\) minus area of triangle with vertices \(R\), \((2, 0)\) and \(P\).

\[ \int_1^2 (x-1)^4\,dx = \left[ \frac{(x-1)^5}{5} \right]_1^2 = \frac15 \] \[ \text{Triangle} = \tfrac12 \times \left(2 - \tfrac74\right) \times 1 = \tfrac18 \] \[ \text{Shaded area} = \frac15 - \frac18 = \frac{3}{40} \]

(b)

Start by finding R. The tangent at P has gradient four, so it's y equals four x minus seven. Put y equals zero and x is seven quarters.

The shaded bit is the area under the curve from x equals one, where the curve touches the axis, up to x equals two. Then take away the triangle under the tangent, from R to P.

\[ \int_1^2 (x-1)^4\,dx = \left[ \frac{(x-1)^5}{5} \right]_1^2 = \frac15 \]

The triangle's base is two minus seven quarters, which is a quarter, and its height is one. So its area is one eighth.

\[ \frac15 - \frac18 = \frac{3}{40} \]

The shaded area is three fortieths of a square unit. Small, but it's all yours.

209/2162025 P2 Q9Whole question
Model solution
(a)

\(P\) lies on both graphs and has \(x\)-coordinate \(\alpha\):

\[ k\alpha = \alpha(10 - \alpha) \;\Rightarrow\; k = 10 - \alpha \quad (\alpha \ne 0) \] \[ \begin{aligned} R_1 &= \int_0^{\alpha} \left[x(10 - x) - (10 - \alpha)x\right] dx \\ &= \int_0^{\alpha} \left(\alpha x - x^2\right) dx \\ &= \left[\frac{\alpha x^2}{2} - \frac{x^3}{3}\right]_0^{\alpha} \\ &= \frac{\alpha^3}{2} - \frac{\alpha^3}{3} = \frac{\alpha^3}{6} \end{aligned} \]
\(R_1 = \dfrac{\alpha^3}{6}\) (shown)
(b)

\(R_2\) is a right-angled triangle with base \(\alpha\) and height \(k\alpha = \alpha(10 - \alpha)\):

\[ R_2 = \tfrac{1}{2}\alpha \cdot \alpha(10 - \alpha) = \tfrac{1}{2}\alpha^2(10 - \alpha) \] \[ \begin{aligned} \frac{\alpha^3}{6} &= \frac{\alpha^2(10 - \alpha)}{2} \\ \alpha &= 3(10 - \alpha) \\ \alpha &= 7.5 \end{aligned} \] \[ k = 10 - 7.5 \]
\(k = 2.5\)
Teaching script
(a)

P is on both the line y equals k x and the curve y equals x times ten minus x, at x equals alpha. Set the y-values equal and divide by alpha, which isn't zero. That links k and alpha: k is ten minus alpha.

\[ k = 10 - \alpha \]

R one is the region between the curve and the line from nought to alpha. The curve is on top, so integrate curve minus line. The ten x terms cancel, leaving alpha x minus x squared.

\[ R_1 = \int_0^{\alpha} \left(\alpha x - x^2\right) dx = \left[\frac{\alpha x^2}{2} - \frac{x^3}{3}\right]_0^{\alpha} \]

At alpha that's alpha cubed over two minus alpha cubed over three, which is alpha cubed over six.

Watch for: integrating with k still floating around. Swap k for ten minus alpha first, so the answer comes out in alpha only.

(b)

R two is the triangle under the line, with base alpha and height k alpha. Using k equals ten minus alpha, its area is half alpha squared times ten minus alpha. Set the two areas equal.

\[ \frac{\alpha^3}{6} = \frac{\alpha^2(10 - \alpha)}{2} \;\Rightarrow\; \alpha = 3(10 - \alpha) \]

Divide by alpha squared and times by six. Four alpha is thirty, so alpha is seven point five. Then k is ten minus seven point five, which is two point five.

Watch for: handing in alpha as the answer. They asked for k.

Kinematics

C1 Differentiation and integration › Kinematics
210/2162019 P1 Q11Whole questionSyllabus 4047 paper
Model solution
(i)
\[ \begin{aligned} v = \frac{ds}{dt} &= 3t^2-12t+9 \\ \text{At rest: } 3t^2-12t+9 &= 0 \\ 3(t-1)(t-3) &= 0 \end{aligned} \]
\(t = 1\) and \(t = 3\)
(ii)
\[ \begin{aligned} a = \frac{dv}{dt} &= 6t-12 \\ \text{When } t=1,\ a &= 6(1)-12 = -6 \end{aligned} \]
Acceleration \(= -6\ \text{cm/s}^2\)
(iii)

The dot comes to instantaneous rest and reverses direction at \(t = 1\) and at \(t = 3\), both within the interval. The value of \(s\) at \(t = 4\) gives only the displacement from \(O\), not the total distance moved in both directions.

The dot changes direction at \(t=1\) and \(t=3\), so \(s(4)\) is its displacement, not the distance travelled.
(iv)
\[ \begin{aligned} s(0) &= 0, \quad s(1) = 1-6+9 = 4 \\ s(3) &= 27-54+27 = 0, \quad s(4) = 64-96+36 = 4 \end{aligned} \] \[ \text{Total distance} = |4-0| + |0-4| + |4-0| = 4+4+4 = 12 \]
Total distance \(= 12\) cm
Teaching script
(i)

"At rest" means the velocity is zero. You've been given displacement, so differentiate once to get velocity, then set it to zero.

\[ v = \frac{ds}{dt} = 3t^2-12t+9 \]

Set it to zero and pull out the common factor of three before you factorise. Makes life easier.

\[ 3t^2-12t+9 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; 3(t-1)(t-3) = 0 \]

So the dot is at rest when t equals one and when t equals three seconds.

Watch for: setting s equal to zero instead of v. Zero displacement means the dot is back at O, not that it has stopped.

(ii)

Acceleration is the derivative of velocity, so differentiate your answer from part (i).

\[ a = \frac{dv}{dt} = 6t-12 \]

The first time it's at rest is t equals one. Put that in.

\[ a = 6(1)-12 = -6 \]

The acceleration is negative six centimetres per second squared. Keep the minus sign. It's telling you which way the dot is being pushed.

Watch for: substituting t equals three because it was the last number you wrote. Read which moment the question is asking about.

(iii)

This one wants an explanation, a sentence or two. The idea is that the dot turns round. From part (i), it stops and reverses at t equals one and t equals three, and both of those happen inside the first four seconds.

So s at t equals four only tells you where the dot ends up relative to O. It doesn't count the trip out and the trip back.

Write something like: the dot changes direction at t equals one and t equals three, so s of four is its displacement, not the distance travelled.

Watch for: something vague like "the dot moves backwards". Name the actual times it changes direction.

(iv)

For total distance, chop the journey up at every turning point. Work out s at the start, at each turning time, and at the end.

\[ s(0) = 0,\quad s(1) = 4,\quad s(3) = 0,\quad s(4) = 4 \]

Now add up the size of each stage. Nought to four is four centimetres out. Four back to nought is four back. Nought to four again is another four.

\[ |4-0| + |0-4| + |4-0| = 12 \]

Total distance: twelve centimetres.

Watch for: adding the signed values, which gives you four. Every stage counts as a positive length, even the ones going backwards.

211/2162020 P1 Q11Whole questionSyllabus 4047 paper
Model solution
(i)
\[ \text{At } A,\ t = 0:\ v = 15(0 + e^0) = 15 \]

Speed at \(B\) is twice the speed at \(A\).

Speed at \(B = 30\) m/s
(ii)
\[ \begin{aligned} 15\left(\tfrac{10}{20} + e^{10k}\right) &= 30 \\ e^{10k} &= 1.5 \\ k &= \tfrac{1}{10}\ln 1.5 \end{aligned} \] \[ \begin{aligned} s &= \int_0^{10} 15\left(\frac{t}{20} + e^{kt}\right) dt = 15\left[\frac{t^2}{40} + \frac{e^{kt}}{k}\right]_0^{10} \\ &= 15\left[\frac{100}{40} + \frac{e^{10k} - 1}{k}\right] = 15\left[2.5 + \frac{0.5 \times 10}{\ln 1.5}\right] \\ &= 37.5 + \frac{75}{\ln 1.5} = 222.47 \end{aligned} \]

Since \(v \gt 0\) throughout, the displacement equals the distance.

\(AB \approx 222\) m
(iii)
\[ a = \frac{dv}{dt} = 15\left(\frac{1}{20} + ke^{kt}\right) \] \[ t = 2:\quad a = 15\left(0.05 + 0.040547\,e^{0.081093}\right) = 15(0.05 + 0.043972) = 1.4096 \]
\(a \approx 1.41\) m/s\(^2\)
Teaching script
(i)

At A, t is zero. Put that in: t over twenty is zero and e to the nought is one.

\[ v = 15(0 + 1) = 15 \]

The speed at B is double that, so thirty metres per second.

Watch for: putting in t equals ten here. Part (i) needs the speed at A first.

(ii)

You need k first. At B, t is ten and v is thirty.

\[ 15\left(\tfrac{10}{20} + e^{10k}\right) = 30 \;\Rightarrow\; e^{10k} = 1.5 \;\Rightarrow\; k = \tfrac1{10}\ln1.5 \]

Distance is the integral of velocity from nought to ten. t over twenty integrates to t squared over forty, and e to the k t integrates to e to the k t over k.

\[ s = 15\left[\tfrac{t^2}{40} + \tfrac{e^{kt}}{k}\right]_0^{10} = 15\left[2.5 + \tfrac{e^{10k}-1}{k}\right] \]

e to the ten k minus one is nought point five. Dividing by k is the same as multiplying by ten over ln one point five.

\[ s = 37.5 + \frac{75}{\ln1.5} \approx 222 \]

Velocity is positive the whole way, so displacement equals distance. AB is about two hundred and twenty-two metres.

Watch for: dropping the minus one at the lower limit. e to the nought over k is not zero.

(iii)

Acceleration is the derivative of velocity. t over twenty gives one twentieth, and e to the k t gives k e to the k t.

\[ a = 15\left(\tfrac1{20} + ke^{kt}\right) \]

Put t equals two, with k as one tenth of ln one point five. Keep k unrounded in the calculator.

\[ a = 15(0.05 + 0.043972) \approx 1.41 \]

The acceleration is about one point four one metres per second squared.

Watch for: rounding k early. Use the stored value, or the third significant figure can wander off.

C1 Differentiation and integration › Kinematics
212/2162021 P2 Q8Whole question
Model solution
(a)

At \(t = 0\), \(V = 24e^0 = 24\). At \(B\), \(v = 12\):

\[ 24e^{-\frac t4} = 12 \;\Rightarrow\; e^{-\frac t4} = \frac12 \;\Rightarrow\; t = 4\ln 2 = 2.772\ldots \]
\(t = 4\ln 2 \approx 2.77\) s
(b)
\[ a = \frac{dv}{dt} = -6e^{-\frac t4} \]

At \(B\), \(e^{-\frac t4} = \dfrac12\):

\[ a = -6\left(\frac12\right) = -3 \]
Acceleration \(= -3\) m/s² (deceleration of 3 m/s²)
(c)

\(v \gt 0\) throughout, so distance \(=\) displacement.

\[ \begin{aligned} AB &= \int_0^{4\ln 2} 24e^{-\frac t4}\,dt = \left[-96e^{-\frac t4}\right]_0^{4\ln 2} \\ &= -96\left(\frac12\right) + 96 = 48 \end{aligned} \]
\(AB = 48\) m
Teaching script
(a)

At A, t is zero, so V is twenty-four. B is where the speed has halved, so set v equal to twelve.

\[ 24e^{-\frac t4} = 12 \;\Rightarrow\; e^{-\frac t4} = \frac12 \]

Take natural logs: minus t over four equals ln a half, so t is four ln two, about two point seven seven seconds.

\[ t = 4\ln 2 \approx 2.77 \text{ s} \]

Watch for: setting \(v = 12\) without first finding \(V\). Show \(V = 24\) from \(t = 0\) before you halve it.

(b)

Acceleration is d v by d t. Differentiating twenty-four e to the minus t over four brings down a minus a quarter, so you get minus six e to the minus t over four.

\[ a = -6e^{-\frac t4} \]

At B you already know e to the minus t over four is a half, so a is minus three.

\[ a = -3 \text{ m/s}^2 \]

So he's decelerating at three metres per second squared, which is what you'd hope for when there are roadworks ahead.

Watch for: giving \(+3\). He's braking, so the sign is negative.

(c)

The velocity stays positive the whole way, so distance and displacement are the same thing here. Integrate v from nought to four ln two. Twenty-four e to the minus t over four integrates to minus ninety-six e to the minus t over four.

\[ \left[-96e^{-\frac t4}\right]_0^{4\ln 2} = -96\left(\tfrac12\right) + 96 = 48 \]

AB is forty-eight metres.

Watch for: dividing by \(-\frac14\) wrongly. Dividing by \(-\frac14\) means multiplying by \(-4\): \(24 \times -4 = -96\).

C1 Differentiation and integration › Kinematics
213/2162022 P1 Q12Whole question
Model solution
(a)

The dot moves between \(A\) and \(B\), with \(O\) the midpoint of \(AB = 120\) cm. The greatest displacement from \(O\) is \(60\) cm, and the greatest value of \(a\sin nt\) is \(a\), so \(a = 60\).

One oscillation takes 6 s, so the period of \(\sin nt\) is 6:

\[ \frac{2\pi}{n} = 6 \;\Rightarrow\; n = \frac{\pi}{3} \]
\(a = 60\); \(n = \tfrac13\pi\) (shown)
(b)
\[ x = 60\sin\frac{\pi t}{3} \;\Rightarrow\; v = \frac{dx}{dt} = 60\left(\frac\pi3\right)\cos\frac{\pi t}{3} = 20\pi\cos\frac{\pi t}{3} \]

Since \(-1 \le \cos\frac{\pi t}{3} \le 1\), the maximum speed is \(20\pi\).

\(v = 20\pi\cos\dfrac{\pi t}{3}\) cm/s; maximum speed \(= 20\pi \approx 62.8\) cm/s
(c)
\[ \text{acceleration} = \frac{dv}{dt} = -\frac{20\pi^2}{3}\sin\frac{\pi t}{3} \]

At \(A\), \(x = -60\), so \(\sin\frac{\pi t}{3} = -1\):

\[ |\text{acceleration}| = \frac{20\pi^2}{3} = 65.79\ldots \]
\(\dfrac{20\pi^2}{3} \approx 65.8\) cm/s²
Teaching script
(a)

The dot swings between A and B, which are a hundred and twenty centimetres apart, with O right in the middle. So the furthest it ever gets from O is sixty. The biggest value of a sine n t is a, so a is sixty.

\[ a = 60 \]

One full swing takes six seconds, so the period of sine n t is six. And period is two pi over n.

\[ \frac{2\pi}{n} = 6 \;\Rightarrow\; n = \frac{\pi}{3} \]

Watch for: taking \(a = 120\). The amplitude is measured from the centre, so it's half of \(AB\).

(b)

Velocity is the derivative of displacement. Sine becomes cos, and the chain rule brings out a pi over three. Sixty times pi over three is twenty pi.

\[ v = 20\pi\cos\frac{\pi t}{3} \]

Cos never goes above one in size, so the top speed is twenty pi, about sixty-two point eight centimetres per second.

\[ 20\pi \approx 62.8 \text{ cm/s} \]

Watch for: leaving out the factor \(\frac\pi3\) from the chain rule.

(c)

Differentiate again for acceleration. Cos becomes minus sine, and another pi over three comes out.

\[ \frac{dv}{dt} = -\frac{20\pi^2}{3}\sin\frac{\pi t}{3} \]

At A, x is minus sixty, so sine pi t over three is minus one. The size of the acceleration is twenty pi squared over three, about sixty-five point eight centimetres per second squared.

\[ \frac{20\pi^2}{3} \approx 65.8 \text{ cm/s}^2 \]

Watch for: giving a negative magnitude. They asked for the size, so it's positive.

C1 Differentiation and integration › Kinematics
214/2162023 P2 Q7Parts (b)
Model solution for parts (b)
(b)(i)

The particle never changes direction if \(v\) never becomes negative. The minimum value of \(5\sin 0.2t\) is \(-5\), so the minimum velocity is \(c - 5\).

\[ c - 5 \ge 0 \;\Rightarrow\; c \ge 5 \]
Smallest value of \(c = 5\)
(b)(ii)

With \(c = 8\), \(v \ge 8 - 5 = 3 \gt 0\) for all \(t\), so the particle does not change direction. The third second is from \(t = 2\) to \(t = 3\).

\[ \begin{aligned} \text{Distance} &= \int_2^3 (5\sin 0.2t + 8)\,dt \\ &= \Big[ -25\cos 0.2t + 8t \Big]_2^3 \\ &= (-25\cos 0.6 + 24) - (-25\cos 0.4 + 16) \\ &= 25\cos 0.4 - 25\cos 0.6 + 8 \\ &= 10.393\ldots \end{aligned} \]
Total distance \(= 10.4\) m (3 s.f.)
Teaching script for parts (b)
(b)(i)

A particle only changes direction when its velocity changes sign. So "never changes direction" really means v never goes negative.

The lowest five sin zero point two t can go is minus five. That makes the lowest velocity c minus five, and we need that to be zero or more.

\[ c - 5 \ge 0 \;\Rightarrow\; c \ge 5 \]

So the smallest c can be is five.

Watch for: trying to solve v equals zero for t. You don't need t at all here; you need the lowest value of the sine part.

(b)(ii)

With c equals eight, the velocity never drops below three, so the particle never turns round. That means distance is the integral of v, no splitting needed.

Careful with the wording: the third second runs from t equals two to t equals three, not from zero to three. Five sin zero point two t integrates to minus twenty-five cos zero point two t, and eight integrates to eight t.

\[ \int_2^3 (5\sin 0.2t + 8)\,dt = \Big[ -25\cos 0.2t + 8t \Big]_2^3 = 25\cos 0.4 - 25\cos 0.6 + 8 \]

Calculator in radians, please. It comes to ten point three nine three, so the distance is ten point four metres to three significant figures.

Watch for: using 0 to 3 as the limits, or working it out with the calculator still in degrees.

C1 Differentiation and integration › Kinematics
215/2162024 P2 Q8Whole question
Model solution
(a)

At \(t = 0\), \(v = 25\): \(25 = A(0) + 11(0) + C\), so \(C = 25\).

At \(t = 4\), \(v = 85\):

\[ \begin{aligned} A(4)^{2.5} + 11(4) + 25 &= 85 \\ 32A &= 16 \end{aligned} \]
\(C = 25\) because the speed at \(t = 0\) is \(25\) m/s; \(A = 0.5\)
(b)
\[ a = \frac{dv}{dt} = 2.5(0.5)t^{1.5} + 11 = 1.25t^{1.5} + 11 \] \[ t = 4:\quad a = 1.25(8) + 11 = 21 \]
\(21\) m/s\(^2\)
(c)

The speed is positive throughout \(0 \le t \le 4\), so the distance is the integral of \(v\).

\[ \begin{aligned} PQ &= \int_0^4 \left(0.5t^{2.5} + 11t + 25\right) dt \\ &= \left[\frac{t^{3.5}}{7} + \frac{11t^2}{2} + 25t\right]_0^4 \\ &= \frac{128}{7} + 88 + 100 \\ &= 206.29 \end{aligned} \]
\(PQ = 206\) m
Teaching script
(a)

The velocity is A t to the power two point five, plus eleven t, plus C. You've been given two facts: the speed is twenty-five when t is zero, and eighty-five when t is four. Use t equals zero first, because everything except C disappears.

\[ t = 0:\ 25 = C \]

So C is twenty-five, and that's also your explanation: C is the speed at the moment the clock starts. Now t equals four. Four to the power two point five is thirty-two, and eleven times four is forty-four.

\[ 32A + 44 + 25 = 85 \;\Rightarrow\; 32A = 16 \]

So A is a half.

Watch for: getting four to the power two point five wrong. Do the root first: root four is two, then two to the fifth is thirty-two.

(b)

Acceleration means differentiate the velocity. The two point five comes down in front and the power drops to one point five. Eleven t becomes eleven, and the constant vanishes.

\[ a = 1.25t^{1.5} + 11 \]

At t equals four, four to the power one point five is eight. One point two five times eight is ten, add eleven, twenty-one metres per second squared.

Watch for: putting t equals four into the velocity instead. Easy slip when the two expressions look alike.

(c)

For the distance PQ, integrate the velocity from nought to four. Quick check first: can the velocity go negative in between? No, every term is positive, so the integral is the distance and nothing needs splitting.

\[ PQ = \left[\frac{t^{3.5}}{7} + \frac{11t^2}{2} + 25t\right]_0^4 \]

The first term: a half times t to the three point five over three point five, which tidies up to t to the three point five over seven. At t equals four that's a hundred and twenty-eight over seven, then eighty-eight, then a hundred. The lower limit gives zero.

\[ \frac{128}{7} + 88 + 100 = 206.29 \]

PQ is two hundred and six metres to three significant figures.

Watch for: dividing by two point five instead of three point five. You add one to the power first, then divide by the new power.

216/2162025 P2 Q7Whole question
Model solution
\[ \begin{aligned} v &= \int \left[(2t+1)^{-1} + 0.3t + 0.2\right] dt \\ &= \tfrac{1}{2}\ln(2t+1) + 0.15t^2 + 0.2t + c \end{aligned} \]

At \(t = 0\), \(v = -3\): \(-3 = \tfrac{1}{2}\ln 1 + 0 + 0 + c \Rightarrow c = -3\).

\[ v = \tfrac{1}{2}\ln(2t+1) + 0.15t^2 + 0.2t - 3 \] \[ t = 5:\quad v = \tfrac{1}{2}\ln 11 + 3.75 + 1 - 3 = 2.949 \]
\(v = 2.95\) m/s
Teaching script

You've got acceleration and want velocity, so integrate. The first term is one over two t plus one. That integrates to a half ln of two t plus one, with the half coming from the two in front of t.

\[ v = \tfrac{1}{2}\ln(2t+1) + 0.15t^2 + 0.2t + c \]

The starting velocity is negative three, so put t equals nought. Ln one is zero, so c is negative three.

\[ v = \tfrac{1}{2}\ln(2t+1) + 0.15t^2 + 0.2t - 3 \]

At t equals five: a half ln eleven, plus three point seven five, plus one, minus three. That's two point nine four nine, so the velocity is two point nine five metres per second.

Watch for: leaving out the constant. Forget it and your velocity at t equals five comes out three units too high.
